About this text

The Wish-Fulfilling Cow, an Extensive Commentary on the Amarakośa, known in Sanskrit as the Amarakośaṭīkā Kāmadhenu and in Tibetan as Chiwa Mepé Dzö kyi Gyacher Drelpa Döjö Bamo, is Toh 4300 in the Tengyur, where it belongs to the section on the sciences of language. It is the Buddhist paṇḍita Subhūticandra's detailed commentary on the Amarakośa, the famous Sanskrit thesaurus composed by Amarasiṃha, which groups the synonyms of Sanskrit words by subject, from gods and the heavens to plants, animals, the human body and the social classes. Following mainly the grammatical system of Candragomin, the commentary explains how each word is formed and what it means, often adding a Tibetan gloss and quoting earlier lexicons, grammars and poems. Opening with homage to the Lord of Speech and verses of blessing, it served Tibetan scholars as a key reference for understanding the vocabulary of Indian scripture and literature, and its colophon records a later revision of the translation by the Zhalu translator Dharmapālabhadra.

Translation

In the language of India: amarakoṣaṭīkā kāmadhenu nāma. In the language of Tibet: The Extensive Commentary on the Amarakoṣa, called The Wish-Fulfilling Cow.

Homage to the Blessed One, Vāgīśvara, the Lord of Speech.

Sprung from the attainment of the great supreme ground,
bearing branches and leaves of nectar and miraculous power,
may the wish-fulfilling tree of omniscience
bring forth flower and fruit for you.

Like the river Ganges streaming down,
his compassion goes along the three paths;
ocean of glory, treasure of nectar,
to the crescent moon of existence I bow.

The ship of Dharma that carries one across the great ocean of existence
is built from the wish-fulfilling tree of compassion;
plying hard the oar of faith with a deeply serene mind,
may one reach the far shore and gain the jewel one longs for.

As a bee gathers up drop by drop the nectar it draws from flowers,
he who milks and bestows the drink of excellent understanding,
like the lovely crest-flower of the lords of poets,
glorious Amara, who spoke it, he alone is victorious.

Whatever jewels lie in the ocean of lexicons of countless traditions,
the arrayed treasuries that are necklaces for the throat of poetry,
and though the work composed by glorious Caṇḍagomin is outstanding too,
still I compose this procedure for fully deriving the words.

Next, in order that sin may be wholly pacified by teaching what benefits others, and that perfect merit may be accumulated, he states an offering of praise to the Blessed One Samantabhadra: the verse beginning "Whose."

Knowledge is complete knowledge of every object there is. Compassion is the wish to draw beings out of suffering. Because these two are like an ocean, they are an ocean: the knowledge and compassion of the Blessed One, the Buddha, are an ocean. Here the word "ocean" draws a comparison through shared qualities, such as unfathomability. That which is made by knowledge and compassion is "possessed of unfathomability and the rest"; this is a compound formed under the rule "variously, in the sense of the agent." Since the action has a future sense, it is not needed here. The explanation "knowledge and compassion are an ocean" establishes the agent, and its word is left unexpressed; or else this is a compound of the sixth case, expressing relation. Knowledge and compassion have become like an ocean, and an ocean is a vast basin. "The ocean is firm and vast" is well known among the gods.

Unfathomable means not comprehended by others: no one can sound the far shore and the depths of the ocean of his knowledge and compassion. For the knowledge and compassion of that Blessed One pervade the whole sphere of what can be known. The ocean of suffering is without bottom or end, and helpless beings pervade it and fill it; whoever is able to carry them across to the very end is sinless, having abandoned faults such as attachment.

Qualities are loving-kindness, patience, peacefulness and the like. The root guṇa, "in the sense of calling," is one of the roots ending in a that belong to the remainder of the class beginning with cur, because the marker vṛt was placed before that class with its members was complete. By "in the senses of calling, of habituation and of praise," the affix comes under the rule erac.

"O wise ones, he is the very one to be served by you": so it should be understood. In particular, such great qualities as love and an intellect that has reached the far shore, which seek to accomplish the exalted ultimate, and supreme compassion and the rest, never run out and are never forgotten in bringing about the benefit and happiness of others; therefore they are inexhaustible. Or else he is inexhaustible because, even while abiding in saṃsāra, he is free of aging and death. Unfathomable means that the bottom of the ocean is not touched. Because the sinless qualities themselves have become a mine of jewels, he is inexhaustible, that is, completely full. Because he firmly sets all people on the course that leads to the fruit, the fruit of engaging in his service is shown.

"For glory and also for immortality": just as in "he goes in order to cook," this is the fourth case, in the very sense of the purpose. The word "also" is a particle that joins the two together. Glory is prosperity. "For the sake of immortality" means relying on him as one relies on an elixir. The ocean of knowledge and compassion is to be served by the wise who want happiness in existence for the sake of glory, that is, for the perfection of the three aims of life, and by those who want liberation for the sake of immortality, that is, for liberation.

Here, by "the ocean of knowledge and compassion" and the rest, the Omniscient One is indicated, the Blessed One, the Sugata, whose nature is to be supremely kind to all sentient beings without any reason. This, then, is the poetic quality called clarity. As the Ornament of Poetry called the Neck-Ornament of Sarasvatī says:

That whose meaning is perfectly clear
is called clarity.

For example:

Now that this has risen, the lotus ponds open their seals and bloom;
the hills and groves are like the fresh blossoms of the mandāra;
the sorrow of the ruddy geese who cannot stay together is destroyed, and pair joins pair;
like the copper-colored cheeks of a monkey flushed with fury.

Here, by the dispelling of darkness the sorrow of the ruddy geese is dispelled, for the male and female ruddy geese do not stay together at night; and through the dispelling of that and the rest, although the defining mark of the sun is not stated, it is indicated with perfect clarity by the sense. It is just like that here.

This verse is also applied to the treatise. Here, by the poet himself, there is first the gift of knowledge in all the fields of learning, and its glory is excellence. Because, through the very gift of knowledge, it becomes the cause of excellence, it is like an ocean; so the ocean is the treatise. This is the ornament of a metaphor of the cause, as in "in depth it is equal to the ocean." Its unfathomability is its being without equal, since it possesses the qualities just described. For those tormented by suffering, "sin" is the term used; the sinless are the qualities themselves, such as brevity and clarity, which are causes of happiness. It is served through hearing, reflecting, explaining and the like. Inexhaustible means perfectly complete. Or else it is the measure of the order of all nominal words and of the order in which the genders are clearly stated. In this way it truly and fully gives that to those who seek action and to those who desire. And whatever it is, the affix aṇ comes after the object and the agent.

Here, as is intended, the word "ocean" means the very sequence of the meanings that are heard in usage, and that is specified as "gender"; it belongs to the expression "the teaching of nouns and genders." Glory is the perfection of the three aims of life. As master Vyāḍi said:

For those made beautiful by the wealth and perfection
of the three groups, glory, voice and understanding,
whatever quality is a provision free of fear,
that quality too is declared to be glory.

Or else, whatever is the defining nature of the speech of the Omniscient One is the perfection of one's own aim; and the cause of the three aims, which has that as its cause, is the perfection of others' aims. Both of these together are glory. Liberation, attained through effort in the perfection of one's own and others' aims and in the accumulations of merit and wisdom, is immortality.

Next he states the treatise through whose study the aims of persons are accomplished: "I shall set forth the teaching of nouns and genders." Because he wishes to explain gender in common, there is no ordering by the neuter gender alone, just as in "two utterances make one word."

If one asks what the word is that applies to the names of heaven and the rest: by the rule on "vyoman and the rest," the word comes from the root mnā, with the affix manin and the elision of the m. Or else it comes from nam, since roots have many meanings. This is explained as "ṇama, in the senses of bowing and of sound." In expressing that which causes one to bow, by this the form nāma arises, with manin and the rest as before.

Or else gender is whatever gender, feminine and the rest, is made clear by the affixes dīp, ac and the like; that is, feminine, masculine and neuter themselves. It is the treatise that teaches these. Because it instructs students by means of these nouns and genders and makes them understand them in detail, it says "is to be set forth": what is established by the inner sense itself is made fully clear by the outer sense itself.

Or else, in praise, the present-tense affix laṭ is used for the future, the present and the imminent. Likewise in the world, what one wishes to accomplish, as though it were certain to happen, is spoken of as if it were present, or as if it were already possible. For example, someone says, "I have mostly got it already." One thinks that it certainly exists, or one says, "It is mostly obtained." For that very reason, in praise, the word that shows it vividly, "as if possible," is used.

If one asks, "Since earlier masters have taught nouns and genders, why does he set this forth?" he says: "Having gathered from other treatises." Here the various other treatises are the lexical treatises such as the Trikāṇḍa and the Utpalinī, and the treatises on gender expounded by master Vyāḍi, Vararuci, Candragomin, Vāmana and others; having gathered these up completely and heaped them together, he sets it forth here. Therefore this work is complete, because it gathers from the other treatises and states every name of every meaning there is. The words "inexhaustible," the negative particle, the words for "a little," and the purposes are all fully explained together with their meanings.

When it is bound into chapters, the nouns are easily understood; hence it is set forth by sections, which means that it is marked out by sections of three characteristics. A section is so called because like things are gathered together in it, that is, things alike in class, action, quality and substance.

Someone may object: "Even if he sets this forth after gathering from other treatises, it will become very long because of its division into sections." It will not, for it is explained in a condensed text that discards meaningless particles such as verse-fillers and again compresses everything into a few expressions. Stated in a special way, and discarding what is badly said, are the refined sections. The sections are condensed and refined because they are parts of a section. Explaining the meaning of "condensed": the Utpalinī and the like lack condensation; the Garland of Names and the like are deficient in gender; the Chapter on Gender and the like are deficient in nouns; the Garland of the Immortal and the like are incomplete; the work of Vopālita and the like lack sections. Because this work surpasses all of these, it bears fruit.

However, if the gender of the nouns were stated and then, for each referent, the noun stated again in the same way, in order to rule out completeness in the others; and likewise if it were made into as many words for nouns and as many words for genders as there are, the text would become excessively long. Therefore "mostly," that is, for the most part, the feminine, masculine and neuter should be known through the distinctions in the forms of the words and through the operation of the feminine affixes, such as āp.

Further, through nouns, through the qualifiers of nouns, and through a pronoun that refers to another's noun, even the word of every noun becomes differentiated. Of these, distinction in the form of nouns is, for example: kaumodakī, feminine, "the one possessed of the kumuda"; gadā, "club"; khaḍgo, "sword"; nandakhphaḥ, "the Joy-Maker"; salilaṃ, "water"; kamalaṃ, "lotus"; jalaṃ, "water," neuter. Change through the qualifiers of nouns is, for example: gaṇḍau kapolau tatpara hanu. Becoming a pronoun by referring to another's noun is, for example: katuḥ kuttaḥ snehayātraṃ sebālpā kuttayaḥsumān.

"In some cases, through co-occurrence": "in some cases" means where there is no distinction of form. Here, in order to determine the gender, a gender that distinguishes it must be shown. For example: aśvayugaśvinī, bhānuḥkaraḥviyadviṣṇupadaṃ. Because words such as aśvayuk have no distinction of form, their gender is to be determined by the gender of what stands beside them. The genders that determine the distinction of form are those of aśvanī, kara and viṣṇupad, and the genders of what co-occurs with them are determined by these.

"In some cases, from a specific statement about it": because masculine, feminine and neuter are taken up from a specific statement about the word, in some cases a word is to be known as masculine, feminine or neuter. "In some cases" is understood to mean where there is no distinction of form; or where there is no sign that determines the gender through co-occurrence; or a gender that has several intended exceptions to the co-occurring gender. For example: ballarirmmañjariḥ steyostriyaḥ sumanasaḥ puṣpaṃ khaniḥ, which are feminine, with khani meaning "mine"; and viṭaṅkaṃ, masculine and neuter.

Someone may ask: "This intention of determining gender by distinction of form is absent in the members of a dvandva compound. In that case, in a collective dvandva, by what statement is its gender made clear through distinction of form?" It is established as neuter by the rule "the collective is neuter." And whatever makes the gender clear through distinction of form in the mutual dvandva, its statement is the very same. "Horse and mare" is established as masculine, carrying an exception to the other gender, because the gender of the mutual dvandva is known from the other member itself. Therefore the earlier words and this one come to have different genders, and words of different genders are also compounded together.

Nor is there anything that determines the gender of words that necessarily enter into the retention of one member. These too come to be of different genders. For example: "goddess and god" are "gods"; "hen and cock" are "cocks"; "cow and bull" are "cattle"; and "white woolen cloth and white cotton cloth" are "white garments," of which one says "this is white" or "these are white."

"In order to state the distinctions": in order to state those distinctions of nouns and genders, namely genders that differ from one another, the dvandva and the retention of one member, "no dvandva has been made, nor any retention of one member." The intent is that distinct genders and nouns have not been made into such forms. For example: greha and geha, "house"; jana and janama, in the sense of birth; "the untrue" and "the impermanent," both meaning untrue; jīmūta, in the senses of cloud and mountain. Such is the meaning of "made," the word at the end. Or else the affix kta is used because what was intentionally desired has been accomplished and is praised.

Someone may object: "But words of different genders have also been made into dvandvas and retentions of one member, for example:

Vidyādharas, apsarases, yakṣas,
rākṣasas, gandharvas, kiṃnaras,

and śvaśu and śvaśrura become śvaśrura in the dual." He has said, "of those not stated": apart from the well known, no statement of distinctions is given, and they are not made into dvandvas or retentions of one member. Nor are those that are stated set out in any order other than the natural one.

Since such a formation is itself free of any specification of gender, this complete avoidance of the retention of one member should be studied separately in Pāṇini and the others, for in them the definition of the retention of one member for feminine, masculine, neuter and so on is made very clear. Master Candragomin, showing the power of words that are not retentions of one member, makes it known that a single power of a word becomes a word expressing many meanings, just as the word rodasī expresses both sky and earth.

"Then why are words of different genders mixed together, instead of dividing the names into a feminine section and a masculine section as appropriate, as in the Ratnakoṣa and the like?" Since the text would become too long if specification and distinction by form and the rest were carried out in that way, he explains having set aside the heavy burden of statement, saying: "There is no mixing, except by order." Order is arrangement in succession, meaning sequence and situation. Apart from that, nothing here has been made into a thoroughly mixed nature; the intent is that there is nothing like going back to do what contradicts the order.

Here the meanings such as the gods connected with heaven, sky, the directions, time and the rest, in their proper order and each together with its connections, are set out so that the objects expressed may be understood in their primary nature. By showing the orders of the synonyms, connections and nouns that depend on each situation of what they express, words in the singular, dual and plural are easily understood and understood just as they are. For that reason such an order is adopted. Further, the word "also" and the like, which have fixed synonyms such as those of heaven, and the mention of Śrī and Gaurī and the like in the context of the lion and of Hari, are a mixing of genders that does not contradict the order, as was said: "Apart from order, no mixing has been made here." The separate synonyms are set forth, as far as possible, in the order of the feminine section and the rest. Here, whatever the gender, that very situation and connection of the gender is stated at the head, as it is read in the group; and so it is to be understood everywhere, as far as possible.

"Again, what of the Ratnakoṣa's 'svargga, heaven; trideva, the three skies; and tripiṣṭaya, the three bases, are neuter,' and the like?" Here tripiṣḍaya, svarga and tridiva are simply neuter. "Abode of the gods" and the like mean the abode of the immortals. In knowing the separate orders, such as the masculine section, a great deal of mixing should also be seen. Such mixings of gender are far removed from order. Therefore the separate synonyms too are set forth in the order of heaven, sky, the directions, time and the rest, that is, in the order of the feminine chapter and the rest. Stating synonyms separately in each section, as in the Ratnakoṣa and the like, is pointless and becomes a cause of long-winded explanation, so what would be the use of it?

The specification, fully explained for the sake of brevity of the text, is "among the three genders" and so on. "The threes and the genders" makes "having three genders," a collective compound: the three genders. In that domain the word "among the threes" is to be understood. For example, one says taṭaṃ triṣu: taṭaṃ means "bank," and tṛṣu means "among the three."

Next, "among the two" refers to a pair; it is a word for masculine and feminine. For example, as in aśanirdvayoḥ: aśaniḥ means "thunderbolt," and dvayoḥ means "among the two." Even though the words "two" and "three" express number in general, here they apply specifically to the particular number just explained. In such passages as "lacking two, srasabe, everything that bears honey is not two," too, the number is taken as general, and the pair is understood by inference. Though it is not stated aloud, the statement that in the specification of number the particular number in such cases as "thunderbolt, two" is to be taken as the word "two" should be inferred here in the same way. In such passages as "ardharātra and niśithi, the two," the specific number is stated explicitly, so there is no overextension.

An excluded gender is whatever specification of gender is given; it should be understood as having the remaining meaning, the remaining genders of the noun. Because it expresses the sense of the genders that remain once one is excluded, it is "having the remaining meaning." For example, dvipo'striyaṃ: here, since the feminine is excluded, it is to be understood as masculine and neuter; and likewise from the exclusion of the masculine gender and the rest. Here too, establishing two genders under the guise of excluding one is for the sake of brevity of the text.

Now, so that it may be understood with little text, it is said: "What ends in tu and what begins with atha is not part of what precedes." dvepaḥ means "island," and astriyaṃ concerns its being feminine. What ends in tu and what begins with atha, although connected with the preceding words, nouns, genders and so on, are not part of what precedes.

Of these, a noun word, for example: śabhā karñi dhutiśca birabaśyayastu nīhāra praleyaṃ mahikā cātha himānī himayaṃhati. Here, everything up to ścabi is a set of synonyms for beauty. Breaking off at the tu at the end of abaśyayastu, there follow synonyms for snow, up to mahīkāca; it is said that this marks one further, different meaning. That is its extent. Because atha marks a different meaning, himānī himasaṃhatiḥ means a mass of snow. These examples for gender and the rest appear in the earlier translation but are not in the Indian manuscript.

A word, for example, as in: antardhā byabadhā suṃsi tvantadhiḥ bhārya jāyātha suṃbhunni dārā. In "woman and wife," here the word tu, by the maxim of the stick and the cake, draws in the action word, and it also draws in the noun word connected with it. Likewise, in "ghasro dināha dība rutpābee dibasa bāsara, the two," it draws in the gender word.

A word of meaning, too, for example, as in: śāsetva biṣaṅgaḥ koṣātakyatha kaṭmalaḥ. The word atha has the sense of "immediately next"; because it marks its own set of synonyms, what begins with the word atha is likewise not part of what precedes.

In "anukrośosyaṇvāhasaḥ potābānamatho jhaṣaḥ puṃsye bāndhu prahī: that which comes from a well is masculine," the word tu does not prevent "that which comes" from applying to the masculine. What depends on something else becomes part of what precedes. There, "another gender too": because that is applied separately, the preceding gender itself is also recognized. There, "a distinction of form that arises does not apply" is the restrictive sense of the rule of exclusion. That "that which comes" is not itself part of what precedes is as in "well-water": when "well" is given masculine gender, the result is that the non-masculine words connected with it are also determined by it.

In the verse

The mineral of the mountain is realgar and the like,
And red ochre in particular,

the set of the excluding specification is overridden. Other reasoning too I shall explain in the section on mountains.

Having briefly set out the full explanation of the arrangement of the treatise, he now begins the treatise proper with the passage beginning "heaven." Here the subject matter of the treatise is the three worlds. Of these, heaven is named first because it is foremost of all; then the gods in general who dwell there; and because its particular members are shown as named foremost, the Buddha, Brahmā, Viṣṇu and Rudra are to be named.

Further, with "first, of the directions," following its fixed order, after the directions come the lords of the directions, from the east and Agni through to Kubera. Following the fixed order of the directions, there come the upper direction, the sky, and its lord, the moon, lord of the lunar mansions; following its fixed order, the lunar mansions; the sun, lord of the great planets; then again what arises incidentally from that, the morning and so on; and, following the order of time, the eons of destruction and of formation are also named.

There, in the view of the Naiyāyikas and others, the maker of all beings, comparable to the self as the agent of dharma and the like, the unseen force (adṛṣṭa), is the omniscient Lord (Īśvara). In the view of the Sāṃkhyas, these three worlds that the person enjoys are produced by the Principal, primordial Nature (prakṛti), and, perishing in the end, are held to belong to the assembly of agents of dharma and the like. Then the characteristics of body, intellect, objects and senses; after that, the enjoyment of the five objects; and, following the fixed order of sound, song, and incidentally in that sequence cymbals, dance and so on, are all included in passing, completing the first section.

Arising from the order of the directions, the lower direction, the underworld, and its lords, the nāga kings, are also named in the first chapter itself; in the chapter on the earth, the underworld is named first. Moreover, it is not divided into three chapters by the sequence of the three abodes, heaven and so on, as Bhāguri does.

In the second chapter, following the order of the earth, there comes the city; following the fixed order of the village, the market town; the hunters' settlement too is a kind of village, and it depends on mountains and forests. After that, lions and countless other creatures that dwell in mountains and forests are also included in passing. Human beings in general and their particular kinds, brahmins, kṣatriyas, vaiśyas and śūdras, are likewise named in that way in the third, on the basis of the two chapters stated before.

Likewise there are the words beginning with "virtuous," the qualifiers of the immortal connected with non-harming; the agents of action beginning with "object"; the words of many meanings beginning with "sky"; the indeclinable groups beginning with āṅ; and the words that make the genders known, the compendium of genders. This is the condensed meaning of the treatise.

The meanings of the parts too are to be stated in order. From "heaven" and so on through to "the triple base" are names of heaven. There, the word svaḥya, because it is used together with the word svargga, would take the masculine.

Indeclinable words undergo no decline in the genders and numbers assigned to them, so that where forms would become various, by virtue of conforming to the meaning they become of unmanifest gender. Those in which there is no operation of gender whatsoever are of unmanifest gender, and they are endless.

This is also explained in the Elucidation of Elegance: in the words iyam, ayam and idam, each meaning "this," the genders feminine, masculine and neuter are properties of words, the causes of the formation of words whose meaning has become clear. Among them, the gender that, as cause of the formation with dī and the like, is shown with clear meaning by iyam is the feminine. Then the gender that, as cause of word formation by such rules as "after śas, nā in the masculine," is shown with clear meaning by ayam is the masculine. The gender that, as cause of word formation by such rules as "of ika, before aca, in supa," is shown with clear meaning by idam is the neuter.

Here the following are given in the neuter gender: śuddhaṃ, pure; miśraṃ, mixed; saṃkīrṇṇaṃ, scattered; upasarjanaṃ, subordinate; ābiṣṭaṃ, pervaded; and abyaktaṃ, unmanifest. Of these, the cause of the formation of a single gender is the pure: khaṭvāṃ, bṛkṣaḥ, kuṇḍaṃ, of feminine, masculine and neuter gender respectively. That which mentions the formation in two genders is the mixed: marīciḥ ūrmiḥ urciḥ chardiḥ kaṣāyaṃ kapāṭaṃ. That which mentions the formation in three is the scattered: taṭī taṭaḥ taṭaṃ śrikhalā śṛṃkhalaṃ. That which mentions the formation of a word that is a qualifier, having the form of the qualifier, is the subordinate: śukla śuklaḥ śuklaṃ paṭvī paṭuḥ paṭu. Ray, wave, ray, vomit, dregs. That which, even though it is a qualifier, must necessarily be formed in a fixed gender is the pervaded: split, in the domain of nature, chain, principal. bharyāḥ dārāḥ klatraṃ. That in which the word that is the ground of gender ought to be formed is the unmanifest: abyakto, namely such words as bañca, ṣaṭa, kati, uccaiḥ and vayaṃ.

Granted that when it is used in this way its qualifier is neuter, as in "ramyaṃ svaḥ bimala naktaṃ," because gender rests on worldly usage; nevertheless, since the word sva is also read in the indeclinable group and among the words of various meanings, there is the word "heaven" also for a happy destiny, for the other world, and for the lofty.

If one asks why it is taught again here, some say it is for the sake of faultless synonyms. There, the word naktaṃ, which expresses a synonym for night, is likewise stated here. This word sva is pracura, frequently used. For instance, Māgha says: "ādṛśyatādarśatalāmalechaye bābī svaḥchu," in the waters of the ocean. kaphinā: in its manifest appearance heaven is beautiful; it attains the state of being like a jewel mirror of many auspicious things, examined and examined all over. That is also indicated by the synonyms; likewise, by the synonyms that are mostly used, it is also explained in the section on words of various meanings.

The explanation and the statement too: the word "heaven" is indicated here as well, for the purpose of making recollection clear, or else for the purpose of including whatever is an indeclinable word. It is taken up first for the sake of auspiciousness.

svṛ is in the sense of sound. By "kvipa bci" and the like, there is bci. ṛja is in the sense of going; because it has many meanings, it is also in the sense of abiding. Thus it is said: "ṛja is in the senses of going, abiding and acquiring." Here it means to cause to abide: argga is abiding. For the agent too there is ghaña. Because one abides well there, it is svargga. Or else, because it is attained through excellent merit, it is svarga.

śaṃ is happiness; its opposite is akaṃ. Because that is absent in it, it is nākaḥ. Here, in the rule "many, in the sense of another," since supa carries over, it is a compound of two words. It belongs to nakha and the rest; because it is called "the good abode," there is no elision.

"Where the three play" means that three play there. To the word diba, by the rule for what has ika next to the final, and from "joyful," the suffix kā is added at the end; it is a compound in the sense of another word. Or else, as with kāka, guha and the like, it is ka, since in many cases pra and the rest are absent. It is the place where the three chief gods, Brahmā, the Lord (Īśvara) and Viṣṇu, play.

"Abode of the Thirty" and "world of the gods" are compounds of the sixth case. "Abode" means base; "world" means abode.

"Play" means: by the rule "variously, from gami, ḍo." By "from gorau, in its own sense," since the word go serves as an indication, there is au also from dyaṃ: dloḥ dyābau. Because one plays there, it is "play." This is by dibi of the uṇ group of rules; Pūrṇacandra holds that here it is "diva with ut." With the suffix ḍip of the uṇ group, that the word diva is heard in a single tone is established here by the sūtra "in calling from afar." By the very nature of words, "the two": by that specification they are feminine.

Likewise, in "after śas, na in the masculine," here there is that of radnam; in the neuter, ṣaṇḍakān and paṇḍakān. Where in reality there is no masculine thing, a natural masculine gender is nonetheless expressed; this is gender that has arisen from the nature of words. For example:

In the beginning, two sons were born from himself:
Nara and Nārāyaṇa.

It is also like the application of a word with two meanings in explanations such as "In the Twelve Thousand commentary of candrapalba, the master of jar is neuter."

In "the triple base," "base" means abode. Since the word for base, piṣṭaya, is also of masculine and neuter gender, it is a compound of the sixth case; and since the meaning of the final member is principal, by the distinction of form of the principal gender it is neuter, and by exception masculine. Since it obtains the neuter as well, it is established once more as neuter.

That this too is known as a neuter word: by explaining the distinction of form in "lokaḥ piṣṭayaṃ bhubanaṃ," it makes clear the word that is the basis of gender for the other gender as well, it is said. In the Ratnakoṣa: "Base means world, the abode where Agni goes." In the Amaramālā too: "Abode and base are of masculine gender." This, base and abode, is to be understood in full up to the end of the section that is the compendium of genders, and I shall explain it applying the particulars.

From "immortal" through to "god," the three verses give the gods in general. Since a word for a class expresses the individuals that possess the class, and the individuals are many, they are given in the plural.

Because they die, they are mortal. By "from naña, in the case of jara mara mitra mṛta," from the particle of svar and pūtra, from mṛṅ, there is the suffix ās. Since they have no death, they are the immortals.

Because they always remain as if twenty-five years old and no aging appears in them, they are the unaging; it is a compound in the sense of another word.

Because they play: by the rule on the agent and so on, the suffix aca.

Because they have the nature of birth, abiding and destruction, they are the Thirty, those of three states. They do not, as humans do, change through sickness and aging and waste away; for they are born, abide and also die as if twenty-five years old.

The wakeful are those without sleep, who are always fully awake. By "jñā kṝ pṝ and what has ika as penultimate," ka.

Possessing the essence: the gods and the asuras churned the ocean. The goddess Śrī emerged, and Viṣṇu took her. A half-moon emerged, and the Lord made it the ornament on the crown of his head. A vase full of poison emerged, and the Lord placed it in his throat, so that his throat turned blue. A vase full of liquor emerged, and the gods took it, so they are known as sura sura. The essence may be the liquor, or the essence of the ocean, or ambrosia. Because they possess the liquor that came from the churning of the ocean, they are possessing the essence; by arśa and the rest, aca. And it is explained that because they took the liquor, the gods are known as possessing the essence.

Because the joints of the gods' fingers and so on are beautiful, they are fair-jointed; or else because their bodies are exceedingly fine. Because all that they need and wish for is accomplished merely by thinking of it in their minds, they are good-minded. The lords of the threefold play are the lords of the happy destiny, that is, the chief ones.

The word for heaven, dyau, is used because they dwell in the sky; since it belongs to pṛṣodara and the like, the letter o is made au. In the Trikāṇḍaśeṣa, diva is explained as ending in at. Further, for the world of heaven there are: the world of the gods, syādabaroha, the arising of fruits, mandara, saibika, the abode of Śakra, space, the firmament, and the sky. Those ending in ar, uṇ and at are also read in the Liṅgānuśāsana. Aśvaghoṣa too uses it in the Buddhacarita: "deba ḍimbani kutumba dibo lvamra"; and "There is nothing unbeautiful," and "nobinā puraṃ marutvātā bṛttabadhi yathā divaṃ."

By the rule "for bṛṣi takṣi raji bandhi prati dibi yu, kanina," applied variously and also singly, from dibi too there is kanina. Thus dibaukasa is the reading of the author of the Bhāgavṛtti. That from dibi, once ūt has been made, since there is no prohibition when a sound follows, the letter u is changed to yaṇ, giving dibaukasa, is the view of the master Maitreyarakṣita. In the Garland of Names too, since there is no prohibition when a sound follows, yaṇ is applied to "illuminator of heaven," and we find heaven explained as sky and firmament.

Aditi, "not giving," is the wife of the sage called Kāśyapa. There, by "erakinta," dīśa with it, and by "ās ūd," for offspring, edhaka. From dibasa, as anti applies, kvipa. By the rule "from saptami, variously," it is elided. Because it belongs to suṣama and the like, it has ṣa. Elision is also accepted.

Because they have auspicious lines on their necks, hands and feet, they are well-lined. In the sense of praise, by arśa and the rest, aca.

"The joy of Aditi" means her sons. Because they are the sons of Aditi, they are the sons of Aditi; here ṇya is added to Aditi. "The letter ṛ" is the name of the first mother of the gods; because they came from her, they are "sons of the letter ṛ," as found in the Trikāṇḍaśeṣa. Therefore, by "in bhabanti" and "those beginning with śaṃku," there is kurat and also the elision of ta.

Because they have no sleep, they have no dreams either. martyā means mortal: from mṛ gṝ bāhasi ṇami dami llasī hurbi, tana. By the rule "from srura sarnta kṣema pa biṣṭha," in its own sense, yata. Because they do not die, they are the immortals, those whose nature is deathlessness.

Because their food is ambrosia, they are ambrosia-eaters. Because the gods receive the oblations offered into the fire, and in order to eat the burnt offering their mouth is fire, they are fire-mouthed. They are also called sacrifice-eaters: for eating the kratu, kvipa.

Their words, which have the character of angry curses, are arrows; because they do the work of arrows, they are word-arrowed. Because they destroy the asuras, they are enemies of the asuras. Because they have a good assembly, they are of good assembly.

By the rule "this is its deity," from the particle, from deba in its own sense, tala. By the rule "from tal, īta and ūt too," it is feminine. Deity itself is god: by the rule "optionally from prajñā and the rest," aṇ. By the statement that from the base of a word with its own sense the genders do not carry over with apa, it is masculine and neuter.

The passage beginning "Ādityas, Viśvedevas" names the group gods, the gods that come in assemblies. The meaning is that since these are explained as particular assemblies of gods, they are expressed by the word "group gods."

Among them, the Ādityas are twelve;
The Viśvedevas are remembered as ten;
The Vasus are rightly declared to be eight;
Likewise the Tuṣitas are thirty-six;
The Ābhāsvaras, sixty-four;
Those called the Vāyus, forty-nine;
Those called the Mahārājikas
Are likewise two hundred and twenty;
Those called the Sādhyas are said to be twelve;
The Rudras are declared to be eleven.
These are the group gods.

Among them, the individual names of the Ādityas are:
Indra, Dhātṛ, the Wayfarer,
Parjanya, the Praiser, Not-Former, the Noble,
Human-Form, Viṣṇu, the Radiant,
Varuṇa, and Mitra as well.

Vasu and the Hero, Kratu,
the Clear, Kāla, Kāma, Burden-Eye,
the Person, his own, namely the Left, and Substance:
these are proclaimed the Viśvedevas.

Āpa and Dhruva, Soma,
Dhara, Anila, and Agni,
and those called Pratyūṣa and Prabhāsa:
these eight are remembered as the Vasus.

Strength and Mind, After-Viṣṇu,
Strength's Son, and the sons of men,
and likewise the Treasure of Austerity,
the Rim, the Swan, Dharma and Fame,
Day-and-Night, the Equalizer, and Born-of-Action:
these are known as the twelve Sādhya gods.

Ajaikapād, Growth and Praise,
Rudra, and the Diligent One,
Hari, and then Śambhu,
Tryambaka, Victor over Others,
Īśāna, and Tribhuvana:
know these as the eleven Rudras.

The rest are to be learned from the scriptures; they are not stated here for fear of prolixity.

From aśiṅ laṭi kaśi khaṭi biśa, kvana. Since it has various poisons, it does not change to another gender. nanāśuṇthyāṃ is masculine. With regard to all the divisions of the gods, "among the threes": Rudra. Also for the Sādhyas and the Viśve, in such expressions as "and the Rudras too," one reasons that the operations of sarba and the like are not prevented, because they show earlier usage, because they explain the whole chapter, and because they are not other than principal nouns.

basa is in the sense of dwelling. By the rule "from paṭa asi basi trapi hani mani andi kandi bandi," u. tuṭa is in the sense of rejoicing. Among these, from having agency, each being mentioned in turn, by the rule "tuṣṭa, from joy itself," suba. By the rule "from a root lacking the sense of going, lacking an object, and also for the locus," kta.

Because of their nature of shining with surpassing excellence and beauty, they are the Ābhāsvaras. By "sthā bhās," there is also the suffix vara.

The Mahārājikas, "great garland," are so called because their ranks are arrayed in rows. From the common derivation, and also for a name, from yāba and the rest, kana. That these gods called "great garland" are called "great kings" is said by the master Śūra in praising the good path. By the rule "from bṝhya, yata, and by ini too," ṭhana.

Because they are to be accomplished, they are the Sādhyas, "those to be accomplished": from ṛ and hal, ṇyata.

Knowledge means eye-salves, mantras and the like; for "holding," the suffix aca. The vidyādharas, "holders of knowledge," are Puṣpadanta and the like. Since this is not explained elsewhere, it is made clear as a split coordinate compound in order to explain the distinction of gender. As for synonyms, from the Rabhasakoṣa:

Vidyādhara, one who takes any form at will,
sky-goer, the true, and the youthful.

The apsarases are the consorts of the gods, the courtesans of the gods, such as Urvaśī. yakṣa is in the sense of worship: by the rule "in the remainder of cura and the rest, it performs worship," the suffix aca. The yakṣa are the yakṣas, Kubera and the rest. The rākṣasas are the Terrible One and the like. The gandharvas are the singers of the gods, such as Free-of-Harsh-Sound and Chooser-of-Melody. The kiṃnaras are those with human bodies and horses' faces and the like, who sport in song. The piśācas are a kind of being that eats raw flesh, followers of Kubera. Because it belongs to pṛṣodara and the like, piśita is changed to pi and āśa to śāca: piśāca. "śakuni śakunti piśāca piśaṃgā śapatha niśātha kṛśa anukiśora": this is the division called "warm."

Because they hold the treasures: by the rule "from kṛ bṛṣi mṛji śaṃsi duhi guha," there is also the suffix ṣa. For a name, from yāba and the rest, kana. Or else, because they conceal, by "dā," the suffix ḍa. The guhyaka are the guhyakas, the secret ones, Maṇibhadra and the like, followers of Kubera.

The siddhas are those endowed with the qualities of minuteness and the rest. The bhūtas are a kind of piśāca, some of whom speak with great voices, such as the Downward-Facing and the Upward-Looking. From application to bhūta, by arśa and the rest, the suffix aca.

These are to be called by the word "sprung from the gods," because they themselves arose from having first had the gods as their cause. Or else, because like the gods they did not arise from a mother's womb, they are called "those whose origin is divine."

One verse gives the names of the asuras. The gods made their own the essence that came from the churning of the ocean, and the asuras did not obtain it; so they are called asura, meaning "not possessing the essence." do is in the sense of cutting off entirely. For a name, kti as well. It is said: "do so mā sthā: for their ti, ati." The Golden One and the others, whose father is the sage Protector of Beings and whose mother is Diti, the Giver, are sons of the Giver, and so they are the Daityas. By the rule "from ditya" applied to ditya, there is here the suffix ṇyī. From the word: by "erakinta" and "dīṣa ūta āsa" and the like, the suffix dhaka.

The Bow-born are the asuras born of the mother called Bow, such as Śambara. Formerly the sage called the Clear One had thirteen daughters, Aditi, Diti, Danu and others; of these, Diti, called the Giver, is the first mother of the asuras. The distinction among the asuras' names, such as Daitya and Dānava, is a distinction drawn from the mothers' names; but because of their similar nature and because they act together, they are presented as bearing a single designation, and so are connected without distinction. Likewise, the use of Dānava and Daitya explained by the Deliverer is fitting only insofar as "glory" is cited with regard to attaining Daitya. An example of Daitya and Dānava, as stated by the Golden One: "dānaba miba dāṃṣṭa bibhinna bakṣaḥsthalam." Māgha holds that, on the basis of a distinction in the ultimate sense, Daitya and Dānava differ as sages and men do.

They are also called "enemies of Indra." "Disciples of Śukra": the asuras are disciples of the planet Śukra.

"Former gods" is a descriptive compound. It is told that formerly the abode of the gods belonged to the asuras; the gods seized it and drove the asuras down to the slopes of Mount Meru. "Revilers of the gods" means those who are hostile to the gods: from dviṣa, kvipa.

By the rule "after a word-final, not before ṭa, except for a name," the master Pāṇini prohibited the ṣṭu change for the locative plural when the tra class follows. Since su and the rest do not stand in the position of sarbanāma, they naturally bear the designation "word." In such forms as rabiṭsu, svārāṭu, samrāṭsu and ṣaṭsu, the master Candragomin, with the rule "also when not ṭa, the first of nabati and nagara," prohibited the ṣṭu change of the first word. If, then, the letter pa of supa does not undergo the ṣṭu change, why is this undermined by changing the very group of the e root into the letter sa with ata as its ending? As for the suffix ṭha, the Extensive Commentary on the bhāṣa and Maitreyarakṣita too, having analyzed ṣaḍḍha ṣaḍḍha under pṛṣodara and the like, cite the ṣṭu change as an example.

Further, it is said here: since establishing the ṣṭu change depends on the class, the letter pa of supa does not have that nature. For example, in the Cloud Messenger, in "trakṣusibhātrijayaṃ," and in "from ṛta, for bida," and so on, there is no fear of ṣaṣṭhi. Likewise, in "of the fifth following," this is the view of Ratnamati. It is said that by the class sense, when it is intended, it is accepted as far as the full extent of the domain of usage reaches. Merely mentioning bhabatī suta and the like is fruitless. What is mentioned in the commentary applies to the suffix as well, just as it stands. Merely mentioning ekakāmyati sarbakāmyati applies also in the domain of prohibition, just as it stands. The exclusion of "all" here is "all-delighting," meaning "pleasing to all." matāntaraṃ, too, for example: in "in the composition of bakula," here, since the word "composition" is taken up as a synonym for verse, and since there too every derivation as taught has been examined, these are applied like "king" and the like, it is said.

From "omniscient" through to "sage," all are names of the Buddha.

From sarbaṃ jānati, "he knows all," since pra and the rest are absent, ka. sarbajñā: the all-knowing.

He has understood well through his wisdom; or else, he has gone well, never to return to saṃsāra; or else, because he has gone to the accomplishment of the nature of supreme bliss, he is the Sugata. As it says in the Praise of the Good Path:

Having attained in his heart benefit and supreme bliss,
endowed with great love and utterly pure wisdom,
he who has gone, by stages and without stages,
to the wondrous city just as it is: he is called the Sugata.

Because he is endowed with all that is awakened, he is the Buddha. As it says in the Ornament of Instructions on the Sūtras:

Of all that moves and is unmoving, exhausted and unexhausted,
of the past, whatever lacks manifest full comprehension
is conceptual thought; the root of awakening is this:
therefore the great-minded Blessed One is the Buddha.

Variously: by the rule "also from what has an object, with the sense of going, from what has no object, and also for the locus," kta.

The King of Dharma is the king of Dharma who turned the wheel of Dharma. By "sakhi aha sa rājana," the suffix ṭaca. By the rule "not for uṇa and the rest," aca and the like of anti are elided.

Because he went to peace in just the way that the sages came, he is "thus gone"; therefore he is the Tathāgata. Or else, because having come into existence again and again he thus went to perfect peace, he is the Tathāgata. Or else, because he went by those modes of benefiting the world through the qualities of generosity, moral discipline, patience, diligence and the rest, he is the Tathāgata. The loving one who goes there in just whatever way benefits whomever, that is the Tathāgata: endowed with the qualities of generosity and the rest, his mind fully liberated from the faults of desire. As with both explanations, it is a compound under the rule "supa with supa, of one meaning."

In "Samantabhadra," "all-round" means the accumulation of merit and the accumulation of wisdom, and "good" means foremost.

In "the Blessed One," the genuine supremacy of a great nature is blessedness. As it is said:

Truly endowed with every incomparable quality,
the unsurpassed one, free of every fault,
is adorned with supreme greatness of nature;
therefore he is proclaimed the Blessed One.

Diligence and the rest are also blessedness. As it says in the Vyākhyāyukti:

Sovereignty and beauty of form,
glory, fame and wisdom,
and perfect diligence:
these six are called fortune.

Because he possesses these, too, he is the Blessed One.

Because he subdues the māras of the afflictions and so on, and Māra Manmatha, lord of the desire realm, he is the Tamer of Māra: from ji, kvipa. The world is of the nature of desire, form and the formless, as is well known in the world; or else it is of the nature of heaven and the rest. Because he excels in subduing it: from ji, the suffix kvipa. Because he subdues Māra, he is the Victor: by the rule "those beginning with rāsna," from ji, the suffix naka.

The divine eye, the divine ear, knowledge of the minds of others, recollection of former abodes, knowledge of the exhaustion of the defilements, and the miraculous powers characterized by bodily displays such as going and coming through the sky: these are the six superknowledges. His ten powers, knowledge of what is possible and what is impossible and the rest, are to be learned from the scriptures; they are not elaborated here for fear of prolixity.

What is not two is nonduality; because he has the nature of speaking reality, he is the Speaker of Nonduality. By the rule "from what is not a class word, in the sense of disposition and also of correctness," ṇini for bada.

Because he trains sentient beings and shows them what is beneficial, he is the Guide: ṇīla ṇū. Because he has become king of the sages, he is the Lord of Sages. Because he is filled with glory, he is Dense with Glory: by the rule "kṣubhnā svāhā, from the preceding word, for a name," there is the change of naṇa.

As it is said:

That which is bright with the jewels of the eighty minor marks
And marked by the thirty-two major marks of the Guide,
Belongs neither to Brahmā nor to the lords of the gods:
It is the glorious body of the tathāgatas.

"Dense" means a mass; because he is made of that, it is a compound with the agent.

Because he teaches and disciplines sentient beings, he is the Teacher (śāstā): by the rule "for the agent," the affix tṛc. Likewise, Ratnamati says that, since in the rule "kti ti śi" śāstā is given separately as an example, the affix tṛc is alternatively explained for this very word. For that very reason there is lengthening by the rule "āp, tṛ and the rest": śāstārau, śastāraṃ. That rule applies only to restricting the class, just as when one says "the brahmin Kauṇḍinya alone eats the food," this does not forbid brahmins from eating, but forbids kṣatriyas and others from eating. This very point is explained by the master Rakṣita in the Tantrapradīpa: "In the section of specific statements, the mention of nastā and the others among these words is made for the purpose of restricting the words; therefore it applies to nothing other than the nominal words themselves." Because "from the ancient sages" is the indicator, by the rule "in the sense of those taught in scripture," there is the affix bod.

Because he knows all phenomena in all their aspects, or else because, like a sage, he does not speak of phenomena, he is the Sage (muni): by the rule "from mani, also ut," there is the affix i and the change of a to u.

The immeasurable qualities of the buddhas should be learned from scripture. These are synonyms for buddhas in general.

With one verse come the names of the perfect Buddha Śākyamuni. "Śākyamuni" and the rest are so called because he was born in the Śākya clan. The sons of a king called Kecana, who came from the Ikṣvāku lineage, were banished to the forest by their father. In that forest, relying on the sage called Kapila, they made their dwelling beside a tree called śāka, became Kapila's disciples, and were endowed with knowledge. Hence they are Śākyas: by the rule "dika and the rest," the affix yat. As the poem Saundaranandacarita says:

Because they walked about in a dwelling place
Covered over with śāka trees,
Those who sprang from the Ikṣvāku lineage
Are therefore called the Śākyas.

Since he was born of the Śākya clan, he is a Śākya, and he is also a sage; therefore he is Śākyamuni, a compound of qualifier and qualified. Śākyasiṃha, "Lion of the Śākyas," means the chief of the Śākyas. As soon as he was born, treasures of jewels appeared and all aims were accomplished, so he was given the name Sarvārthasiddha, "He Who Has Accomplished All Aims." Since this is intended as a compound referring to another thing and in the manner of qualifier and qualified, by the rule "optionally final, as in 'one who has set up the fire' and the like," the word siddha is not the prior member.

Śuddhodana: because he ate in accordance with knowledge, the treatises call him Śuddhodana, "Pure Food." Because his food is pure, there is the replacement by the following form, since the word belongs to the group "śakandhu and the rest"; and for his offspring, the affix iñ after a, giving Śauddhodani.

Gautama: one born in the lineage of the sage Kapila. His disciples are the Śākya Gautamas, and since the Blessed One too was born of that lineage he is Gautama: by the rule "in the sense of its lord," the affix aṇ. Gotra means lineage and uttama means supreme; hence he is also called "possessing the supreme lineage." As the Saundaranandacarita says:

His was the supreme lineage of Kautsa;
Those who sprang from it are Gautamas.

This rests on the Smṛti treatise, as it is said:

The one who gives food, the one who protects from fear,
Whoever gives knowledge,
The one who begets, and the one who gives a bride:
These five are called father.

When one wishes to state the affix of descent: from ṛṣi, Kuru, Vṛṣṇi and Andhaka, the affix aṇ. Then, by elision under the rule for Atri, Knower of the Stream, Bhṛgu, Abandoner of Evil and the many others, "in the plural, when not feminine," it is Gautama. It is just as when one wishes to state the descent of the disciples of Bharata: it is by elision under the rule "for utpa and the rest, añ, yañ and iñ, in the plural, when not feminine."

Jana, in the sense of being born, together with tanoti and karoti, takes the affix bic: janita. From tṛc, with uṇ, since there is no elision of iṭ, it becomes janayitā.

Arkabandhu, "Kinsman of the Sun," and Māyādevīsuta, "Son of Queen Māyā": these should be learned in detail from scripture.

With two verses comes Brahmā. The measure of his body is immeasurable and especially exalted; since bṛhat is explained as "vast," he is called Brahmā, as the Sāmba Purāṇa explains. By the rule "vyoma and the rest," from bṛhi there is the affix manin and also the augment n. He is designated Brahmā, and it is told that although Viṣṇu looked from above downward and Īśvara (Śiva) looked from below upward, neither found the measure of his body.

Ātmabhū, "Self-Born," because he was not propelled by the winds of karma: the affix kvip. Surajyeṣṭha, "Eldest of the Gods," because he is the eldest of the gods. Parameṣṭhin, "Abiding in the Supreme," because he abides in the supreme of abodes: by the sūtra of the uṇādi affixes, also the elision of the n of ana; and by the rule "in the seventh, variously," the case ending is not elided. Pitāmaha, "the Grandfather," because he is the father even of the Prajāpatis, the Lords of Creatures.

Hiraṇyagarbha, "Golden Womb": as the Smṛti treatise says:

Viṣṇu, the Self-Existent Bhagavān,
Wishing to bring forth the various creatures,
First created the waters
And cast his seed into them.
A golden-hued and most beautiful
Egg arose by itself from the waters;
From it the self-existent Sacrifice was born,
Renowned as Brahmā, the Self-Existent.
Within that golden-hued one
The Bhagavān dwelt for a year;
The egg became two halves,
And from them above and below took form.

By figurative usage, the golden-hued egg is "gold," and its core is the "womb." Lokeśa, "Lord of the World," means the chief. He is also called Svayambhū, "Self-Existent": the affix kvip, for svayaṃbhū.

Caturānana, "Four-Faced": Brahmā emanated a maiden called Tilottamā and himself became infatuated with her. As he gazed at her going in the four directions, right, left and so on, four faces arose. When he gazed at her going upward, an upper face arose, making five. Īśvara saw this from the sky and, as punishment for that shameful act, cut off the upper face. From then on he was known as Four-Faced, because he has four faces.

He is also called Dhātā, "the Maker": to the root dadhāti, in the sense of sustaining, the affix tṛc. Because he arose from the lotus of Viṣṇu's navel, he is called Abjayoni, "Lotus-Born."

Druhiṇa, "the Offender": because he was infatuated with the maiden he himself had emanated, his upper face was cut off; hence he is the Offender. By the rule "from druhi, variously," dru; and for dakṣi and the like, also in.

Because he wishes to bring the elements into being, he is called Viriñci, "Producer of the Various." It is stated from the root raca, "in the sense of striving separately," which belongs to the cur class and the rest. For the agent, the affix ac. Because the word belongs to the group "pṛṣodara, druhiḍa and the rest," the a becomes i, and there is also the augment num. Viriñcha: because he produces the various, he is Viriñci. He is also called Kamalāsana, "Lotus-Seated": this is the view of the master Rabhasa. That he is called Viriñci because he is skilled in and devoted to building the cities of the three worlds is also the view of the master Sambalī.

Sraṣṭā, "the Creator," because all beings are held to have arisen from him. Sṛjatī: with tṛc, by the rule "for sṛji and dṛśa, before jhal, am."

Prajāpati, "Lord of Creatures," is the chief of all beings.

He is also called Vedhas, "the Knower": vid, in the sense of knowing. From the in of vidhi, the affix asi.

Viśvasṛj, "Maker of the Universe": this arose because the god Brahmā thought, at the time when the eon was forming, "I shall produce other beings as well." From sṛjati with viśva, the affix kvip. Because the word belongs to the group "nyaṅku and the rest," at the end of a word and before val the first c becomes a guttural; and for a vowel that is not the first, since the n of nipāta is not desired in that domain, the form is viśvasṛka. In the system of those who do not accept sṛji in the group "nyaṅku and the rest," it becomes ṣ: viśvasṛḍ. The Pāṇinian masters, in the rule "ku for the affix kvin," take the view that this is correctly understood as the substitution "the affix kvin, after whatever has the affix kvin"; on that view the final of what has the affix kvin becomes a guttural: svagbhyāma. For what does not end in kvisa: rajjusṛgbhyāma. On the view that understands it as not substituted, for what ends in kvip: rajjusṛgbhyāma, viśvasṛbhyāma. Likewise prajāsṛka and so on.

With vidadhāti: by the rule "after pra, ma and the rest, from dadhā, ki," vidhi. These are the names of Brahmā.

With four and a half verses comes Viṣṇu. He is Viṣṇu, "the Pervader," because all beings are pervaded and bound by him. By the rule "from sṛ and viṣi, kit," the affix nu.

Because he relies on various paths he is nara, and since he is the son of that very one he is Nārāyaṇa. By the rule "from the prior word, for a name," the change to ṇa; under the view "by others too," the lengthening.

Vāsudeva, and also Nārāyaṇa: the Trikāṇḍaśeṣa says, "Vasudeva is Viśvarūpa, He of All Forms."

Kṛṣṇa, "the Dark One," because he possesses the quality of darkness.

Vaikuṇṭha comes from his mother's name: because he was born of the mother called Vikuṇṭhā, by the rule "from the remainder," the affix aṇ.

Viṣṭaraśravas: he is heard to dwell at the foot of the viṣṭara tree. The affix asun.

Hṛṣīkeśa, "Lord of the Senses," is the master of the senses.

Dāmodara, "Garland-Bellied": the goddess called Fame is Viṣṇu's wife. She wished to place a garland of flowers on his head, but since she could not reach it she placed it on his belly. Because he is marked by that, he is Dāmodara.

Keśava, "Slayer of Keśin": it is told that Viṣṇu slew the asura called Keśin. For keśin, in the sense "he slew the asura," the affix ḍa. Because the word belongs to the group "pṛṣodara and the rest," there are changes of letters. So too it is said:

Because you slew Keśin,
Therefore I heard it taught:
Keśava, as you are called in the world,
Is a name that arose from that account.

Mādhava: because he descended from the lineage of the man called Madhu, he is known as Mādhava.

Ātmabhū, "Self-Born," because he himself slays beings and brings them forth. The affix kvi.

Daityāri, "Enemy of the Daityas": the asura called Giver was slain by him; hence he is Daityāri.

Puṇḍarīkākṣa, "Lotus-Eyed": in order to worship Rudra, Viṣṇu made a torma offering in the form of a thousand white lotuses. When Rudra made one of them vanish, Viṣṇu plucked out one of his own eyes and set it in the place of the white lotus; hence he is known as Lotus-Eyed. By the rule "klṛ sklṛ, for one's own limb," the affix ṣac.

Govinda, "the Cowherd": when he was born into a clan of cowherds and a great rain was falling, he lifted up a mountain and protected them; thereupon the brahmins appointed him lord of the cowherds. By the rule beginning "dhāri pāri," the affix śa; by the rule "of śa, muca and the rest," the augment num.

Garuḍadhvaja, "Garuḍa-Bannered": the garuḍa is his emblem.

Pītāmbara, "Clad in Yellow," from his garments.

Acyuta, "the Unfallen": his station is permanent, without decline or change.

Śārṅgin: Śārṅga, "the Horn Bow," is Viṣṇu's bow; because he possesses it, the affix ini.

From the word viṣu, the meanings of the word "many" are extended, so it is an indeclinable. The master Bopālita says: "Since viṣu is explained as having various meanings, why is it classified here in the group of indeclinables? That is elaborated elsewhere." Hence Ratnamati explains in the sūtra on "loma and the rest" that viṣu itself is formed from that by "joining five" and by "kinta." Because he pervades all beings he is Viṣvaksena, written viśvaklena. Here, by the rule "for a name, not after ku," there is no ṣ after the guttural class.

Janārdana, "Crusher of Jana": because he slew the asura called Jana, "Being," who dwelt in the ocean, he is the Crusher of Jana. From "nandi and the rest," the affix lyu. Others say he is the Crusher of Beings at the time of the destruction of the eon.

Upendra, "the Lord Above," is the lord above the surface of the earth, the chief of the world. Uparāntra, through the group "pṛṣodara and the rest," is made into upendra. So it is said:

Indra, upon the path above me,
And you, abiding as lord of the earth:
Kṛṣṇa is called the Lord Above;
He is indeed the god of heaven.

Or else, because he is near to Indra, he is Upendra, "Near-Indra," a compound referring to another thing.

Indrāvaraja, "Born after Indra," is the younger brother of Indra, the lord of the gods; it is told that because he slew the asuras, the enemies of the gods, he is Indrāvaraja.

From that as his emblem and from his use of it, he is Cakrapāṇi, "Wheel in Hand": the first of the right hands among his four hands holds the wheel.

Padmanābha: the navel of Viṣṇu is the hub of a lotus. By the rule "from nābhi," the affix ac.

Madhuripu, "Enemy of Madhu," because he is the enemy of the asura called Madhu.

Because Viṣṇu pervades every dwelling place whatsoever, he is Vāsudeva, "the God of the Dwelling": by the rule "variously, from kṛ, vā, pā, ji, mi, svadi, sādhya and sṛ," the affix uṇ. Since he is also the dwelling, he is the God of the Dwelling. Vāsudeva, "Son of Vasudeva," is also his name; that was already explained earlier, in the section explaining Nārāyaṇa.

Trivikrama, "He of the Three Strides": in order to deceive the asura Bali, "the Strong," he pressed down with three strides. Viṣṇu took on the form of a dwarf before the asura Bali and asked for a single stride of ground at a time; when Bali said, "Take three," he enlarged his body, set his two feet on Mount Meru and on the floor of the ocean, and, since there was no ground left for the third, set it on Bali's head. Therefore he is known as Trivikrama.

Devakīnandana, "Delighter of Devakī," is so designated by the name of Viṣṇu's mother. From deva, for offspring, the affix iñ after a; by the rule "and before kit" and the like, a becomes aic. Because it is a prior name, "vṛddhyaṃ bhāva ity anārṣam" indicates a class; from this, by the rule "for a human class," the affix ṅīṣ. Daivikī is the goddess, and the one who delights her is her son.

Śauri, "of Śūra's line": in the line of Viṣṇu's grandfather there arose one called Śūra, "the Hero," so he belongs to that lineage.

He is also called Śrīpati, "Lord of Śrī."

Puruṣottama, "Supreme Person": the supreme person is the self, and therefore, because he is the supreme self, he is called Puruṣottama.

Vanamālin, "Wearer of the Garland," because he wears a garland of forest flowers: from "vrīhi, mālā and the rest," the affix iṇi.

He is also called Balidhvaṃsin, "Destroyer of Bali": since he has the disposition of destroying the asura Bali, by the rule beginning "from a class," the affix ṇini.

Kaṃsārāti: the asura was called Kaṃsa, "Bronze"; he dragged him by both feet, dashed him down and slew him.

Adhokṣaja, "Born from Below": Brahmā was born from an egg and Īśvara is self-arisen, but this one was born from the lower faculty, that is, born from a womb.

Viśvambhara, "the All-Filler": because all beings are pervaded and filled by him, he is the All-Filler. By the rule beginning "bhṛ, vṛ, tṛ, ji," the affix kha.

Kaiṭabhajit, "Conqueror of the Asura," because he slew the asura called Kaiṭabha.

Vidhu, "the Smiter": he dashes the asuras against rocks and the ground and slays them. By the rule "from iṣi, vyādha, bhidi, gṛdhi, dhṛṣi, pṛthi and mṛdi, ku, and of these ka in the sense of going," yaṇ is changed to ik, giving vidhuḥ.

Śrīvatsalāñchana, "Marked with the Śrīvatsa": on his chest there is a particular tuft of hair shaped like the śrīvatsa, a distinctive mark of a great person. Because he is marked by that sign, he is called "marked by the śrīvatsa."

Śrīvatsalāñchana, Adhokṣaja, Mādhava and Svayambhū come from the Ratnakośa. In these, words such as "marked by the śrīvatsa" are formed from what marks him and what he uses. These are the synonyms for Viṣṇu's name.

Vasudeva, "God of Wealth," plays with jewels: from "action," the affix aṇ. Ānakadundubhi, "He of the Kettledrum," knows the exceptional art of drumming. This is the name of Viṣṇu's father.

With two verses comes Balabhadra, who is Viṣṇu's elder brother. Balabhadra, "Excellent in Strength": he has exceptional strength. Because he slew the asura Pralamba he is called Pralambaghna, "Slayer of Pralamba": by the rule "ṭak," the affix ṭak; by the rule "also in the sense of going, from hana," the a is elided; by the rule "kjinna, the ha of hana," it becomes a guttural. Baladeva, "God of Strength," is the one endowed with exceptional strength: he is strength and he is also a god, so he is Baladeva. Here, from "arśa and the rest," the affix ac. Acyuta is Viṣṇu, and because he was born before him he is called Acyutāgraja, "Elder Brother of Acyuta."

Rīṅ is in the sense of hearing. From what ends in bic, the affix matup, and since "the asterism Revatī" is read among "gaura and the rest," by the rule "for a name," v optionally. Because she was born at the time when the moon is joined with the asterism marked by it, she is called Revatī, "Born under Revatī." By "from a division of time, for an asterism," the affix aṇ. By the rule "for vatśāla, from asterisms, in the plural," it is elided. When aṇ and the like are elided, by the rule "luk gauṇyādīnām" ṅīṣ too is elided; then again, from "gaura and the rest," ṅīṣ. The one who delights her is her husband, Revatīramaṇa, who delights her with his perfect form. In the sense of "causing to hold to the end," by the rule "from jva and the rest, for aṇ," the affix ṇa.

Kāmapāla, "Protector of Desires," because he protects desires and sports: by that rule, the affix aṇ.

Since his weapon is the plough, he is Halāyudha, "Plough-Weaponed."

Nīlāmbara, "Clad in Blue," from his garments.

Rohiṇī is the wife of Vasudeva. Since he is her son, he is Rauhiṇeya: for offspring, from "śubhra and the rest," the affix ḍhak.

Tāla is a kind of tree. So the Sāmba Purāṇa says:

When the banners are raised,
The tāla banner is made in the form of a tāla tree,
And the makara banner has the shape of a makara.

The master Rabhasa also explains that he is so called because he holds the pestle, the plough and the tāla.

Saṅkarṣaṇa, "the Good Dragger," because with his plough he drags his enemies thoroughly: from "nandi and the rest," the affix lyu.

He is also called Sīrapāṇi, "Plough in Hand." Here sīra, lāṅgala and hala, all meaning plough, are his hand emblems, from what marks him and what he uses.

Kālindībhedana, "Crusher of the Nāga": it is told that he tore apart with his plough weapon and slew the nāga Kālindī of the river Yamunā. From "nandi and the rest," the affix lyu.

Bala, "the Strong," is endowed with a plough of exceptional strength: from "arśa and the rest," the affix ac. These are the names of Bala.

From Madana up to Brahmasū are the names of Kāma, the son of Viṣṇu. Madana, "the Intoxicator," because he intoxicates and maddens with desire: from "nandi and the rest," the affix lyu; by the rule "he intoxicates," the affix kip.

Manmatha, "Churner of the Mind," distracts the mind.

Māra, "the Slayer": through the ten stages of desire and the rest, by the force of passion he even causes death.

Pradyumna, "the Mighty," because he is hard for anyone to repel.

The makara of the fish family is itself a fish; because he is marked by it, he is Mīnaketana, "Fish-Bannered."

Kandarpa, "Crusher of Brahmā": it is told that even Brahmā, struck by the weapon of utter delusion, became infatuated with the maiden he himself had emanated. Here the change of "brahmaṇi dṛstavān" into "kandarpaḥ" is by "pṛṣodara and the rest." Or else kam, ending in am, is an indeclinable expressing pleasure; since he possesses pleasure he is the Pleasurable. For these, by "dṛṣyati urṇasati," the affix ac. From dṛsta itself, darpakaḥ.

Darpaka, "the Proud," because he makes everyone delight in desire.

Anaṅga, "the Bodiless": when Īśvara was dwelling on Himālaya, the snowy mountain, being worshipped by the goddess Gaurī, the gods fell into doubt, saying, "Is it true that he is practicing austerities together with Gaurī?" So they sent the god Kāma there to look. The god Kāma shot his five arrows one after another. At the first and the second, not even a stirring of thought arose in Īśvara; at the third and the fourth there was a mere stirring of thought; and when the fifth struck, through his staying with Gaurī his austerity was broken. In the next instant he regretted it and wondered, "What went wrong?" Knowing that the fault was Kāma's, fire came forth from his three eyes and burned up the god Kāma. Then, since without desire altogether there would be no taking of birth, he made Kāma a being without a body.

Kāma, "Desire," is the desire for union and the like. For kāmayati, in the sense of generating the wish for pleasure, and for the one who unites, the affix ac after what ends in ṇi. By the rule "also after what ends in am," shortening; but by the rule "not of kami, ami and cam," there is none.

His five arrows are called the Maddener, the Enticer, the Utter Deluder, the Drier and the Killer. So the great commentary on Jayadeva's treatise on prosody says:

The Delighter, the Enticer,
The Deluder, likewise the Swooner,
And the Killer: these are proclaimed
To be the five arrows of the Lord of Desire.

Smara, "Remembrance," because he is remembered in one's own mind: smara, the affix ac.

Śambarāri: the asura called Śambara was slain by him, and therefore he is the Enemy of Śambara. Again, because through attachment and the like he slays the five senses, he is also the enemy of restraint.

Manasija, "Mind-Born," because the union of man and woman and the like arise from one's own mind. By the rule "in the manner of amorous play he is born in the minds of living beings," the affix ḍa. By the rule "the seventh, variously," elision.

Kusumeṣu, "Flower-Arrowed": he pierces the heart without wounding the body.

Ananyaja, "Born of No Other": because he arises from himself, he is not born from another.

Puṣpadhanvan, "Flower-Bowed," because desire increases.

Rati is the wife of the god Kāma; hence he is Ratipati, "Lord of Rati." He is also called the Lord of Supreme Delight, and also Makaradhvaja, "Makara-Bannered." Ātmabhū, "Self-Born," means that he is not from another.

Brahmasū, "Impeller of Austerity," because he casts austerity far away. Sū is in the sense of impelling; by the rule "brahma tapaḥ suvati," the affix kvip. Brahmasū is Kāma. In the Amaramālā, after the word brahmasū, the word i is read, from its denoting Kāma. Kṛcchayoḥ: Mīnaketu, Pañcabāṇa, Kusumeṣu, Brahmasū, I, Aniruddha and Uṣāpati. The Answers to Questions, the Necklace of the Learned, composed by the Servant of Rudra, says that the one fearless of all phenomena is Brahmā himself. Rabhasa, however, applies the name to his son Aniruddha. Scripture says that Aniruddha arose from the lotus of Brahmā's navel.

Viśvaketu, "Banner of the World": because all the three realms are pervaded by his rays of light, he is the Banner of the World. The master Rabhasa says that because he holds a banner on a bright flag, he marks. Viśva means what is pervaded, and ketu is his bright splendor: a locative compound of pervading and its ground. The Trikāṇḍaśeṣa says it is ketu because he appears unobstructed of himself. There the words are construed as "the ṛśya antelope is the emblem of his banner." The sword weapon belongs to Aniruddha. Likewise "evarṣyaketana" is also from scripture; by this, the first repha of the word ṛśya and the first vowel as well. This is explained in the Sāmba Purāṇa:

The tāla banner is made in the form of a tāla tree,
The makara banner is made in the shape of a makara;
So too, what is placed near
Is said to be near the ṛśya.

Aniruddha, "the Unobstructed," because others cannot hold him back. Uṣā is the daughter of the asura called Bāṇa, "Arrow." These are the sons of the god Kāma.

From Lakṣmī up to Haripriyā, these are names of Viṣṇu's wife. Lakṣmī, "She Who Marks," because she marks and shows everything: by the rule "from lakṣi, optionally muṭ," the affix ī. Since it ends in ī, there is no elision of su. If one asks how she is Lakṣmī: when she is addressed as "O glorious one," it becomes this: "matallakṣmī vaja svakañci dapara miti; from erakanti, ṅīṣ," says the master Rakṣita. By the rule "for stating together clearly," here, for the correct statement "excellent body," the master Pūrṇacandra, applying the separately established example kukkuṭa to "na sadī uṅ" and teaching it, explains the mention of aṅ, it, as, īt and ūt. In his system, although there is no ṅīṣ, there is shortening in the vocative. Other systems, in the Companion to the Roots, explain that names such as lakṣmī are not shortened.

Padmā, "She of the Lotus," because she always holds a lotus in her hand: from "arśa and the rest," the affix ac. Padma means kamala, hence Kamalā. The intention behind stating it even without distinction is that it is not applied to the words "mud-born" and "lake-born."

Śrī, "Glory," because she is to be resorted to: for śṛ, sru, dru, pru and ju, kvip with lengthening. "From erakanti, ṅīṣ, for śṛ and for as," says the master Maitreyarakṣita in the commentary Dhātupradīpa.

The conch, discus, club, sword and jewel of Lakṣmī's lord bear, in that order, the names Pāñcajanya and the rest. In the western ocean there dwelt an asura called Pañcajana, who had taken the form of a timi fish; because the conch was born from his bones, it is called Pāñcajanya, "Sprung from Pañcajana": from gambhīra and pañcajana, the affix ñya.

Sudarśana, "Fair to See," because the faithful see it with ease: by the rule "from śāsi, yudhi, dṛśi, dhṛṣi and mṛṣa," the affix yuc.

This club of Viṣṇu entered the earth and delighted the earth, hence Kaumodakī: by the rule "from the remainder," the affix aṇ.

The sword Pāñcajanya is Nandaka, "the Delighter": in the sense of wishing, the affix vun.

Ṣṭubha is in the sense of stiffening. Because it makes the earth stiff, it is the Earth-Stiffener, Hari's jewel, Kaustubha: it makes things unmoving and firm like a pillar. By the rule "from nāryanta," there is no ṣ; from "near the end in ik," kaumdak, the affix ka. From "the remainder of this," the affix aṇ. These are the emblems of Viṣṇu.

With one verse come the names of Garuḍa. Suparṇa, "Fair-Winged," is endowed with excellent wings: in the sense of possession, the affix matup. By the rule "ṛpa," the change to v: kostubha; this is prevented by "yava and the rest." Garu is poison, and because he destroys it he is ḍa; by "pṛṣodara and the rest," Garuḍa. Because he is a descendant of the sage Tṛkṣa, he is Tārkṣya: from "garga and the rest," the affix yañ.

Vainateya, "Son of Vinatā," because he is the offspring of his mother Vinatā: by the rule "from dī, āsti and ūd," the affix ḍhak. The story is this. Vinatā and Kadrū had both been made wives of a certain king. Once, when they saw the horse of the sun's chariot, Vinatā said to Tṛkṣa, "It is white," and Kadrū said, "It is black." The two made a wager: "Let us see; whoever is right shall be the king's queen, and whoever is wrong shall be a servant." Then Kadrū paid homage and service to a certain nāga and said, "Make the sun's horse appear black. If you do not, I shall have to be a servant." He agreed. The sun's horse appeared black, and Vinatā became a servant. When she had fallen into great suffering, the sage Tṛkṣa gave her three eggs and said, "These will help you, but do not break them for seven days." She grew impatient, and when on the third day she broke one, it became lightning and went off into the sky. Then on the sixth day she broke one, and it became the dawn star.
