By "kapiri and the rest," the l is changed to r, so "karka" too is as before. Lāñchana is the Marked, because by it the moon is marked. Cihna is mark, lakṣma is sign, and lakṣaṇa is characteristic.

Among the names for beauty, the moon's lovely radiance: suṣamā is the All-Beautiful, because it is supreme among all beauties. Paramaśobhā is supreme beauty. Śobhā is also beauty or fairness, because śubha and śumbha have the meaning of auspicious and good. Kānti is beauty or loveliness, from the verbal root kan, given in the sense of shining. Dyuti is excellence or brightness. Chavi is clarity or splendor.

The words ending with mahikā are names of frost and snow. Avaśyāya: because it is cool all around, it is frost. Nīhāra, "gathering decisively," is hoarfrost. "The prefix is altered" means that its short vowel changes in many instances under suffixes such as ghañ, so that nihāra here becomes nīhāra. Tuṣāra is pale rain, that is, sleet. Tuhina is dew. Himam is snow. Prāleya is snow as well. Himācala, the Snow-Unmoving, is snow that stays unmoving; it is called the snow mountain or glacier mountain. Mahikā is the Soother: by "maha is in the sense of honoring," because it dispels the torment of heat. Himānī and himasaṃhati, "possessing snow" and "mass of snow," are the glacier mountain.

Among the synonyms for the cool as an object of touch, śītam and suṣīma are in the sense of cool. Śiśira is cold or cool. Jaḍa is frozen: it is the word for water, jala, with the l changed to ḍ by the rule of pṛṣodara and the rest. Tuṣāra is the cold of a swirling snowstorm. "Śītalā bhūḥ," in the feminine, is the cool touch of the earth. Likewise "śītalaṃ jalam," in the neuter, is cool water.

"Śītalo vātaḥ," with masculine gender, is a cool wind; here śītalaḥ means a chill gust. Śītaḥ and hima also mean cool.

Among the names of the stars: the particular star that stays unmoving above all the planets is dhruva, called the Steadfast. Because it is the son of the royal sage Uttānapāda, "Upturned-Foot," by the sūtra "from many," the patronymic suffix iñ is added, giving Auttānapādi, called Son of Upturned-Foot.

Among the names of Canopus: aga is the Unmoving, that is, a mountain, and tyāya is casting away or hurling. Because it belongs to pāraskara and the rest, by the rule that adds the augment suṭ, it becomes Agastya, "Mountain-Caster" or "Mountain-Hurler." Long ago the sage called Varuṇa, god of the waters, saw the goddess Urvaśī and his seed was spilled. It was placed in a pot, and from it this sage was born; so he is known as the Pot-Born, Kumbhasambhava. Because he is the son of the sages Mitra and Varuṇa, the taddhita suffix iñ is applied, by "ata iñ," for their descendant, giving Maitri and Vāruṇi, called Son of the Friend and Son of Varuṇa.

The wife of this sage Agastya is Sadharmiṇī, called She Who Has the Quality, because she possesses the quality of pride. Because she both overpowers the pride of visible form and is a seal, she is Lopāmudrā, called the Overpowering Seal.

Among the general names for stars: nakṣatram, because it does not perish or does not change, is a lunar mansion. Ṛkṣam is the Goer, from the root ṛṣi, given in the sense of going, with the suffix kha. Bham is the Radiant: bhā, in the sense of shining, with the suffix ḍa. Tārā and tārakā also mean star; they differ only in their suffixes. Uḍḍupa is the Flyer.

Among the names of the asterism Aśvinī: Dākṣāyaṇī is called Daughter of the Clear One, because she is the daughter of the Lord of Creatures, Prajāpati, called Dakṣa, the Clear One. Aśvayuj and aśvinī are the asterism Aśvinī: because it has the shape of a horse, it is the Horse-Possessing or the Mare-Possessing.

Among the names of Viśākhā: rādhā is the Accomplisher, the suffix ac on the root rādha, given in the sense of accomplishing. Viśākhā is the asterism Viśākhā, the Wide-Pervading: the suffix ac on the root śākhṛ, given in the sense of pervading.

Among the names of Puṣya: puṣya is the Nourisher, and tiṣya is the Satisfier. Because this lunar mansion brings about what is desired, the suffix kyap is applied to puṣ, flourishing, to sidh, accomplishing, and to tiṣ, satisfying.

Among the names of Dhaniṣṭhā: śraviṣṭhā is the Wealthy-Famed, a compound made of dhaniṣṭha, wealthy, and śṛṇoti, hearing or fame. Samādhaniṣṭha is called the All-Wealthy.

Among the names of Bhādrapadā: prauṣṭhapadā is Bhādrapadā. Pada is the print left where a cow sets down its foot; the word "foot" serves to convey likeness. The word bhadrapada means an excellent limb or part, construed as "it follows in an excellent station, like the lord of the planets."

Among the names of Mṛgaśiras: mṛgaśirā is called Deer-Head, because this asterism is set in a shape resembling the head of a deer. That same asterism is called āgrahāyaṇī, Beginning of the Year, from the usage by which the full moon in Mṛgaśiras is declared the start of the year. Ilvala, the Moving, is five very small stars set at its head.

For the names of the planet Jupiter there is one verse. Bṛhaspati is the guru of the gods, because Jupiter became the guru of the gods. Surācārya is Teacher of the Gods. Gīṣpati is Lord of Voice or Lord of Speech. Dhiṣaṇa is the Intelligent. Guru is the Teacher. Jīva is the Sustainer: the Rāmāyaṇa tells that Jupiter heals with mantras and medicines the gods wounded by the asuras in battle. Because he is a descendant of the sage Aṅgiras, he is Āṅgirasa, called Lineage of the Sun. Vācaspati is Lord of Speech. Citraśikhaṇḍija is called Son of the Variegated-Crested. There were seven sages sharing the name "Crested," and because Jupiter was born of Aṅgiras, one of the Variegated-Crested, he is so called.

For the names of the planet Venus there are two quarter-verses. Śukra is the Bright, because it is without sorrow and excellent in every way. Daityaguru is guru of the asuras, as it is said:

As teacher of the gods and of the asuras,
the poet's son, Venus, was chosen.

Kāvya is Son of the Poet: to kavi the patronymic suffix ṇya is given. Uśanas is the Radiant or the Lovely, formed from the root vaśa, given in the sense of being lovely. Bhārgava is Son of the Sage Bhṛgu, because he is the son of the sage called Bhṛgu, "Evil-Abandoner." Kavi is the Poet.

For Mars, the Auspicious Planet, there is half a verse of names. Aṅgāraka is Red-Eye. Lohitāṅga is Red-Body. Kuja and bhauma are Earth-Born and Sprung from Earth. Mahīsuta is Son of the Earth.

For the names of Mercury there is one quarter-verse. Rauhiṇeya is Born of Rohiṇī, because his mother is the lunar mansion Rohiṇī. Budha is Mercury. Saumya is Son of the Moon, because his father is the moon.

For the names of Saturn there is one quarter-verse. Sauri is Son of the Sun, and śanaiścara is the Slow-Goer.

For the names of Rāhu there is half a verse. Tamas is the Dark One. Rāhu is Rāhu, because he afflicts the sun and lets it go. Svarbhānu is Illuminator of Heaven. Saiṃhikeya is called Son of the Lioness, because he is the son of Siṃhikā, "the Lioness," sister of the sage Hiraṇyakaśipu. Vidhuntuda is the Moon-Crusher: vidhu is the moon and tuda is crushing.

The seven sages, the Variegated-Crested, are these: Marīci, the Radiant; Aṅgiras, the Sun; Atri, Knower of Continuity; Pulastya, Vast-Light; Pulaha, Crusher of Asuras; Kratu, Possessor of Sacrifice; and Vasiṣṭha, called Establisher of Abodes.

Rāśi is a house of the zodiac. Udaya is rising. Lagna is the ascendant, the conjunction of time. Meṣa is the Ram, and vṛṣa is the Bull.

Then come the names of the sun. Sūra is the sun. Sūrya is the sun, because by spreading its light it brings happiness. Aryaman is the One to Be Shown, for the Sāmba's Ancient Account says, "It is the supreme thing to be shown, the light of the world." Āditya is Born of Aditi, because he was born of the mother called Aditi. Dvādaśātman is the Twelvefold Self, because its bodies have the nature of twelve parts.

Divākara is the Day-Maker, because its nature is to make things appear. Bhāskara is the Light-Maker. Ahaskara is the Maker of Appearance. Bradhna is the Destroyer of Dimness. Prabhākara and vibhākara are the Thorough Illuminator and the Full Illuminator. Bhāsvat is the Clear Light or the Luminous. Vivasvat is Eye-Form, because it abides as the eye for all beings. Saptāśva is the Seven-Horsed, because it has seven horses. Haridaśva is the Green-Horsed, because its horses are green in color. Uṣṇaraśmi is the Hot-Rayed, because the scorching of its rays is exceedingly hot.

Vikartana is the Transformed. The craftsman of the gods. As the commentary on prosody says:

The wind, Mind-Swift, Five-Colored,
likewise the Spotted One,
Proclaimer, Great Banner,
and Mountain-Crusher are the horses of the sun.

Because the All-Maker worked as a craftsman upon the sun's body, it is so called.

Arka is the Worthy of Worship, because the gods worship it: ghañ on the root arca, given in the sense of worshipping. Mārtaṇḍa is the Unrounded. Mihira is the Rain-Pourer: the suffix kirac is applied to the root miha, given in the sense of sprinkling, because it shares its root with megha, cloud. Aruṇa is the Light-Goer: ṛ, in the sense of going, with the suffix unan. Pūṣan is the Nourisher. [Destroyed by Rain. Drokmi, Düle Gyal and Lo Drakpa render it "Thick Womb," and Sapaṇ "Unrounded"; in the commentary the reason is not clear.] Pūṣan is so called because it nourishes the bodily support.

Dyumaṇi is the Jewel of the Sky, because it is the sky's ornament. Taraṇi is the Deliverer. Mitra is the Friend, because it is loving toward all beings. Citrabhānu is the Many-Colored Ray. Vairocana is the Illuminator. Vibhāvasu is the Rich in Light, because it possesses the substance of light. Grahapati is the Lord of the Planets, because it has become lord even of the gods' guru and the others. Tviṣāmpati is the Lord of Radiance. Ahaḥpati is the Lord of the Day. Bhānu is the Luminous: bhā, in the sense of shining; by "after dā and bhā, nu," the suffix nu. Haṃsa is the Destroyer, because it destroys dimness: han, in the sense of harming; by the sūtra beginning with vṛ, the suffix sa. Sahasrāṃśu is the Thousand-Rayed, because it has a thousand rays. Tapana is the Scorcher, because it scorches with its hot rays. Savitṛ is the Radiant, whose activity is to send forth rays. Ravi is Light-Form: by "because its form has light," the suffix i. Thus the sun has thirty-seven names.

As for the names of the sun's retinue: the twelve gods, Indra and the rest, dwell at its sides, and the chief among them is called the Abider. On the sun's left is Śakra, in the form of the Staff. On its right is the fire god Agni, reddish-yellow in color. At the southern gate of the second tier is Yama, who bears the name Māṭhara, the Abider, because he stays close to the sun like its garment. At the other sides of the gate stand those called Caṇḍāṃśu, the Fierce-Rayed, and Pāripārśvaka, the Encircler.

With half a verse come the names of the sun's charioteer, the Thighless. Sūrasūta is the sun's charioteer. Aruṇa is the dawn, because it moves with a pale red color. Anūru is the Thighless, because this charioteer's limbs are incomplete. Kāśyapi is of Kāśyapa's line, because he is a descendant of the sage Kāśyapa: to kāśyapa, the patronymic suffix iñ is applied by "ata iñ." Garuḍāgraja is the Elder of Garuḍa: that the dawn was born before Garuḍa has already been told above, in the story of Vinatā.

With half a verse come the names of the halo of the sun. Pariveṣa is the Encircling. Paridhi is the Enveloping, or the sun's halo. Upasūryaka is the Near-Sun. Maṇḍala is the maṇḍala, the circle.

For the general names of the rays of the sun and other luminaries there are two verses. Kiraṇa is a ray, because it destroys dimness: on kṛñ, the suffix kyu. Usra is the Light-Maker, because light dwells in it: vasa, in the sense of dwelling, with the suffix rak, and ik in place of yaṇ. Mayūkha is the Measuring Goer, which is always near the sun: from mā, given in the sense of measuring, and ukhi, in the sense of going, with the suffix ac, changed to mayūkha by pṛṣodara and the rest. Aṃśu is the Goer to the Quarters: on am, given in the sense of sickness, the suffix ku and the augment śuk, in the sense of making go into the quarters. Gabhasti is the Light-Goer: ga is from gami with the suffix ḍa, in the sense of going; bhasti, appearance, is from the root bhasa, given in the sense of reviling and also of shining, with the suffix ktin, often. Hence the Light-Goer. Ghṛṇi and dhṛṣṇi also mean light or appearance. Bhānu is the Illuminator. Kara is ray. Marīci is also ray, or mirage. Dīdhiti is the Blazer: on the root dīdhī, given in the sense of shining, ktin and the augment iṭ.

Prabhā is light; ruc is clarity; ruci is appearance; tviṭ is lamp; bhā is appearance; ābhā is light; chavi is presence; dyuti is brightness or lightning; dīpti is blazing; rocis is clarity; śocis is shining lamp; prakāśa is full clarity; dyota is clarity; ātapa is all-scorching. Thus the three, prakāśa and the rest, are known in the world as light free of darkness.

Koṣṇam is lukewarm; that and the rest are names of warmth. Tigmam is hot, tīkṣṇam is very hot, and kharam is harsh or fiercely hot. Mṛgatṛṣṇā is called Deer-Thirst, because it appears as water to thirsty deer and stirs their craving. Marīci is mirage.

The words ending with samaya are names of time. Kāla is time because it is to be counted: by "kali is in the sense of number: it counts," ghañ. Diṣṭa is time because it shows. Anehas is time because it comes again and again. Samaya is time because it goes rightly.

Among the names of the lunar day: pakṣati is a lunar day, because it is the root of the fortnight. Pratipad is a lunar day, because it is the point at which the moon rises and wanes; both are the first lunar day. Tithi is the general name for the lunar day.

With two half-verses come the names of the day. Ghasra is the Darkness-Cooled, because it is far removed from darkness. Dina is day, and so the others are to be understood.

Pratyūṣa, aharmukha and the rest, "Darkness-Cleared," "Door of Day" and so on, are names of daybreak. Prabhāta is evening; that and the rest are names of the day's end and of night. Saparvan means "together with the juncture." Sandhi is the junction, and pratipad is the first lunar day.

Among the names of the full moon: paurṇamāsī is the full moon, and pūrṇimā is the Measurer made complete. Rākā and niśākara are the night and the Night-Maker.

With half a verse come the names of the new moon. In the word amāvāsyā, the word amā has the meaning "together" and is indeclinable. Because at this time the sun and the moon invariably dwell together, amāvāsyā is "dwelling together," that is, the new moon. It is also called "the moon unseen."

The time in which, for human beings, a natural, unforced movement of the eyelash occurs is called nimeṣa, the blink. Eighteen of these make a kāṣṭhā, which bears the name "station." Thirty of those make a kalā, a part [the name of two breaths]. Thirty of those make a kṣaṇa, an instant [the name of ten chu srang]. Twelve of those are called a muhūrta, a moment [the name of two chu tshod]. Thirty of those make an ahorātra, a day and night. Fifteen of those make a fortnight; and in their order, the bright fortnight bears the name "former" and the dark fortnight the name "latter."

Each pair of months, beginning with the month of Mṛgaśiras, makes a season, and three seasons make a course. Counting from the sun's transit into Puṣya up to Āṣāḍhā, the sun travels north, so this is the northern course. Counting from its transit into Āṣāḍhā up to Puṣya, it travels south, so this is the southern course. Two courses make a varṣa, a year. The month in which the full moon is joined with Puṣya is the month of Puṣya; the same is applied to Maghā and the rest.

With half a verse come the names of the month of Mṛgaśiras. The month marked by the full moon's conjunction with the lunar mansion Mṛgaśiras is mārgaśīrṣa, the month of Mṛgaśiras, or the first winter month. Sahā and mārga are the same month. Saha is in the sense of being able: because this month has ability, the suffix asun is applied, giving sahas, ability, as it is said, "It increases the strength of living beings." Āgrahāyaṇika is the Beginning of the Year, because the year is begun from this month.

The month in which the full moon is joined with Puṣya is pauṣa and taiṣa, the month of Puṣya, or the middle winter month. Here too sahas means increasing strength. Tapas is the Month of Austerity, and māgha is the month of Maghā, or the last winter month.

Phālguna is the month of Phalgunī; tapasya is the Month of Austerity; and phālgunika is the Possessor of Phalgunī, or the first spring month. Caitra and caitrika are the Possessor of Citrā, or the middle spring month. Madhu is the Desire-Granting Month, because in it everyone thinks of Kāmadeva, the god of desire: man is in the sense of knowing, and by the rule "of phali, pāṭi, nami, mani and jan, also nāk, dh and t," the suffix u is applied and the final n is changed to dh. Vaiśākha is the month of Vaiśākha, or the last spring month. Mādhava is the Honey-Circling, because in it there is much honey dwelling in the flowers.

Jyaiṣṭha is the month of Jyeṣṭhā, or the first summer month. Śukra is the Bright: as it is a name of the planet, so it is a name of this month. Śuci is the Purifier: as it is a name of fire and other things, so it is a name of this month. Āṣāḍha is the month of Āṣāḍhā, or the middle summer month. Śrāvaṇa is the Possessor of Hearing, because in it there is much hearing. The word nabhas means the sky, and it is also a name of this month. Śrāvaṇika is the month of Śravaṇā, or the last summer month.

Proṣṭhapada, the Bull's Foot, bhādra, bhādrapada, and again the Cow's Foot: these are the month of Bhādrapadā, or the first autumn month. Āśvina is the month of Aśvinī, or the middle autumn month. Iṣa is the Spectacle, from the root iṣa, given in the sense of going. Kārttika and kārttikika are the Possessor of the Pleiades and the month of the Pleiades, or the last autumn month. Bahula is the Holder of Many. These are the names of the individual months.

Among the names of the six seasons: hemanta is early winter; śiśira is late winter; vasanta is spring. Puṣpasamaya and surabhi, the Time of Flowers and the Bringer of Ease, are spring. Grīṣma is the hot season; uṣmaka is the time of heat; nidāgha is thorough burning; uṣmopagama is the arrival of warmth; uṣma is warmth; uṣmāgama is the coming of warmth; tapa is the time of heat. These are names of the hot season. Prāvṛṭ is the rainy season. Śarad is autumn.

For the names of the year there is half a verse: saṃvatsara, the year; vatsara, the Counted; abda, the Going-and-Coming; and hāyana, the Discarder.

One month, reckoned in human years, is one day and night of the pretas. Likewise one year, reckoned in human terms, is one day and night of the gods. So the northern course is the gods' day, and the southern course is their night. Twelve thousand years, each consisting of three hundred and sixty days and nights reckoned for the gods, are one age of the gods. One thousand ages of the gods are one day of Brahmā. As it is said:

These twelve thousand
are well known as an "age";
this same, multiplied by a thousand,
is declared to be one day of Brahmā.

That is, it is one thousand sets of the four human ages. As it is also said:

The Kṛta, the Tretā, the Dvāpara,
and the one called Kali: these are the four ages.
The sage teaches that one day of Brahmā
is to be known as a thousand of these.

Since the length of the night is the same as the length of the day, two thousand ages of the gods make one day and night of Brahmā; that is, two thousand sets of the four human ages. As it is said:

The day and night of the pretas
is a month; that of the gods, a year;
two thousand sets of the four ages
make Brahmā's pair, O best of the twice-born.

Of these, the length of the Kṛta age, in human years, is one million seven hundred and twenty-eight thousand years. The Tretā is one million two hundred and ninety-six thousand years. The Dvāpara is eight hundred and sixty-four thousand years. The Kali is four hundred and thirty-two thousand years.

As it is said in the Ādibuddha:

Zero, zero, sky, nāga, hand, sage, and the hare-marked one (the moon): this is the measure of the Kṛta age.
Sky, sky, zero, taste, nine, and the day-maker (the sun) are well known as the number of years of the Tretā.
Zero, zero, sky, Veda, taste, and the one called arm-goer: these truly are the years of the Dvāpara.
Zero, space, sky, eye, quality, and ocean become the number of years of the Kali.

Added together, these come to forty-three hundred thousand and twenty thousand years. So too it is said:

Heaven and space, zero, space, eye, time, ocean: this is the measure of the four ages summed together.

Here, nāga is eight. Hand is two. Sage is seven. The hare-marked one is one. Taste is six. The day-maker is twelve. Veda and ocean are four. Eye is two. Quality is three. Teeth is thirty-two. These numbers too are to be taken as the well-known numbers. The words heaven, sky, and space are synonyms for zero, and in calculation zero is indicated by a dot. All these separate ages are to be set down in figures in reverse order.

Thus the setting out (prastara) of the Kṛta age is, for example, 1,728,000; that of the Tretā, for example, 1,276,000; that of the Dvāpara, for example, 864,000; that of the Kali, for example, 432,000; and that of the four ages added together, for example, 432,000. Beginning from the dot at the end of each setting out (prastār), one should count in reverse order. How is that? As it is said:

One and ten, and likewise a hundred,
a thousand, ten thousand, a hundred thousand, and a million:
in order, each later one is tenfold.

Here, ten thousands is a ten thousand, and ten hundred thousands is a million. The million does not occur in the Kali and the Dvāpara, because their count ends at the hundred thousands. In this way, from the separate ages and from the figure (aṅka) of their sum, the years and so on explained earlier are easily understood.

A day and night of Brahmā is two kalpas of humans: keeping in mind the word "those," two kalpas are to be understood. Of these, the day of Brahmā is the kalpa of formation for humans, and the night is the kalpa of destruction.

Here, "other mind" (manvantara) refers to Manu, that is, the son of Brahmā. One day of Brahmā is fourteen of Manu. One day of Manu measures seventy-two ages of the gods; in human ages, that is two hundred and eighty-four. Those fourteen Manus come to one hundred and ninety-four ages of the gods, and in human ages to four thousand ages less twenty-four. And for the Manus this is a number of one million seven hundred and twenty-eight thousand human years, the measure of the Kṛta; there come to be fifteen junctions. As it is said in the Sūryasiddhānta:

Seventy ages together with one:
this is held to be the period of a Manu.
A number of years equal to the Kṛta,
that is declared to be the junction.
Those periods of the Manus, moreover,
are to be known as twelve in a kalpa.
With the first junction of the kalpa,
of the Kṛta's measure, they are declared to be fifteen.

One portion of the Kṛta age is the measure of the Kali age; two quarters are that of the Dvāpara; three quarters are that of the Tretā. Since these four ages revolve like a wheel, the fifteen junctions come to twenty-four human ages, which are six ages of the gods. Adding the junctions to the fourteen periods of Manu, the total comes to one thousand ages of the gods, and four thousand human ages. That is one day of Brahmā.

With half a verse, the names of the kalpa of destruction. sambarta is destruction. pralaya is destruction, because things completely dissolve. kṣaya is exhaustion, and anta is end. These are synonyms for destruction.

With one verse, the names of evil. pāpa is evil because through it one falls downward: the root prī, "to fall," with the suffix pa. kilviṣa is evil because it sports and makes one slack: kila means "to sport," and also "to slacken"; buk is an augment (āgama), a particle. kalmaṣaṃ is evil because it exhausts good karma: the root sai, "to waste away," and by the rule of sriṣodara and the like, the letter rā of karma is changed into la. kaluṣa is evil because it has the stain or turbidity of faults. bṛjina is "that which discards": it is evil because it abandons the good. ena, aghaṃ, and aṃghaṃ are also names of evil, and aṃgha is also found as aṃha, as in the Extensive Praise of Lokeśvara, which follows it. duritaṃ is "that which makes far," and duṣkṛtaṃ is "misdeed." These are names of evil.

With half a verse, Dharma. Because it upholds everything, it is dharma, that is, Dharma. puṇya is merit. śreyasī is "highly praised," or well-being. sukṛtaṃ is "well done." bṛṣa is called bañchataṃ, "desired," because it is to be desired: the suffix ka is applied to bṛṣ because it has ika as its penultimate.

With one verse, the names of happiness. muda is joy, prīti is delight, pramad is gladness, harṣa is elation, pramoda is rejoicing, mod is pleasure, sammāda is contentment, ānanda is bliss, thurānanda is the exhaustion of evil, śarma is comfort, śāda is the excellent, and suṣa is happiness.

With one verse, the names of virtue. sraśreyasaṃ is well-being. Here the word sva, a word whose nature is to denote a particular time, has the sense of praising the word that follows it. śibaṃ is peace. bhada is good: this bha is in the sense of virtue, and ran is the suffix. kalyāṇa is virtue. maṃgalaṃ is auspiciousness: magi, "to go," with the suffix alaca. śubha is virtue. bhābukaṃ, bhabikaṃ, and bhabyaṃ are fortune, the fortunate, and goodness. kuśala is "that which dispels the bad," that is, virtue. kṣema is well-being.

For the karma of virtue and non-virtue as such, half a verse. daibaṃ is "appointed by the gods," because it comes from the gods. diṣṭaṃ is "relied upon by the gods." bhāgadheya is "the fortunate": to bhāga is added dheya, a taddhita suffix in its own sense. bhāgyaṃ is one's share of fortune. niyatirbidhi is "the determining accomplisher."

For the names of cause as such, two lines. hetu is cause: from hi, "to go," in the sense of the action of going and transforming, the suffix ru by the rule "from kami mā ni jani hi." kāraṇa is "the maker": from kṛña, in the sense of the agent, the suffix laśyu and lengthening. bījaṃ is the seed, because it generates: jana, "to be born," with the suffix ḍa, and lengthening by the rule "a preverb standing before is lengthened in other cases too." nidanaṃ is the cause or basis, because it definitely bestows the result. ādikāraṇaṃ is the first maker.

With two lines, synonyms for self. kṣetrajñā is "the knower of the field," the field being the body. ātma is self: at, "to go," with the suffix manin and lengthening, by the rule "byoma and the like." puruṣa is person: pṛ, "to protect," with the suffix kuṣan, and changed into pur.

With half a verse, the names of living being. praṇī is a living being, because it holds and possesses the life breaths in the body. cetana is a sentient being. janmī is one who has birth. jantu and janyu are a person or a creature. śarīriṇa is one who has a body.

With half a verse, the names of mind. citta is mind, because it thinks. ceta is thinking. hṛdayaṃ is the heart. stantaṃ is mentation. hṛda is the core. mānasaṃ is the mental, and māna is mentation.

With one verse, the names of intellect. buddhi is intellect: mentation's understanding of a pot, a cloth, a chariot, and so on. manīṣa is "what the mind desires," or "where the mind goes." dhiṣaṇa is the one possessed of confidence. dhīḥ is intellect, because it thinks. śemuṣī is "that which rouses from sleep." mati is understanding. prekṣa is close looking, or realization. ubalabhi is apprehension. ceta is mind. sambida is correct knowing. pratiprajñapti is individual discernment. cetana is thinking. dhīḥ as non-forgetting, dhāraṇabatī as possessing the power of retention, medhā as a keen mind, and saṃkalpa as correct conception: these are particular kinds of intellect.

For the names of mental engagement: karmamānasaṃ is the action of mentation, cittabhoga is the enjoyment of mind, and manaskāra is attention.

For viewing an object by means of valid cognition, three names: caryu is investigation, saṃkhyā is enumeration, and bicāraṇa is analysis.

For supplementation, one line. adhyāhāra is supplementation: in order to complete what is incomplete, an additional word is to be supplied. tarka is reasoning: it is formed from the root kritī, "to cut," with the suffix ghaña and the reversal of letters. uha is conjecture.

For doubt, four names: bicikitsā is doubt, saṃśaya is uncertainty, saṃdeha is hesitation, and dvāpara is two-mindedness.

For the names of freedom from doubt: nirlaya is ascertainment, as in the example yathāsthāṇau sthaṇu ṛti pratīti nirlayanaṃ: it is certainty about the ground, through being convinced, regarding what stands as it stands, that it is indeed standing. niṣcaya is certainty: conviction in the nature of definite apprehension.

For the view that denies karma and its results, two names. methyādṛṣṭi is wrong view: apprehending what is not true. nāstikatā is nihilism: holding that there is no giving, there is no fire offering, there is no heaven, and there is no liberation.

byapāda is malice, and drohacittanaṃ is a harmful intention or a hostile intention, that is, malice.

For error, three names. bhrānti is error, like taking a rope for a snake: bhramu, "to waver," with the suffix kanti and lengthening. mithya is the wrong, and atibhrama is utter whirling.

With one verse, the names of acceptance. sambida is acceptance. āgu is taking a side: it is formed with the suffix ḍu applied to gami preceded by āṅ. pratijñā is a pledge. niyama is certainty, āśraba is what is to be heard, saṃśraba is what is rightly heard, aṅgikāra is making one's own, abhyupagam is acceptance, pratiśraba is what is to be heard individually, and samādhi is meditative absorption.

For the names of nirvāṇa: mukti is liberation, kaibalya is the unmixed or the isolated, nirbāṇa is nirvāṇa, śreyaḥ is the highly praised, niḥśreyas is the definite good, amṛta is the deathless or nectar, mokṣa is freedom, and apabarga is abandonment or release.

Next, in the section on colors, for the names of white: śukla is white, śubhra is "that which makes virtue," śuci is pure, śveta is bright white, biṣada is white-hued, śyeta is clear, and paṇḍar is pale white: from paḍi, "to go," the suffix aran, as with ambara, and lengthening by the rule "in others too." abadāta is white: daip, "to purify," with kta. sita is white: ṣi, "to bind," with kta. gaura is white: guṅ, "sound," with the suffix rak and ai. balakṣa, dhabala, and arju are also white.

hariṇa, pāṇḍara, and pāṇḍara are pale yellow, yellowish pale, and pale. iṣatpaṇḍa is slightly pale. dhūsara is ash-grey.

With half a verse, the names of black. kṛṣṇa is black, nīla is blue, asita is not white, śyāma is dark blue, and kāla, which in two Indian manuscripts of the root text appears as kilā, evidently a corruption, is black: from kala with ghaña. śyāmla is "bearing dark blue." maceka is deep black.

tpāta and gora are yellow, and haridrabha is the color of turmeric. pakhāśa, harita, and harita are names of green.

rohita is red, lohita is lac red, rakta is blood colored, śoṇa is reddish, and kokanarda is the color of the red lotus. aruṇa is an indistinct red, or the color of dawn. pāḍala is pink, a color in which white and red are mixed. śyabaḥ is yellowish black, and kapiśa is golden. dhūmra, dhumala, and kṛṇḍalohita are names for a mixture of black and red: dark red, smoke colored, and liver colored.

With half a verse, reddish yellow. kaḍara is reddish yellow. For kisala it is said, tatra nīlajītaḥ kapilaḥ, and there kapila is explained as bluish yellow. siṅga is the color of bezoar. kadru, that is siṅgala, is golden in color.

With half a verse, the names of variegated color. citra is bright or multicolored, like the color of a cuckoo's throat.

karmīr and kalmākha are dappled or mixed. śabala is variegated or dappled. karlura is manifold.

With one verse, words are explained. Here Brahmā is held to be the presiding deity, and since speech arises from him, it is brahmi, "born of Brahmā." Because it descended into the world of bharata, it is called bhārati; and it is bhāṣā, language, because it speaks. gīḥ is word, bāk is speech, bāṇī is utterance, and sārasvati is language, or Sarasvatī, or "possessing a lake": since it exists by depending on a lake, it ends in the possessive suffix. It is famed that long ago, from the world of Brahmā, it relied on the lake of Brahmā and descended into this world in the form of a river. byāhār is talk, ukti is an account or a word, lapitaṃ is speaking, bhāṣitaṃ is explaining, bacanaṃ is utterance, and baca is word.

Words that are not correctly formed according to grammar are apaśapta, corrupted sound; in the treatises of grammar all of these are called apabhraṃśa, the broken. In poetry it is the speech of cowherds and the like, and for that very reason it is well known as the broken speech of the world. Sound that expresses meaning by way of base and suffix is the correct, and the term refers to the sound of the treatise of Sanskrit. That, moreover, expresses meaning by being properly joined with words that end in tiṅ and sub.

For the Veda, three names: śruti, "what is heard"; beta, the Veda; and ambāya, instruction. These are the three. ṛka is the Proclaimer: ṛca, "to praise"; by the sense "it praises the gods," the suffix kvipa is applied. sāma is melodious words, and yajuṣī is sacrifice. śikṣa is training: this and the rest are the limbs of what is called "the heard." So too it is said:

Training, ritual, grammar,
etymology, the lore of the stars,
and the study of metre:
these are declared the six limbs of the Veda.

Of these, training examines the places, the instruments, and the efforts of the letters ā and so on, as in "ṛ ṭu ra ṣa from the crown of the head," and the like. kalpa, ritual, establishes the acts of sacrifice. byākaraṇa, grammar, makes sounds understood in their particulars and explains correct speech. niruktaṃ, etymology, explains by rules such as the augment (āgama) of letters and the transposition of letters. jyotiṣaṃ, the lore of the stars, is the treatise for calculating the planets. cchanda, metre, is the treatise on verse.

oṃ is the praṇaba, meaning "highly praised." From aba, "to protect," because it belongs to the class of byoma and the like, the suffix manin; the final ac and so on is elided; by the rule "jvara tvara," ūṭ; and at is made eṅ; thus oṃ is formed. By its nature it takes no number.

itihāsa is ancient lore: an account of how things came about very long ago, the works such as the bharata composed by the sage Vyāsa and others. purabṛñtaṃ is "what happened before": an account of the past.

udātta and the other two belong to the tones. udātta is pronounced high, anudātta is pronounced low, and svarita is pronounced with a middle pitch.

prabandha is composition or connection, kalpanā is imagining, and kathā is narration or tale, and it has a marvelous subject illustrated by signs. A vast tale in the manner of the language of the piśācas, like the doings of the people, horses, elephants and so on of a great country, is here to be called cambū. prahelikā has a meaning hard to understand; it is also called prabahlikā, "what is shown by hints." For example: "There is one that does not close its eyes when it sleeps; it is because the force of the hair swells." That is: the fish, when asleep, does not close its eyes; it is the river's current swelling.

For the Veda, smṛti: the expression "concealed word" is found in the Mirror. It is called recollection because it comes from the recollection of the great sages.

For the names of worldly talk: kambidantī means saying whatever one has heard, kacebeganabardhate, worldly talk, the speaking of anything at all, whether true or untrue. janaśruti is what people have heard. Sign, and prabṛtti, are tidings and report. bṛttanta is "the end of what occurred," and hearsay. udanta is a message.

For the explanation of name: āhvaya is calling, or name; there is also what is called ākhya; aṅka is appellation; abhidhāna is denomination; and namadheya is name, dheya being a taddhita suffix added to "name." nāma is also "so-called," or name, or title.

For the names of dispute: bibāda is controversy or contention. byabahāra is convention. upanyasa is setting forth: ghaña is added to the root as, "to be," preceded by upa and ni. bādmukha is the door of speech. udahara is example, or statement. The synonyms of word or of name are easy to understand, so they have not been written down.

For the names of the seven tones: the sounds that arise from the strings and from the throat are the seven tones, niṣāda and the rest. Niṣāda is like the cry of an elephant. Ṛṣabha is like the lowing of cattle. Gāndhāra is like the bleat of a goat. Ṣaḍja is like the cry of a peacock. Madhyama is like the call of a crane. Dhaivata is like the neigh of a horse. Pañcama is like the call of a cuckoo. So too it occurs, as explained by the sage Nārada:

Ṣaḍja resounds like the peacock's cry;
ṛṣabha is made with the voice of cattle;
gāndhāra is the voice of the goat;
madhyama is like the crane sounding its call;
pañcama is like the cuckoo calling
in the season when the flowers bloom;
dhaivata rings out like the horse's neigh;
niṣāda is like the elephant trumpeting.

From an ancient treatise:

For mirth and for delight one should sing
the tones of pañcama and madhyama;
for the heroic, the fierce, and the marvelous,
ṣaḍja and ṛṣabha likewise.

The tones of gāndhāra and niṣāda
should be sung for the sentiment of compassion;
for battle together with fear,
the tone of dhaivata should be sung.

So it occurs. Their meaning is this. In keeping with the way the Tathāgata formerly showed himself sporting and delighting with his host of queens, and in a mood of pride, one sings the songs of pañcama and madhyama. Further, in keeping with his showing himself in a fierce form and displaying special marvels such as the subjugation of Māra, ṣaḍja and ṛṣabha are sung. Since he is endowed with the sentiment of compassion, in keeping with that one sings the songs of gāndhāra and niṣāda. He is shown as though arraying the wheel of battle, and in keeping with that one sings the song called dhaivata. These and many other tones of song are to be studied in the treatises of the sage bharata and of biśakhila. Furthermore:

The seven tones, the three scales,
the twenty-one modulations,
and the forty-nine melodic figures:
that much is the maṇḍala of tones.

That is, at the end of a song there are words such as jayati and tana. So, in the commentary on the introduction to the Laṅkāvatāra Sūtra, where the lord of the rākṣasas, Rāvaṇa, petitioned the Tathāgata with tones such as ṣaḍja accompanied by the sound of the vīṇā, this very exposition of the master Jñānavajra has been written here as an illustration.

Of these, niṣāda is niṣāda, because this tone surely brings serenity. ṛṣabha is ṛṣabha, because it sets things going, the mind being fully engaged: from ṛṣi, "to go," with the suffix abhaca. gandhara is gāndhāra, because within the tone it holds a share of fragrance; lengthening by the rule "in others too." gandharā is the song of the people of that country. ṣaṭja is ṣaḍja, because it is born relying on these six: the nose, the throat, the chest, the palate, the tongue, and the teeth. madhyama is madhyama, because it arises distinctively from the middle of the places of articulation. dhaibata is dhaivata, because it is to be sung by the intelligent: from dhimata, aṇ by the rule "from the remainder," and wa by the rule "in a name." pañcama is pañcama, because it is the completion of the five. kala and dhvani are indistinct melodious sound. kakali is a subtle sound.

For the names of music: tāpa is clapping, or cymbals. biṇa is the vīṇā, and ballakī is a three-stringed vīṇā. A vīṇā with many strings is the lute of the kiṃnaras and others. Those whose face is covered with skin are drums: the paṭaha, and the murja, the great clay drum, and so on. baṃśa is the flute and śaṃkha the conch; these are śuṣira, instruments with a hollow sound. kāṃsya is the gong. By the words "and so on" are meant the ghaṇḍa, the bell, the nūpura, the anklet, and other such sounds. mṛtaṃga is the clay drum or round drum. The ḍhakka, the bherī or great drum, the ānaka, the dundubhi or kettledrum, the ḍamaru or hand drum, the ḍiṇḍima, "throwing and casting," the maḍḍu or great hand drum, the jharjhara or one-faced clay drum, the mardala or song drum, the paṇaba or gong drum, and the sāmya or cymbals.

Of these, mṛttikāṅga, mṛdaṅgā, and muraja are synonyms. Because it is born from mur, it is murja. aṅgi, ālgyiṅ, and ūrdhaka are three kinds of muraja. Of these, aṅkī has the shape of the harītaki, the myrobalan fruit. ālaṅgyi is gopuccha, having the shape of a cow's tail. ārdhikā, urñiḥsbita, and urdhandama are synonyms: because the sounding of the upper face goes upward it is ūrdha; the sound arises from air driven upward. yogikāpālikādibādyaḍamaruḥ means: the instrument of those who sustain yoginīs and others is called the ḍamaru; by etymologies such as ḍamarauti and śaṃku it is the ḍamaru, that is, the hand drum. paebabibulamaḍḍu means that the same instrument, when large, is called maḍḍu. "maḍḍuka is the same as mardhala," says the master rabhasa. ḍiṇṇima means "throwing and casting"; it is also called ḍaṇḍima and ḍeṅguri. rjharaṇi, which transforms particular sounds, is the one-faced clay drum; it is also called karaḍaka. Further, the triṇa, the capeṭa, the gomukha, the huḍukkā, and so on are kinds of musical instrument.

Next, the names of the sentiments of dance. The eight sentiments of dance, beginning with the erotic, are the "tastes" of dance, because they are to be savored. For example, when a person eats and savors food well prepared with various condiments, he becomes especially delighted. In the same way, joy arises in the mind from seeing and experiencing what is made vivid by various means.

Here, what is called joy is a state whose nature is intense delight. The supreme nature of a particular transformation of one's own mind that comes from its accumulation is the sentiment called śrigāra, the erotic. So too it is said:

The joy that arises in each
from the meeting of man and woman
is explained as the erotic,
the cause of joyful play and the like.

It is, moreover, of two kinds:

It has many supreme natures,
and man and woman are its cause;
enjoyment and spectacle:
the erotic is held to be of two kinds.

Of these, the wealth of enjoyment is the nature of sporting and delighting in pools, fine mansions, pleasure gardens, garlands, sandalwood, women, and other such things. Spectacle is the wealth that delights when it is seen; it is wealth because it gives rise to sentiment. As it is said:

That from which sentiment arises
is declared to be wealth;
from it happiness is experienced;
the wise bring it fully about.

The nature of energy is bīra, the heroic: it is prosperity, discipline, the power of austerity, and conquest. It too is of three kinds: heroism in giving, heroism in the Dharma, and heroism in battle.

The nature of gathered grief is karuṇa, compassion. What is produced by the loss of what one desires and of wealth, by evil, killing, bondage, beating, reviling, torment by the afflictions, and the like is the sentiment of compassion.

The nature of gathered wonder is adbhuta, the marvelous or the awesome: going to mansions, pleasure gardens, rocky mountains and the like and seeing fine things there, and seeing assemblies, celestial palaces, illusions, magic shows, works of craft and the like. It arises from obtaining the wealth the heart desires.

The nature of gathered laughter is hāsya, the comic: seeing someone hiding the letter a and the like, with contortions, with clothes slipping, or unfastened, and so on.

The nature of gathered fear is bhāyānaka, the terrifying: seeing Mahābhairava, the rākṣasa Rāvaṇa, pretas and the like; empty houses and halls, the wilderness, the sight of killing and binding; and, when frightened, exhausted, or weary, hearing the sounds of jackals, owls and the like.

The nature of disgust is bībhatsa, the repulsive: seeing, hearing, and hearing tell of the repulsive, rotting flesh and the like.

The nature of gathered anger is raudra, the furious. It arises from the nature of performing violent acts of body and speech, and it is the cause of entering the front of battle.

These are the eight sentiments. In the Ratnakośa it is said:

The erotic, the heroic, the repulsive,
the furious, the comic, the terrifying,
the compassionate, the marvelous, and the peaceful:
these are the nine sentiments of dance.

If one asks why the ninth, the peaceful sentiment, is not explained here, it should be said that the peaceful sentiment follows upon the erotic sentiment. For the erotic of one who desires the Dharma is of three kinds, and the first of these is so by being the cause of benefit to oneself, through austerities, vows, and the like.

The master rabhasa says:

The erotic, the heroic, the repulsive,
the comic, the furious, the terrifying,
the compassionate, the marvelous, the peaceful,
and the tender: these are the ten sentiments.

Others say that the eight sentiments are gathered into four, and so there are four sentiments.

Stating the synonyms of the erotic and the rest: śuci is the pure, that is, the unalloyed. ujjvala is the blazing, that is, the charming. These express the nature of erotic activity. As the master byāḍi says:

The pure and the blazing are the erotic;
it increases joy and delight.

Delight in giving, in the Dharma, and in the fighting of battle is the heroic sentiment. kāruṇya and karuṇa are compassion; the former has the suffix ghyaṇ. ghṛṇā is tenderness: ghṛ, "to wash," and the suffix nak by the rule "rasna and the like." kṛpā, dayā, and anukamba are also tenderness. unukrośa is sympathy.

Next, hasa, hāsa, and hāsya are the three names of laughter, called laughing, laughter, and mirth. bibhatsa and bikṛta are the contorted and the repulsive. bismaya is wonder. adbhūta is the marvelous. aścarya is the astonishing. citraṃ is the extraordinary. bhairabaṃ is the terrifying, dāruṇaṃ the unbearable, bhīṣaṇaṃ the frightening, dārurṇa the unbearable, bhīma and bhiṣma the fearsome, ghoraṃ the fierce or the dreadful, bhayanakaṃ the terrifying, and bhayaṅkara and pratibhayaṃ the fear-causing and the fearful in each case.

Next, for the names of wish: iccha, kaṃkṣāṃ, spṛhā, tṛṭa, bāñchā, and lissā are synonyms for desire or wish: hope, desire, and wish. kāma is desire, abhilāṣa is longing, tarṣa is craving, and mahānlālas is great desire.

For the names of sleep: nidā is sleep, śayana is lying down, svāpa and svapna are sleeping or dreaming, and sambasa is called "well settled."

For the names of festival: uddharṣa is close delight: after ut, the letter ha of harṣa is changed into dha. maha is an offering or a seasonal festival, and uddhaba is a spectacle: after ut, ha is changed into dha. utsapa is a festival.

Here ends the section on heaven, from the Kāmadhenu, the extensive commentary on the Amarakośa composed by Subhūticandra.

Next, from the section on the underworld. First, five names for the underworld. adhvabhubanas is "below." pātāla is "beneath the feet," or the underground: by the rule "ālañ after pati caṇḍi," the suffix ālaña is added to pātā. In balipadma, bali is "the powerful," the lord of the asuras, and padma is abode: the abode of the powerful, or the abode of the asuras. rasātāla is "beneath the essence." nāgaloka is the world of the nāgas.

Next, ending with śuṣa, the names of holes as such. Of these, kuhara is a hole. bibar is a hollow. śuṣiraṃ is a window. bilam is a small hole. cchitraṃ is an opening. nirbyatha is a hole of fear. rokaṃ is a lattice window. randhraṃ is a hole or a hollow. śvabhraṃ is a skylight. bapa is a hole from which wind issues. śuṣi is a hole. These two express holes in the earth. garta is a pit.

Next, for the names of darkness: andbhakāra is darkness. dhvāntaṃ is blinding dark. tamisraṃ is lightlessness. timiraṃ is gloom. tamaḥ is pitch darkness. dhvānte is gathered darkness. ghaṭa is the very dense. andhatamasaṃ and abatamasaṃ are also darkness.

Next, for the names of nāgas: nāga is a nāga, and is called "mountain dweller." kādrabeya is called "son of Kadrū": because they were born of Kadrū, the wife of the sage Kāśyapa, the patronymic suffix eyaṇ is added to kadru. The lords of those nāgas are śeṣa, called "the one with a remainder," ananta, called "the endless," and bāsuki, called "the son of Vāsuki." They are also called sarbarāja, king of serpents.

Next, for the names of snakes as such: gonāsa is "cow nose," tiliccha is "lair dweller," ajagara śayu is "the sleeper," bāhasa is "wind eater," alagardha is "swift goer," jalabyala is the water snake, rājila is "the one with rows," having garland-like lines on its back, maludhāna is "the one with a seat," muktakañcuka is "the one that sheds its skin," sarpsa is "crawling on the ground," pṛdāku is "crooked on the ground," bhujag is "arm goer," bhujaṅga and bhujaṃgama are the same, ahi is "swift goer," aśībiṣa is "the one with poison in its fangs," cakri is "the one with coils," byāḍa is "crooked goer," sarīsripa is "crawling crookedly," and kuṇḍali is "the coiled," for its body stays coiled like an earring. gūḍḍhapāda is "hidden foot," because its feet are hidden and cannot be seen.

From a scripture:

Its feet too are to be known:
two hundred and twenty-two.

Thus the number of its very feet is given. cakṣuḥśrabā is called "one that hears with its eyes." kākodar is mahoraga. phaṇi is "the hooded one." darbīkara is "ladle head." As it is said:

The great hooded one, the ladle head,
brilliant, fully equal to fire,
phaṇakara, swift and fierce,
black snake, bhūta, lord of men.

dīrgapṛṣṭha is "long back." dandaśūka is "bad food": it has the habit of eating bad food. biliśaya sleeps in holes. uraga is "chest goer" or "belly goer," and mahoraga. pannaga is "one that goes without feet," bhogī is "the hooded," jihmaga is "crooked goer," and pabanāśan is "wind eater." These are the names of snake.

Next, for the names of poison: kākola and the rest are the nine divisions of great poison. Of these, kākola is called dronakā: it is a poison resembling the limbs of a crow. kālakūṭa is called "Black Heap." It arose when the gods and the asuras fought, and it grows in the mountains of malaya, koṅkaṇ, and śṛṅgabe. The poison called halāhala has blue leaves shaped like the leaves of the tāla palm and fruit shaped like a cow's teat, and it burns the trees and other things near it. It grows in the land of himabati, in koṅkaṇ, and in the southern oceans. sāroṣṭika is a lasting poison. śaulkakeya is also a particular poison. bahmaputra is called "Son of Brahmā"; it grows in the land of Kapila and in the mountains of malaya. pradīpana is called "Very Bright": it is red in color and burning, and it grows on Mount añjana. dārada is called "born in darada," for it grows in the land of darada; that land is also well known as Fine Light. barsanābha is called "calf's navel": resembling the leaves of the sindhūbā, it has the shape of a calf's navel, and it keeps the trees near it from growing.

For the names of those who destroy poison: biṣabaitya is a physician for poison. jaṅgali is one who removes poison. byālagrāhi is a snake catcher. ahiruṇḍika is one who contends with snakes.

Next, with half a verse, the general names of hell. nakara and niraya are hell, or "devoid of pleasure." durgati is a bad destiny. The divisions of hell: tapana is the Hot. abici is Unceasing Torment. raureba and mahāraurava are Howling and Great Howling. kālapūtra is the Black Line.

For the names of suffering: yātanā is what is hard to bear. tībrabedana is having unbearable feeling, or intense feeling. pīṭa is torment or a sharp pain. bādha is a stabbing pain. byāthā is the suffering of illness. duḥkhaṃ is suffering. āmānasyaṃ is "companion of the mind." prapūtijaṃ is the suffering born of childbirth. kaṣṭaṃ and kṛcchra are the difficult or the miserable. abhṛlam is called "taking on fear."

Next, with one and a half verses, the names of ocean. Of these, samudra is ocean: by the rule "from undī preceded by sam, dura and the like," the suffix rak is applied. adhiḥ is water treasure: the suffix kī is applied to dhā preceded by apa. akūpāraḥ is called "increased by the moon." pārābāraḥ is called "whose limit is not seen," the meaning being dṛṣyapāraatyarthaḥph, "whose far shore is not seen." sāratpati is the lord of rivers. udanbān is "possessed of water": matus is made the suffix. udadhi is water treasure. sindhu is "possessed of current." sārasvān is "possessed of water." sāgara is "possessed of poison." arṇaba is ocean. ratnākara is the source of jewels. jalanidhi is water treasure. yādaḥpati is the lord of sea monsters. āpampati is the lord of waters.

Next, two verses and a line give the names of water. Of these, āpaḥ is the waters. bāri is water. śalilam is water by being flowing: śala, "to go," with the suffix kiraca, and by the rule "kapiri and the like," ra is changed to la. kāmalam is water because it is desired: by "kāmyate, it is desired," and by the rule "kala after śami kami," the suffix kala is added to kami. jalam is water because it benefits grain: since water produces grain, the suffix aca is added to jala. payaḥ is water because it is to be drunk: asun is made the suffix of pīṅ, "to drink." kīlāla is water because it binds: kālan is made the suffix of kīla, "to bind." amṛta is water because it makes deathless: mṛṅ, "to give up life," with the suffix kta, and naña placed before it. jībana is water because it sustains life: lhu is made the suffix of jiba. bhubanaṃ is "the digester," and banaṃ is "that which makes abide." kabandha is water because it binds the body; kaṃ is the body. udakaṃ is water because it moistens: kan is made the suffix of undi, "to wet." The word daka also exists for water. pāthaḥ is water because it falls downward: pāta, "to drink," with asun and thuṭ as well. puṣkara is water because it nourishes: karan is made the suffix of puṣa, "to nourish." sarbatomuṣaṃ is water because it goes through every opening. ambu is water because it flows downward: from ami, the suffix asun; bhuk is an augment (āgama). arṇa is water because it goes to the end: following the rule "ut also of anti," sun, and nuṭ as well. toya, because it protects. nīra is water because it makes things flow downward; both of these are by pṛṣoda and the like. pāniya is water because it is to be drunk: āniya is made the suffix of pā, "to drink." kṣira is water because it exhausts: kṣaṇu, "to harm," is given the suffix ḍīraca. ambu is water because it binds: abi, "to bind," is given the suffix ku by the rule "śaṅgu and the like." śambara is water because it brings supreme bliss. meghapuṣpa, the flower of the clouds, and ghanrasa, the essence of the clouds: these are synonyms among the names of water.

Next, the names of waves, beginning with bhaṃga: bhadha is waves, taraṅga is ripples, ūrmi is waves of water, and bīci is the successions of water: by bismayacinoti and by thrakhodara and the like, it is changed into bici. Then lo is a great billow. Such terms and the like are to be known as names connected with water; they are easy to understand.

Next, for the names of fish: pṛthuroma is called "long haired," for it has long hairs about the mouth. matsya is fish: this is especially the round one without hair. jhaṣa is hāprāṇajhaṣa: the fish of the ocean of great vitality, adorned with jharā clinging to its skin. From madi, syan. mīna is "possessed of motion": mā, "to measure," with the suffix naña.

By the rule "māsthā" and the like, it becomes i. baisāriṇa is "darting about," because it darts about in various ways. aṇāja is "egg born," because it is born from an egg. bisāra too is "darting about." śakalī is "the armored," because many, such as the rohita, have coarse armor. gaḍuka is "the washer": the suffix kvun is added to gaḍu, "to wash." śakulārbhaka is "the son of the śakula," because it resembles the young of the śakula. sahasradaṃṣṭa is "possessed of a thousand teeth." pāṭhina is "fish eater"; it is also called the fish timi and "fish swallower," and some also call it pāṭha. ulūpi is "leaping upward." śiśuka is "child killer": a kind of fish similar in shape to the sea monster called child killer. nalamīna is the strong fish; some also say talamīna. prauṣṭhina is the warm fish: uṣa, "to burn," made with the suffix than; from the particle, "it burns intensely," it is prauṣṭhī. śahari is "side sleeper," because it sleeps on its side.

Next, for the names of the turtle: kūrma is the turtle, because it possesses the force of its lower body. kamatha is the turtle, because its going is slow. kacchaba is "drinking with the lungs," the turtle.

Next, with half a verse, the names of the leech. raktapāni is "blood drinker." jalauka is also "blood drinker": uca, "to gather," with ghañ in the sense of the agent. So too it is explained in the sūtras of kātyaya in the Kātantra, the brief tantra of grammar: "ghañ after pad, ruja, biśa, spṛśa, and uca." jalaukāsa is the same.

Next, for the names of the oyster: muktaspoṭa is the source of pearls, and śukti is the oyster. For the names of the conch: śaṃkha and kumyu.

Next, for the names of the frog: bheka is "leaping goer," maṇḍuka is the frog, barṣābhu is "born in summer" or "born of rain," and there are śalūra, blaba, "the swimmer," and dandura.

Next, for the names of the pond: puṣkariṇi is "possessed of water" or "possessed of lotuses": puṣkara is a name of both water and the lotus, and the suffix ina is applied in the sense of possession. khātaṃ is ditch water: khana, "to dig," with the suffix kta, and āt by the rule "of jana sana khana." ākhātaṃ is what is dug all around, a pond. debakhātakaṃ is a pond made by the gods: it was made by laborers of the gods and asuras who live on wages. padmākaraṃ is a pond, because it has become a source of lotuses. taḍāga is "having much water." kāsara is "possessed of sound." sarasīra is called toyasāra: having the essence of water.

Next, for the names of flowing water: nadī is a river. sarita is flowing water. tariṅgaṇī is "possessed of waves." saibalinī is "possessed of waterweed." taḍinī is "possessed of banks" or "possessed of shores." hradinī is "possessed of sound." dhunī is "possessed of motion." srotāsvatī is "possessed of current." dvīpabatī is "possessed of islands." prabantī is "possessed of flow." nimbagā is "going downward." And āpagā is called "flowing to the sea."

Next, with one verse, the names of the river gaṅgā. gamagā is called "she who possesses going": the suffix gan is applied to gami. biṣṇupadī is "possessed of Viṣṇu's foot," because it became the water offered to Viṣṇu's feet. jahnutanayā means "daughter of jahnu": because the king of sages jahnu drank it and vomited it out, it became like his daughter. suranimbagā is the river of the gods. bhagīrathī is called "daughter of Bhagīratha": because King Bhagīratha brought it down to this world, it became his descendant, and so the patronymic suffix an is applied to bhagīratha. tripathaga is called "goer on the three paths," because it goes on the three paths of heaven, the human realm, and the underworld. trisrotā, called "flowing in three streams," is as before. bhīṣmasū is called "daughter of the Terrifier." These are the names of the river gaṅgā.

Next, with half a verse, the names of the river kalindī. Of these, kalindī: because it arises from Mount kalindā, it is that mountain's daughter, and is called "daughter of kalandā." sūryatanayā is the daughter of the sun. It is also called yamuna. śamanasvasā is "sister of the Pacifier," that is, the sister of Yama.

Next, with half a verse, the names of the river called rebā: the river called reba, and narmada, called jalakelipradā, the river that grants water play; somodbhaba, "arisen from the moon"; and mekalakanyā: because it arises from Mount mekala, it is that mountain's daughter, and is called "daughter of the mountain."

Next, the names of the river śita. karatoya is "hand water," because it arose from the water used when the god Śaṅkara took the goddess Gaurī as his bride. sadānīrī is "ever flowing," because from it red dust like menstrual blood flows at all times; it is also called "blood flowing." bahudā is "giver of much." saitabāhi is "flowing white": it is called "flowing white" because arju is the one who accomplishes existence, or else because it flows cool. sitadru too is "flowing cool." śatadru is "divided into a hundred": when, out of grief for his son, he bound a stone to his belly and entered it, the sage Vasiṣṭha, Establisher of Abodes, broke it into a hundred parts, so that it divided into a hundred; it is said to remain split into a hundred across the lands of jālandhara and others. bipāśā is called "freed from the noose," because, having bound himself with a noose out of grief for his son, the sage Vasiṣṭha entered it and was set free. bipāśa itself, made bipāḥ at the end of a compound, is also "freed from the noose."

Next, the names of the utpala flower. Because it is a common word, "blue water born" also applies to this utpala. indriwara, "supreme lord," utpala, and bhūruhati, "earth born," are explained by rabhasa. "Delight maker, kubalayaya, supreme lord, and blue water born" are explained by Vararuci. utpala: to pā, "to drink," preceded by ut, by "utpipati, it drinks upward," the suffix kala is made, by pṛṣodar and the like; it is called "drinking upward." kubalaya is "the bracelet of the earth," and from kubala, "strength"; and in the Trikāṇḍaśeṣa kubi is also explained.

For the names of the kumuda flower: sita is white, kumuda is "delight of the earth" or "pleasure of the earth," and kaurava is "proclaimer of the earth."

Next, two verses on the names of the lotus. padma: from padi, the suffix man is made, as with dharma. nalinaṃ is "possessed of a stalk": the suffix ina is applied to nala. arabināṃ is "piercing spokes."

mahotpala is the great utpala. sahasrapattra is "thousand petaled." kamala is "ornament of the water": kaṃ is water and ala is ornament. śatapatraṃ is "hundred petaled." kuśeśa too is "lying in water." paṅkaṅuhi is "born of mud." tāmraṃsa is "dwelling in water": as in tāmaraṃ ghṛtamarṇaśca, tāmaraṃ means both butter and water, and sa has the sense of tiṣṭhati, "abides." sārasaṃ is "water born." sarasīruhaṃ is "lake born." biṣaprasurgo is "born of the lotus root." rājība is "possessed of rows": possessed of rows of anthers. amboruha is "arisen from water." puṇḍarīkaṃ is the white lotus. sītatamboja is the white water born. The red water born has three names: raktasāroruha, which is that same flower; raktotpala, the red utpala; and kokanadaṃ, the red lotus, which is like a red kumuda.

Next, nala and nāla are the stalk, stem, or shaft, from the root ṇala, "to bind." mṛṇala is the root of the lotus and the like; and biśaṅ too, in the sense of lotus root, is explained by the glorious king Sun Vanquisher in his Cluster of Practice. kadamba is a mass of the roots of water born plants and the like. ṣaṇḍa too is a grove, of the roots of water born plants and the like. This the master Rakṣita explains in the Dhātupradīpa: "ṣaḍi, in the sense of illness; aca, in the sense of collection"; and he gives the example "ṣaṇḍabanaṃ, padmaṣaṇḍa," that is, ṣaṇḍa is a grove, as in a grove of lotuses.

Next, karhaṭa and siphākanda are the husk of the lotus. kiñjelka and kesara are the filaments of the lotus. saṃbadikā is a curled leaf, and nabadalaṃ is a new leaf. bījakoṣa is the treasury of seeds, and bārākaṭa is the seed cluster; both are the center of the lotus. Thus:

Heaven, sky, directions, time, intellect,
sound and the rest, together with dance,
the underworld, the hooded ones, hell, and water:
these have been explained together with their groups.

Here, by "and the rest" in "sound and the rest" are meant form, sentiment, and so on. "Together with dance" means possessing song, dance, and music; this was explained by the commentary in the section on tones. It is as follows. In the section on heaven, the gods and so on were explained; in the section on the sky, clouds and so on; in the section on the directions, the eight elephants of the directions and so on; in the section on time, Rāhu's seizing and so on; in the section on intellect, discernment and so on; in the section on sound, udātta and so on; in the section on form, sentiment and the rest, fame, blame, and so on; in the section on dance, bhrukuṃpa and so on; in the section on the underworld, holes and so on; in the section on the hooded ones, the shedding of skin and so on; in the section on hell, misfortune and so on; and in the section on water, boats and so on. In "the sections on heaven and so on," "and so on" means down to the underworld. A section is an arrangement, something whose nature is to be delimited or made into a group.

Here ends the first book of the Kāmadhenu, the extensive commentary on the Amarakośa composed by the master Subhūticandra.

Next, the section on the earth is to be explained:

The section on the earth, cities,
mountains and forests,
medicinal plants, wild beasts and the rest,
men, brahmins, kṣatriyas,
vaiśyas, and śūdras, with their limbs
and minor limbs, are told here.

This sums up the point that here the section on the earth, with its limbs and minor limbs, is explained in ten kinds, by the ten beginning with the earth. "Wild beasts and the rest" means lions and the like.

With two verses, the names of the earth. bhuḥ is the earth, and bhūmi is "the producer": from bhū, "to be," the former with kvipa, and the latter with the suffix mi by the rule "urmi bhūmi and the like." acala is the unmoving, that is, the earth. ananta is called "the limitless," because it has no end. rasā is "possessed of essence." biśvambharā is "the all sustaining." sthirā is the stable: sthā, "to stand," with the suffix kiraca. dhara is the holder. dharatrī is the support. dharaṇiḥ is the bearer. kṣoṇī is the resounding one: kṣu, "to sound," with the suffix inin. jya is the victorious. kāśyapī is called "given to Kāśyapa," for it is told that King Paraśurāma gave it to the sage Kāśyapa as the fee at the end of the horse sacrifice. kṣite is the abode: kṣi, "to dwell firmly," with kti. sarbaṃsaha is the all enduring: ṣaha, "to endure," with the suffix khaśa. basumati is "she who possesses wealth": because it has become the source of gold, silver, and jewels such as hīra, the diamond, matub is made the suffix in the sense of possession. basudhā is the holder of wealth. urbī is the wide. basundharā is the holder of treasure. gautrā is "protector of water": gau is water and trā is protecting. kupa: kai, "to sound," with the suffix ku by the rule "śaṃku and the like." pṛthibī and pṛthvī are the ground and the producer. kṣmā is the patient. abani is the protector. medinī is "she who possesses marrow," because the marrow and fat of the slain asuras Madhu and Kaiṭabha fell upon it and it possesses them. mahī is the venerated, because worship came to be offered to it.

Next, five names for abode as such: jagatī is "possessed of beings." lokaḥ is the world. piṣṭasaṃ is the stable abode. bhubanaṃ is the abode of the earth. jagata is the living.

On "this is the land of bhārata": here are to be explained the lands into which the portion of the earth that is Jambudvīpa is divided by great mountains. As explained in the Trikāṇḍaśeṣa:

bhārata and narabhū,
Kiṃpuruṣa and the Land of Lions,
Bhadra, Uttarakuru,
Hiraṇmaya, ilabṛtaṃ,
and Ketumāla: these
are the nine great lands that rest upon the earth.

Elsewhere, for narabhū and the abode of the kiṃnaras, ramaṇa and Supreme Lion are given. Here, in this human world of Jambudvīpa, the portion divided into nine by the division into nine continents, Indra's continent, the continent of kameru, the Kumāra continent and the rest, measures nine thousand yojanas.

It is the land itself, south of the Himalaya, at the edge of the ocean. A king called Ṛṣabha renounced the world and went into the forest, and he gave this land to the eldest of his hundred sons, who was called bhārata. Because bhārata held it, it is the land of bhārata. So too it is said:

Because the father who went forth into the forest
gave it to bhārata,
from then on this has been known in the world
as the land of bhārata.

Rudra reads it as Jambudvīpa itself, and in the work composed by the sage Vyāsa, Jambudvīpa too is called bhārata.

North of the Himalaya is the land of the kiṃnaras, a thousand yojanas in extent. There the beings eat the fruit of the plakṣa tree and live for ten thousand years. North of the land of the kiṃnaras is the mountain called hemakūṭa, Heap of Gold. North of that is the land called Supreme Lion, eighteen thousand yojanas in extent. There the people of the world eat the juice of the sugarcane and live for eleven thousand years. North of that is the mountain called niṣadha, as in taruṇādityasannibho niṣadha, "niṣadha, like the young sun." In the northeastern part beyond it is the land called Bhadra, where the people eat the fruit of the kadampa tree. In the west is the land called kerumāla, Garland of Flowers, where the beings eat the fruit of the panasa tree. Between them is the land called ilabṛtaṃ, encircled by mountains. In its center is Mount Meru, where all the gods dwell, and the beings eat the fruit of the jambu tree. These three lands together measure thirty thousand yojanas.

North of ilabṛta is the mountain called Blue, made of baiḍūrya, equal in size to niṣadha. North of that is the land called ramṇa, equal in size to the Land of Lions, where the people eat the fruit of the nyagrodha tree and live for thirteen thousand years. North of that is the mountain called White, made of silver. North of that is the land called ñiranmaya, equal in size to the land of the kiṃnaras, where the people eat the fruit of the lakuca tree and live for fourteen thousand years. North of that is the mountain called saṃgama, made of every kind of jewel. North of that is the land of Uttarakuru, equal in size to the land of bhārata, where whatever the beings wish for comes forth from wish-fulfilling trees, and they live for fifteen thousand years. Here, from the northeast of the land of bhārata, the river called śārabati flows toward the southwest and empties into the western ocean.

Next, for the names of the land of the noble ones: madhyadeśa is the middle country. madhyama is the middle. āryābarta is "where the noble are born": ābarta means "born." It is also called puṇyabhūmi, the ground of merit.

Next, with one verse, the names of the path. aanaṃ is a path or a journey. barttma is a way to be gone, or a path. mārga is the path. adhvā is a track. banthāna, pathabī, and sridi are also a path, or a way of entry. sarṇi, pandhati, patya, and barttmani are also the path. ekapadī is "having one foot," a narrow path.

Next, half a verse for a good path. atipanthā is the highway, subantha the good path, satpatha the true path, the supreme path, or the excellent path, and arcite'dhvani the path to be honored.

Next, byadhva, duradhva, and the like are names of a bad path.

Next, gabhuti is called the oxcart, or the measure of the cattle track. Here, twenty-four finger-breadths are called kuṣku and hasta, that is, a cubit. Four cubits make a bow length; there is also what is called nalva. Eight thousand cubits make a krośa, called an earshot; and two thousand bow lengths are called gabluti, which is the same as an earshot. A breadth of ten bow lengths is the ghaṇḍāpatha and the rājamārga, that is, the king's road, the toll road, or the road where bells are rung.

The section on the earth.

Next, the section on cities. Here there are seven names for city. pū and purī are a town, because they guard and protect against enemy attackers and are hard to approach. That which bears the designation of eight hundred villages is nagara, a city. Where it is close to the king's palace, it is battanaṃ, the royal road or the palace grounds. buṭabhednaṃ is a market house or market day, where merchants come and go. sthānīyaṃ is a fearless place, a place where there is no fear of anything at all. migma is an unshakable abode.

Next, for the names of house: gṛha is a home, a dwelling, a nest, or a shelter. geha is a home or a house. udabasitaṃ is an abode. baśme is a home, or a place to enter. padma is an abode or a house. niketanaṃ is a dwelling place. Further, bhabanā, gāra, mandiraṃ, nilaya, amaya, and the like are also dwellings, abodes, and so on. Next, parṇaśala, a hut of leaves, and the like are names of particular kinds of dwelling.

Next, bātāyanaṃ is a wind hole, that is, a window, a skylight, or a lattice. gabākṣa is a cow's eye. These are names of window.

Next, for the names of mansion: harmi is a cool house, a house with a balcony, or one with a curved roof. prasāda is a mansion. saudha is a pure abode. rājasadana is a king's house. svastika is "possessed of well-being," or auspicious. sarbatobhadra is the all good.

Next, three lines, beginning with styagaraṃ, give the names of the queens' quarters: strīagāra means "house of the queens."

The section on cities.

Next, from the section on mountains, first the names of mountain. mahīdhra is "holder": the suffix ka is applied to ḍhṛña, "to hold," preceded by mahī, as with mūlabibhuja and the like. śikhari is "possessed of peaks" or "possessed of summits": the possessive suffix ini is added to the word śikhara. kṣmābhṛta is the bearer of the earth, or the holder of the earth: the suffix kvip is applied to bhṛña, "to hold," preceded by kṣma, in the sense "it holds firmly." ahārya is the unseizable: ṇyata after hṛña, "to seize," preceded by nañ. dhara is the holder, because it holds the earth. sarbata is "the jointed": sarba, "to complete," with the suffix ataca. atri is the mountain. gotra is the protector of the earth: here, in go bhubaṃ, go is the earth. gori is called "rising upward": gṝ, "to make clear," with the suffix i applied by the rule "iki śipta in its own form."

gābā too is a mountain: the suffix kanin is applied to the root gṛ. acala is the unmoving, that is, the mountain. śila is a rocky mountain. śilocchaya is a heap of stones.

Next, among particular mountains, the names of the encircling range. lokāloka is "what appears in the world": it is explained so because it surrounds the whole disk of the earth like a ring of light, and the inner faces of this, which has become a wall, are fully lit by the rays of the sun. cakrabāḍa is the encircling range. trikuṭa is "three heaped," because it stands as three mountains heaped together. trikakuṭ is "three horned," because it has three summits.

Next, asta and carama, "western" and "last," are names of the western mountain. uraya and pūrbaśaraṃ are "the front," that is, the front mountain or the eastern mountain. himabān, the Himalaya and the rest, are also particular mountains; by "and the rest" are meant Mount malaya, Mount dardara, and so on. Of these, the Himalaya, Hemakūṭa, niṣadha, that is, the Obstructor, Mount meru, Mount Blue, Mount White, and finally Mount saṃgama are the mountains that bound the seven lands. Mahendra, malaya, sahya, Vindhya, pariyātra, tripra, and tīkṣā are the seven kinds of mountain of the land of bhārata. The garlanded mountain is the Crane Mountain, as the Trikāṇḍaśeṣa explains: "the Crane too is garlanded." West of Mount Meru is Mount mālyabante, the Garlanded, equal in size to the mountain of rising fragrance. East of Mount Meru is the mountain gandhamādana, which extends thirty-two thousand yojanas lengthwise, is a thousand yojanas in breadth, and reaches down below; it has a rising fragrance. The other mountains too should be known in this way from scripture.

Next, with half a verse, the names of stone. pāṣāṇa is stone. prastara is "the scatterer." graba is a boulder; it was also explained earlier under mountain. upala is a squat stone: upa is "near," and la is "holding." aśmana is a pebble. śila is a slab of stone. dṛśad is stone.

Next, with one line, the names of peak. kuṭa is a heap. śikhara is "crest born," or the tip of a horn: śikha is crest and ruha is growing; by "śikhī rohati," the suffix da is applied to ruha. śṛṅga is the tip of a horn.

Next, with one line, the names of cliff. prapāt is a steep fall, a precipice. taṭa is the mountain's edge, the edge or face of a cliff. bhṛgu is a cliff: the suffix ku is added to bhastha, sa is elided, and yan is changed into aki.

Next, kaḍaka is a mountain slope, and nitamba is the foot of a mountain. nikuñja is a forest, kuñja is a grove, and latāpihitā, called "covered with creepers," is a grove.

The section on mountains.

Next, for the section on forests: āṭabi and ariṇyaṃ are the wilderness. bīpinaṃ is the empty wilderness. gahanaṃ is the dense forest. kānanaṃ is a grove. banaṃ is a forest or a grove. gṛhārāma is a household park, a park near a house. niṣkuṭaḥ is "sure going." upababanaṃ is a nearby grove. kṛtṛmaṃbanaṃ is an artificial grove. kṛḍaḥ is a pleasure ground. utyana is a garden.

Next, for the names of what is set in rows: bithya is a row, āli and abali are also a row and a line, paṃkti is a row stretched out in a line, and śreṇī is an ordered row. lekha is an arrangement or a drawing. rāji is rows in order, or rows.

Next, adkura is a sprout or a shoot. abhinaba is the quite new, that is, the newly born. udbhidi is "breaking through upward": a name for the sprout turned upward from the seed.

Next, one verse on the names of tree as such. Here, bṛkṣa is tree: bṛkṣa, "to cover," with aca. mahīruha is called "earth born": by mahyāṃ rohati, "it grows from the earth," the suffix kā is applied to ruha. śākhi is "possessed of branches." biṭapī is "possessed of leaves"; pālaśa and biṭapa are synonyms. pādapa is "foot drinker": because it drinks the nourishing juices through its feet, the suffix ka is applied to pa, "to drink." taru is tree: as with meru from tri, the suffix u is applied. anokaha, called "obstructing movement," is a tree. kuṭha is a tree. śāla is "possessed of leaves," or the great. palāśi is "possessed of leaves on its trunk." druma is a tree. agama, called "not going," is a tree.

Next, trees marked by flowers and fruit, the kharjura or date palm, the amra, the alakā, and so on, are called banaspatya, "the fruit-bearing forest": the patronymic suffix ṇya is applied to banaspati. Trees marked by not producing flowers and fruit, the panasa, the udumbāra, the plakṣa, and so on, are called banaspati, "the fruitless forest," because there is no patronymic suffix. Plants that perish at the end of the ripening of their fruit, the kadalī or plantain and the like, are called oṣadhi, "a grove of medicinal plants." Therefore trees that produce fruit when the season arrives are called andhya, not barren and not fruitless, and phalegra, "fruit bearing," while those without fruit are called barren and fruitless. phalabān is "possessed of grain," phalina is "possessed of fruit," and phalī is "possessed of the tree's produce."

Next, with three lines, the names of blossoming. praphulla is fully blossomed, utrulla is greatly blossomed, saṃphulla is perfectly or wholly blossomed, and byākoṣaḥ is "freed from the sheath," that is, opening and blossoming out of the sheath. sphaṭaḥ is spreading, blossoming, or bright. phulla is opened, and bikasita is fully opened.
