Next, with three lines, the names of creeper: ballīḥ is a creeper, bratatiḥ a vine, lata a creeper or a willow, pratīni a leaf, nībiruṭa a branch, gulma a shoot, and ulapa a thicket.

Next, śiphājaṭ is the fine roots: the matted, rope-like roots of a tree. śākhāśipha is the root of a branch. abaroha is a hanging branch.

Next, for the names of the upper part of a tree: śira is the head, agra is the tip, and śikharaṃ is the crown or top. For the names of the root of a tree: mūla is the root or base: mūla, "to stand firmly," with the suffix aca. buddhna, aṃhri, and aṃghri are also synonyms among the names of foot. buddhna: budha, "to understand," with the suffix nak, as in uṣṇa.

For the part of choice wood suitable for making images and the like, there are two names: śāra, the heartwood, and majjaḥ, the pith. For the names of the bark of a tree: tvaka is the skin, balkaṃ the husk, and balkaṃlaṃ the peel. Next, kāṣṭhaṃ and daru, the two, are firewood and timber. Next, andhinaṃ is fuel, idhmaṃyaṃ is kindling wood, edhaḥ is the wood of the homa, and samid is wood for burning: these five are names of the wood used for the fire offering.

For the names of the hollow of a tree and the like: niṣkuha is a hollow, and kohāraṃ is "possessed of an inside." Next, ballari, a cluster, and mañjari, a spike, are names for clusters of flowers, fruits, and so on.

Next, with half a verse, the names of leaf: pattra is "that which falls," a leaf; palāśaṃ is foliage; cchadana is "the coverer"; dalaṃ is a leaf; parṇa is a tree leaf; and chada is leafage.

Next, with one line, the names for a gathering of sprigs and the like: pallaba is a shoot or a sprig, and kiśilya is a mass of sprigs. With one line, the names for what is covered by a mass of branches or shoots: bistara is said of a spreading tree, and piṭapa is a spreading crown.

Next, four names for flower. sumanas is "mind delighting," because it greatly delights the mind. puṣpa is "the expander," the flower. prasūnaṃ is "fully born." kusuma is the flower. Next, makaranta is the nectar of flowers, puṣpasara the essence of flowers, sarāga the pollen of flowers, and sumanaraja the pollen of the fair flower: these are the names of the essence of flowers.

Next, the names of particular trees. The tree of awakening has five names. bodhidruma is the tree of awakening, because at its root the Buddha, the Blessed One, manifested awakening. caladala is called "moving leaves," because its leaves are always moving. bispala too is the tree of awakening; in the Ratnakośa, bispala, tree of awakening, and aśvattha are synonyms. kuñjarsan is "elephant food," because elephants eat it. aśvatthaḥ is the abode of horses, or the special abode: by aśve tiṣṭhati, "it makes stand upon a horse," the suffix ka by the rule "from sus," and by pṛṣodar and the like.

Next, the words up to dantaśaṭha are names of the kapittha tree. Of these, kapittha is the abode of monkeys, because monkeys dwell in it: kasau tiṣṭhati kapittha. dadhittha is the abode of curd, because it is like curd: dadhiba tiṣṭhati dadhittha. It is also called drahī and manmatha, that is, "the holder" and "churner of the mind." dadhiphala is "curd fruit," because its fruit is like delicious curd. puṣpaphala is "flower fruit," because its fruit comes from the flower. dantaśaṭha is "spoiler of the tips of the teeth," because its juice harms the teeth.

Next, four names for the udumbāra tree. The word udumbāra has the third letter of the ta class; or else it also becomes uḍambara, and that is called "destroyer of Māra." The similar sound is for the sake of definite indication. jantuphala is the fruit of the juntu. yajñāṅga is the limb of sacrifice. hemadugdhaka is "possessed of golden milk," because a golden-colored milk comes from it.

Next, four names for the coral tree that splits the earth: kobidāra is the earth splitter, camarika is "possessed of a yak-tail whisk," kuddhāla is the earth destroyer, and yugapattra is "possessed of paired leaves."

Half a verse for the names of the seven-leaved tree. saptaparṇa is "seven leaved," and biśālatvaka is "of vast bark" or "of broad bark": its bark is thick. śāradi is called "autumn flower," because flowers appear on this tree in autumn: the suffix aṇ is added to śarāta by the rule "from the remainder." biṣamacchada is "uneven leaves," because it has seven leaves.

Next, eight names for the golden shower tree. āragabadha is the golden shower tree. rājabṛkṣa is the king's tree. śaṃpāka is ripened fruit. caturaṃgula is "four fingered." ārebata is "like a wheel." byādhighāta is the destroyer of disease. kṛtamāla is a garland of flowers; the Illumination of the Erotic explains it as a garland of flowers. subarṇaka is "golden colored."

Next, five names for the great citron. jambhira is the great citron. dantaśaṭha is "tooth harmer": it harms the teeth. jambha is "the sour one." jambhīra and jambhala are the same.

Next, five names for the tree of Varuṇa. baruṇa is the tree of Varuṇa, who is explained as the lord of a direction. barṇa is the fence tree, that is, prakara. seru is the white one. taktiśāka is "bitter leaf." kumaraka is "possessed of youth."

Next, the five beginning with bunnāga are names of the nāga tree. Next, the four beginning with paribhātra are names of the nimba tree. Next, the seven beginning with tiniśa are names of the aktimukta: tinisa; syandana, the dripping tree; nimī, the wheel-rim tree; rathadru, the chariot tree; atimuktaka, "the very free"; bañjala, the vessel tree; and citrakṛt, "the maker of variety." It grows in Mathurā and other lands.

Next, pītana, kapītana, and āmrātaka are names of the tree with emblic-like fruit.

Next, the five beginning with madhuka are names of the honey tree: madhuka, the honey tree; guḍapuṣpa, "molasses flower," because its flower is sweet like molasses; madhudruma, the tree of honey; bānaprastha, "dwelling in the forest"; and madhuṣṭhīla, called "holder of sweetness."

Next, the three beginning with pīlu are names of the pīlu tree that grows in kaṅkaṇa and other lands: pīlu; guḍaphala, "molasses fruit" or "sweet fruit," because its fruit is sweet like molasses; and sransīḥ, "fruit dropper," because its fruits fall. akṣoḍa and karparāma, the two, are names of the pīlu tree that grows in the mountains.

Next, the four beginning with palāśa are names of the kiṃśuka. palāśa is "having leaves of green color." kiṃśūka is "parrot, or what?": its color greatly captivates the mind. As the Questions and Answers of the Ornament of the Learned has it: "Is it a flock of young parrots that has settled there? Or else, since the new flowers of spring are in bloom, is it just the kiṃśuka tree?" parṇa is "leaf colored": pṛṇa, "to satisfy," with aca. bātapotha is "harmful to wind," the destroyer of wind.

Next, the words up to bañjula are names of the betasa tree, whose bark was explained earlier from the treatises of the Veda: betasa; the tree beta; the tree betasa, that is, the medicinal tree; ratha, the chariot tree; aphrapuṣpa, "cloud flower," since its flowers resemble clouds; and others of this kind.

The three beginning with paribyādha are names of the water betasa: paribyādha, "struck all around"; bidula; and nadeyī, "born of water," where nadī indicates a watery place. Next, the five beginning with saubhāñjana name the "beautiful eye salve," so called, according to the Cluster of Wonders, because in springtime it is as beautiful as a young man come into the bloom of youth: śigru; tikṣṇagandhakaḥdri, "sharp scented"; akṣiba, "eye-like"; and mocaka, "the releaser." saubhāñjana itself is of two kinds, one with red flowers and one with white flowers; the one with red flowers is madhuśigru, called "sweet śigru."

The soapberry tree has two names: ariṣṭa, the soapberry, and phenila, "the foamy."

Next, five names for the bilba tree: bilpa, śaṇḍilya, śilūṣa, mālūra, and śrīphala, "glorious fruit." The Classification of the Sage, the Seer, gives "mālūra and śaṇḍilya."

Next, three names for the blakṣa tree. blakṣa: from prakṣarati prakṣa, "it brings about full change," it is prakṣa, "full change"; and when the letter ra of that is changed into la by kapirika and the like, it is plakṣa. jaṭī is "the one with matted locks." And parkaṭi.

The three beginning with nyagrodha are names of the baṭi tree: nyagrodha, bahupā, "many footed," and bata.

Next, six are names of the lodhra tree: gālaba, the lodhra; sabara; lodhra, that is, lohita, "red"; trirīṭa; tilpa; and mārjana, "the purifier."

Next, three names for the mango: āmra; cūta, "the dripper"; and rasāla, "the juicy." For the special mango there are two: sahakāra, "the companion," and saubha āmra, called "the fragrant."

Next, with half a verse, the names of the guggulu tree: kumbha, "possessed of a pot"; ulukhala; kauśika; guglulu; and pura.

Next, five names are for the sebesten: that very word; śleṣmataka, "destroyer of phlegm"; śīta, "the white"; udhala; and bahubāraka, "the one with many around it."

With half a verse, the names of the piyala tree: rājādanaṃ, "the king's gift"; piyāla, "delight maker," "sweet fruit," because sweet juice comes from its fruit. sannakadru and dhanuḥpṭa are also the same.

Next, the seven names beginning with "the deep" belong to the glorious leaf tree: gambhīra, "the deep"; sarbatobhadra, "the all good"; kāśmari; madhuparṇikā, "sweet leaf"; śrīparṇī, "glorious leaf"; bhadraparṇī, "good leaf"; and kāśmarya.

Next, the three beginning with karkandhya are names of the small jujube. Next, the four beginning with kola are the tree of juniper berries. Next, five are names of the ladle tree: svādukaṇḍaka, "sweet thorn"; bikadkata; srubbṛkṣa, the ladle tree; granthila, "the knotted" or "the many knotted"; and byāghrapāda, "tiger's foot."

Next, two: airabata, "earth protector," and nagaradga, the nāga tree; these two are synonyms. Next, two: nadeya, the citron, and bhūmijambu, the jambu of the earth. Kaliṅga holds that all four of these are the nāga tree.

Next, four are names of the tinduka tree: tinduka; sphurjaka; kālaskandha, "black trunk"; and asitisāraka, "black pith." The four beginning with kakendu are names of the markaṭa tree: kākendu, "crow moon"; kulaka, "of good lineage"; crow pīluka, according to the master who composed the commentary on Mañjuśrī's byākarṇī; and kākatinduka.

The five beginning with golidha are names of the tree known in magadha and elsewhere as ghaṇḍapāṭala, "covered with bells." Next, three are names of the long-leaved tree called tilaka. The four beginning with kramuka are again names of the lodhra: kramukā, piḍḍāki, piḍḍā, and lakṣāprasādana, called "clarifier of lac," because when it is put in, it makes the juice come quickly from the lac and clarifies the lac. The six beginning with nūda are names of Brahmā's tree, which resembles the aśvattha in form. The six are kramuka, paṭṭikā, lodhri, gudaka, bhadra, and mustaka.

The four beginning with nīpa are names of the kadamba tree. nīpa: from nayati modaṃ nīpa, because it brings delight, it is nīpa; nīpapriyaka is "the delight maker," "the bringer of delight." kadamba is "possessed of clusters." hali is "possessed of a plough." priya is "the delight maker."

The four beginning with bīrapṛkṣa are names of the bhallata: bīrabṛkṣa is the hero's tree. In aruṣkara there is adurbraṇa: by "aru is a wound," it is called "wound maker." agnimukha is "fire face." And bhallatakī.

The five beginning with gardabhanda are the tree known as gaddhamuṇḍa; its thorns, leaves, and fruit resemble those of the aśvattha. The three beginning with bhurja are names of the birch: bhurja, "the one with ants"; carmi carmi, "possessed of skin"; and mridutvaka, "possessed of soft bark."

Next, the five beginning with piccha are names of the śalmala tree. picchala is "possessed of a tail," because it has a tail. sprurāyuḥni is "of lasting life," because it lives long. The two beginning with rocana are known as kāśambila. The four beginning with ciribilba are names of the kraranja. The four beginning with prakīryal are names of the thorny karañja, and ṣaḍganthā, "six knotted," and the like are kinds of karañja.

The four beginning with rohī are names of the pomegranate tree. plāhaśatru is "enemy of the liver," because it destroys liver disease. dāḍimaputpaka is "possessed of pomegranate flowers."

The four beginning with gāyātrī are names of the khadira: gāyātrī is "the singer." Next, a special kind of khadira: biḍakhadira is the excrement khadira, which grows on waterless desert plains and has a smell like excrement. Because it destroys fat, it is also called arime and "enemy of fat." khadirsita, the white khadira, and somabalka, "moon branch," are synonyms.

Next, the eleven beginning with the byāghrapuccha tree are names of the eraṇḍa tree: byaghrapuccha, "tiger's tail"; gandharbahastaka, "gandharva's hand," because its leaves have the form of five fingers; and pañcādgula, "five fingered." The three beginning with śami are names of the śamā tree. The six beginning with paṇḍika are the intoxicating tree, known as mayana. Those beginning with śakrapādapa, "Śakra's foot drinker," are the six names of the debadāru: pītadāru, "yellow wood"; dāru, the juniper; pūtakāṣṭha; and the resinous tree.

Next, the seven beginning with pāṭala are names of the trumpet-flower tree. The twelve beginning with byāma are names of the priyaṃ tree: śyāma is the dark blue one, priyaṃgu is "the delight maker," and priyaka is the same. The twelve beginning with maṇḍūkaparṇa are names of the red tree. maṇḍukapharṇa is "frog leaf," because its leaf is shaped like a frog. Here a line appears to have been dropped through a slip of the eye; it seems to distinguish the grain priyaṃsku from the tree driyaṃkuya. śoṇaka is the red one. arlu is pinkish, being aruṇa itself, altered by kapilika and the like.

The four beginning with tiṣyaphala are names of the emblic tree. tiṣyaphala is "Puṣya fruit." āmlakī is the emblic. amṛtā is "possessed of nectar." bayaḥsthā is "preserver of age," because it keeps one's age and youth steady.

Next, the six beginning with bībhitaga are names of the beleric myrobalan: bibhītaka is the beleric; bhūtabāsa is the abode of the bhūtas; kalidruma is the tree of strife.

Next, one verse, beginning with abhaya, gives the names of the chebulic myrobalan. abhaya is the fearless, because with it there is no fear of disease. abyatha is "free of illness." bathyā is "the beneficial." bayaḥsthā is "preserver of age." pūtana is "the resinous." amṛtā is "possessed of nectar." haritakī is the chebulic myrobalan, called "the remover." hemabatī is "the golden." cetakī is "the mind maker." śreyasī is "the glorious," or "the auspicious." śiba is "the pacifier."

Next, the three beginning with pitadru are names of the pine: bītadru, the yellow tree; sarala, the holder of essence; and pūtikāṣṭha, the pine.

Next, the three beginning with drumotpal are names of the utpala tree: drumotpala, the utpala tree, and karṇikāra, known as kanmiyāra, which has yellow flowers the size of a lotus.

Next, kaṇḍaphala and the one after it, the two, are the tree with thorny fruit, because its fruit has thorns. The three beginning with nicula are names of the water-born tree. Next, six, which the earlier translators appear to have rendered as golden shower tree, are kākodumbāra, names of the crow udumbā.

Next, the six beginning with ariṣṭa are names of the nimba tree. Next, five are names of the agaru: picchila, "possessed of a tail"; aguru, known as agaru; śinśāpa; kapila, "the tawny"; and bhasmagarbha, "having ash at its core." picchila is also explained for the śalmali tree.

Next, three are names of the śriṣa tree. Next, campeya, campaka, and hemapuṣpa, "golden flower," are names of the cambaka, because its flowers are golden in color. bañjula is the aśoka: because Rudra did not want a wife, Gaurī practiced austerities beneath an aśoka tree; her wish was entirely fulfilled and she became free of sorrow, and so this tree is the aśoka, "the sorrowless."

Next, the four beginning with cāmpe are names of the nāgakesara: kesar is "possessed of filaments," and kāñcanāhvaya is "bearing the name of gold."

Next, five are names of the victorious plant: jayā, "the victorious"; jayanti, "the victor"; tarkāri, "enemy of reasoning"; nādeyī, "river born"; and baijayantikā, "the all-victorious."

Next, the five beginning with śrīparṇa are names of the white sandal. śrīparṇa is the leaf of the glorious leaf tree. agnimantha is the fire stick, because fire comes from rubbing this wood. The four beginning with kuṭaja are names of the kuṭaja tree, known as kuṭaya.

Next, the three beginning with sumna are names of the jāti tree: suman, "the fair minded"; māyati, "the garlanded"; and jāti, the jasmine. Next, two are names of the kunda: maghya is "born in Māgha," because it appears in the last month of winter.

Next, three are names of the bandhujība flower: pandhujībaka is called the flower "life of kinsmen"; bandhuka is "possessed of kin"; and rakta is "the red."

The five beginning with pratiha are names of the karabīra: pratihasa, "laughing in turn"; śataprāsa, "split a hundredfold"; caṇūta, "the fierce"; hayamāraka, "the horse killer"; and karapār, "the hero's hand."

The seven beginning with "the intoxicator": in the Aṅgulimālīya Sūtra it is said, "Like the flower of the bandhujīva, it turns red like the color of a lake." They are names of the dhustu ra: udmatta, "the intoxicator"; kanakāhvaya, "bearing the name of gold"; and madana, "the intoxicating."

Next, four are names of the bījapū: bījapūra, "full of seeds"; palapūra, "full of fruit"; rucaka, "of beautiful color"; and mātuluṅga, the citron.

Next, three are names of the citraka: paṭhi; citraka, "the speckled"; and bahnisaṃjñā, "bearing the name of fire." Next, seven are names of the arkaparṇa: arkāhva, "bearing the name of the sun," arkaparṇa, "sun leaf," and so on.

Next, four names for the parasitic plant: bandā, the parasite; bṛkṣādanā, "eater of trees"; bṛkṣaruha, "tree born," because it grows on trees; and jībantikā, "the sustainer."

Those beginning with "calf eater" are names of the guḍūcī: batsād; cchinnaruha, "growing when cut," because it grows even when cut; guḍūci, the guḍūcī; tantrukā, "possessed of threads"; and the like.

The ten beginning with "line" and "near line" are names of the marmot leaf. Next, eight are names of the ābamārga tree. The nine beginning with phañjika are names of the tshangs ma tree. Next, nine are names of the madder plant: mañjiṣṭha is madder, or dyer's madder.

Next, the ten beginning with yāsa are names of the thorn-head plant.

Next, the nine beginning with pṛṣṇiparṇi are names of the lion's tail plant: pṛṣṇiparṇa is "heel leaf" or "trailing leaf"; siṃhapuccha is "lion's tail," because it has a flower spike shaped like a lion's tail.

The ten beginning with nidagdhikā are names of the kaṇḍaka: bidagdhikā, "the clever one"; spṛśī, "the one to be touched"; byāghrī, "the tigress"; pṛhatī, "the great one of all"; kaṇḍakārikā, "she who makes thorns"; pracodanā, "she who strongly urges"; kulī, "she of good family"; kṣudra, "the trifling one"; duḥpparṣā, "the one hard to touch"; and rāṣṭikā, "the one born in the realm."

Next, the eleven beginning with nīla are names of the indigo plant: nīlī is indigo; rañjinī is "the one that cooks."

Next, eight are names of the somarāja.

Next, those from kṛṣṇa through kola are names of long pepper: baidehī is called "she who frequents Videha," and māgadhī "she who frequents the Central Land"; uṣaṇā is "the one that produces heat."

Next, those up to baśira are names of karipipili, that is, elephant long pepper. baśir also refers to the apāmārga tree and to sea salt.

The verse beginning with palaṅga gives names of the cattle-target tree.

Next, the eight beginning with biśva are names of aconite.

Next, the ten beginning with śatamūlī are names of the hundred-rooted plant.

Next, the seven beginning with pītadru are names of barberry: pītadru is "yellow wood," haridraba is "yellow juice," and so on.

Next, the five beginning with baca are names of sweet flag: baca is sweet flag; agragandha, "peak of fragrance"; ṣaḍgrantha, "six-knotted"; golomī, "hair of the earth"; and śataparbakā, "hundred-jointed." By the distinction of its root there are two kinds, white and red.

The eight beginning with "mother of physicians" are names of what is known as baśaka: baidyāmātṛ is "mother of physicians"; basaka, "the one that dwells"; sidhāsya, "lion-faced," because its flower resembles a lion's mouth; bajidantaka, "horse-toothed," because its flower is rough like a horse's teeth.

Next, four are names of the aparājita: girikarṇa is "ear of the mountain"; biṣṇukrānta, "trodden by Viṣṇu"; aparājita, "unconquerable by others."

The four beginning with "fragrant as sugarcane" are names of what is known as koyilakha: ikṣugandha is "fragrant as sugarcane"; kokilākṣa is "cuckoo eye," for kokila is the cuckoo and akṣa the eye. That is what is known as koyilakha.

From śāleya up to mistre are names of madhurikā, "the sweet one."

The six beginning with sīkuṇḍa are names of what is known as sajji.

The six beginning with balle are names of biḍaṅka: biḍaṃga is "body of excrement"; amogha, "purposeful," that is, bearing fruit; kṛmighna, "destroyer of worms."

The two beginning with bāla are names of what is known as baḍiyāla.

Half a verse gives the names of the grape, which in Tibet is known as byi dang ga: mṛddhīkā is the grape; gostānī, "the cow's teat"; drakṣā, the grape; madhursa, "sweet in taste."

The four beginning with madhuka are names of licorice: madhūka is "the sweet one"; madhuyaṣḍi is licorice.

The four beginning with lāṅgali are names of kāñcaka; śāradī, "autumn flower," was explained earlier, under the seven-leaved tree.

The five beginning with khar are names of the peacock's crest, that is, bod bu.

The five beginning with gopī are names of the dark blue one: śyāmala is "the dark blue one." Some call it nāgajihba, "nāga's tongue."

The four beginning with yoga are names of the medicine called "the miraculous one."

The six beginning with kadalī and rambha are names of the plantain.

Next, the three beginning with mudgaparṇi are names of the plant called "bean-leaved."

The five beginning with brtakī are names of the bāliṅga plant.

The verse beginning with nākuli gives names of a medicinal plant that merchants and others call rāsna medicine.

From bidārigandha, "destroyer's fragrance," up to "abiding firmly," are names of sālaparṇi, "leaf of the sāla."

The two lines beginning with karpasī are names of the cotton tree.

Next, the three beginning with tambula are names of the leaf creeper: tāmbulaballī is "leaf creeper"; tāmbuli, "the one with the go la," that is, "the one with tooth dye"; and nāgaballī, "nāga creeper," because it is the creeper of the world of the nāgas.

The six beginning with dvija are names of a tree known among merchants and others as the aromatic substance reṇuka, whose fruit is shaped like black pepper.

The five beginning with elabalu are names of a fragrant tree known as the elabaluka tree.

The three beginning with saliki are names of the fragrant tree called kunduru.

The half verse beginning with what is called bāla concerns the fragrant plant known as bāla: bālaṃ is "like hair"; hrībera is "body of shame," for hrī is shame and bera is body. As for what is called bardiṣṭha, brda applies to both the peacock and the forest, so here it means "dwelling in the forest." dībyāṃkeśa is "beautiful hair"; ambunāma, "the one named for water."

The five beginning with "following time" are names of a fragrant essence and substance that comes from rocky mountains: kālānusāri is "following time"; bṛddha, "the old one"; uṣmapuṣpa, "flower of stone"; śīta, "the cool one"; śibā, "the peaceful one"; śaileya, which is śaileja, "born of rock."

The five beginning with tālaparṇi are names of the fragrant substance known as mura. Because it bears the very name of the asura called mura, it is daityā, "the asura woman," and murā. tālaparṇi is "palm leaf"; gandhakuṭī, "she of heaped fragrance"; and gandhinī, "she who has fragrance."

gajabhakṣya is "elephant's food"; the eight beginning with it are names of the śallakī tree, from which sihla flows.

Here, subahā is "flowing well"; surabhi, "the fragrant"; rasā, "having taste" or "having essence"; maheraṇa, "greatly moving"; kundurukī, "flowing essence"; sihlakī, "satisfying drip"; and hladinī, "the satisfier." These are names of sihla.

The four beginning with "non-blazing" are names of the dhātaka.

The four beginning with pṛthīkā are names of kakola: pṛthīkā is "she who has the earth"; candrabāla, "moon sand" or "young moon"; ela is kakola; niṣkuṭiḥhu is the hu la, "having many nests."

The five beginning with sūkṣma are names of small cardamom.

The six beginning with byādhi are names of the fragrant substance known as kūṭa: kuṣṭha is costus. That very thing is known as kūṭa. byādhi designates it by the name of a disease.

The three beginning with "conch-bearer" are names of what is known as rcorkuli.

The six beginning with tunna are names of the joy-making tree. It has milky sap and has the form of the aśvattha, as it is said: "Similar in form to the aśvattha, It is called the joy-making tree."

The six beginning with what is called "rākṣasī" are names of the aromatic substance named cora.

The four beginning with "snake's weapon" are names of the aromatic substance called "tiger's claw."

The five beginning with śuṣira are names of the aromatic substance named "stalk."

The five beginning with dhamani are the aromatic substance named haṭa, "the graceful."

The five beginning with śukti are names of nakha: śukti, like a pearl oyster shell; śaṅkha, like a conch; khura, like a hoof; koladala means like the leaf of the jujube; nakha, like a nail.

The eight beginning with kuḍannaṭa are names of li ga dur: kuḍannaṭa is li ga dur, and means "crooked and dancing"; daśapūraṃ, "tenfold full"; bāneya, "forest-born"; paripelaba and plabaga, "the swimmer" or "going by swimming"; kaipartī, "the fisherman" or "the great swimmer."

The six beginning with granthiparṇa are names of granthiba.

The one verse from what is called matuta up to what is called "ornament of summer" gives names of sbṛkka.

The five beginning with "the ascetic woman" are names of spikenard: tapasvīnī, "the ascetic woman"; jaṭī, "she with matted locks"; māṃsī, "she with flesh," that is, spikenard; lomasā, "she with hair"; and so on.

The six beginning with tvakpatra are names of cinnamon: tvakpatra is called "bark leaf."

The five beginning with kurbaśīrṣa are names of jībaka: kurbaśīrṣa is "made into a head"; jībaka, "the sustainer."

The three beginning with kirtatikta are what is known as ciryite, names of the tikti that grows in the land of Mon and elsewhere.

The seven beginning with sapta are names of the aromatic substance called "the sevenfold," a merchant's commodity.

Those beginning with ajamod are names of yamānika; for its two kinds there are four synonyms. ajamoda is "delight of goats."

The three beginning with "extensive root" are what merchants know as puṣkaramūla, "lotus root": kāṣmira is "born in Kashmir"; padmapattra, "lotus leaf."

The two beginning with mārga are names of what is called bhṛṅgarāja, "king of bees"; this is the very thing known as keśarañjana.

The six beginning with dūrba are names of dūrba grass: dūrba is what is known as dūrba; śataparbikā is "hundred-jointed"; sahasrabīrya, "having a thousand potencies"; bhārgabya, "son of bhṛgu," that is, born of Bhṛgu; ruhā, "the growing one"; and ananta, "the endless."

The four beginning with golam are names of white dūrba: golomi is "growing ox hair"; śatapuṣpa, "having a hundred potencies"; gaṇḍālī, "holding pieces"; śakulākṣa, "bird's eye."

The three beginning with kurubinda are names of the mon lug.

The verse beginning with biśa gives names of the bamboo.

The three beginning with granthi are names of "the jointed."

The three beginning with gundra are names of the reed used for arrows.

The three beginning with naḍa are names of the marsh reed.

Four are names of kuśa grass: kuśa is "remover of evil," ku meaning evil and śa meaning pacifying or removing. kutha is "abiding in the earth"; ṅarbha, "the support"; and sabidra, "the purifier."

kartṛṇe is "small grass," and the six beginning with it are names of the fragrant grass known as rāma camphor.

What is called "parasol-shaped" is a particular kind of grass. As it is said:
"The fragrant one and the Hot, as they are called,
Likewise the parasol and the great parasol,
Are called parasol-shaped;
Their root and leaves are well formed."

nārikela and lāṅgali are synonyms. Of these, nārikela means "woman's play" and is a name of the bita tree; lāṅgalī is "having a plough."

This is the section on forests and medicinal plants.

Next, in the section on lions and the rest, half a verse gives the names of the lion. siṅgha is the lion: from hisi, in the sense of harming, since it harms wild animals, there is the suffix aca, and by pṛṣodara and so on the first and the last are reversed. mṛgendra is "lord of beasts," because it is the king of the beasts of the forest. As for pañcāsya, pañca is five and asya is mouth or face, so it is called "five-faced" or "five-mouthed," because, just as with its mouth, it is able to crush the frontal globes of the elephant with its two feet and its hands as well. As for haryakṣa, hari is "the seizer" and akṣi is eye; by the rule "after a word for one's own limb, ṣaca," the suffix ṣaca is added. Hence it is called "seizer-eyed," because its eyes are reddish yellow. keśarī is "the maned one." hari is "the seizer," because it seizes the lives of the beasts.

Next, the tiger has three names: śārdūl, dvīpa and byāghra. Of these, śārdūla is the tiger: from śṛ, in the sense of harming, with ūraca and so on. dvīpī is a kind of tiger that is small and spotted; since it possesses that second skin, there is the suffix ini. This usage is kamalaśīla's: "The small tiger, the tiger, the cat and the otter, With the lion's skin placed in front." byāghra is "the smeller," that is, the tiger.

Next, two are names of the hyena: tarakṣu is the hyena, the size of a large dog and shaped like a dog, which harms livestock, people and so on; mṛgādana is "eater of beasts."

For the names of the pig there is one verse. bāraha is the pig, because it destroys supremely. sūkara is "the cleanser": suca in the sense of clean, and kara in the sense of making; by pṛṣodara and so on the ca is dropped, and by the rule "also in other cases," the su is changed to a long vowel. ghṛṣṭi is "having sound."

kolapoṭi is "the house-cleaner." kiri is "the earth-scatterer": the root kṝ is in the sense of scattering, and by the rule for roots whose penultimate is ik, the suffix ki is added. kiḍi is "the earth-raiser"; likewise, from the verbal root kiṭ comes kiṭi. daṃṣṭi is "the tusked one," and ghoṇī "the long-snouted." stabdharomā is "the bristle-haired." kroḍa is "the earth-turner." bhūdā is "the earth-breaker."

From kapi up to "forest-dwellers" are the names of the monkey. kapiḥ is the monkey. plavaṃga and plavaga mean "the one who runs leaping or bounding," or "the one who goes by springing." śākhamṛga is "the beast of the branches," balīmukha "the wrinkle-faced," mārkaṭa "the sun-lover," and vānara "the one who is like a man," for it is said, vāśabda ivārthe: "the word vā has the sense of 'like.'" kīśa is "of the lineage of the son of the Wind," and vanaukasa are "the forest-dwellers."

Next, bhalluka and the rest are names of the bear.

Next, the three beginning with ghaṇḍaka and khaḍgin are names of the rhinoceros.

With half a verse come the names of the buffalo: lulāpa, "of mixed herds"; mahiṣa, "the one who lies on the ground"; vāhadviṣa, "the enemy of horses"; kāpara, "the buffalo"; and soribha.

With one verse come the names of the jackal. bhūmimāya is "the one who roams the earth." gomāyu is "the harmer of cattle." mṛgadhūrta is "the cheat among beasts," śṛgala "the savage one," vañjaka "the deceiver," kroṣṭi "the howler," and pheru and pherab "the wickedly cunning," or "the crafty one." jambuka is the jackal.

With half a verse come the names of the cat: oru, "the crouching one"; viḍāla, "the one who hides its dung," for it hides its dung as though it were poison; mārjāra, "the washer"; pṛṣadaṃśaka, "the rat-eater"; and ākhubhuk, "the one whose food is rats."

The three beginning with koka are names of the wolf. koka is the wolf: a tawny beast the size of a dog, called kova in Gauḍa and other lands, and known as vika in Mālava and other lands.

Next, the half-verse beginning with mṛga gives the names for wild beasts in general. eṇa denotes a particular kind of deer, and what comes from it is eṇeya.

The six beginning with katali are particular deer from which precious hides come, and so they are called "sources of hides." Just as the word mṛga applies in general to other animals and in particular to the kuraṅga deer, in the way that the word for metal applies in general to gold and the rest and in particular to iron, so the expression "source of hides" likewise applies both to deer in general and to particular kinds.

From kṛṣṇasāra to the end with the hare are the names of individual kinds. Among these, kṛṣṇasāra is the black-bodied deer. ruru is "the roarer." nyaṅku is called "the one surely worshipped." śambara is called "supreme bliss." gokarṇa, because it has ears like a cow's, is "cow-ear"; it is known as ganno. pṛṣata is "the one with variegated spots," for the Ratnakośa says, "pṛṣata is the variegated deer." eṇa is cārunayana, "the fair-eyed": it has short shanks and is small in size. "eṇa is a small deer," says the extensive commentary on the Kośa. ṛśya is blue in color and equal in size to a horse; it is three-horned, and is also called rihita. camara is the yak: it has the shape of a buffalo, is born in the northern lands, and from its tail come the chowries. gandharva is a particular kind of beast, to be understood as what is called the horse-hybrid. śarabha is eight-legged, has eyes facing upward, and kills even lions; as Bharata says:

"The eight-legged one with eyes upturned,
The śarabha, roams the forest;
It goes forth to slay the lion.
Into that form the sage surely entered."

Four of its legs face upward. gavaya is "the not-cow," for it is similar in shape to a cow.

Next, the mouse has three names: anduru, "the mouse"; mūṣeka, "the thief"; and ākhu, "the burrower." girikā, "the mountain mouse," and bālamūṣika, "the young mouse," are two names of the little mouse.

The spider has four names: lūtā, "the spider"; tantravāya, "the weaver"; ūrṇanābha, "the one with a navel of wool"; and mārkaṭakasam, "like a monkey."

Next, among the names of birds, the pigeon has three names: kapota and pārāvata, "the pigeon," and kalarava, "the one who coos," or "the sweet-voiced."

Next, the hawk has three names: śaśādana, "the hare-eater"; patatri, "the variegated winged one"; and śyena, "the hawk."

The owl has three names. ulūka is "the one with ears on top": ūrdhvakarṇa is changed into ulūka by the rule of pṛṣodara and so on. vāyasārāti is "the enemy of crows": vāyasa is the crow and arāti the enemy. pecaka is the owl.

The vulture has two names: lohapṛṣṭha, "the red-backed," and kaṅka.

Next, cāśa and kokīdivi are both names of the bird whose plumage is clear as beryl and whose wings are bright, the one known as "golden crest."

Next, the woodpecker has two names: śatapatra, "the hundred-feathered," and dārvāghāṭa, "the wood-smasher," or "the enemy of wood," that is, the woodpecker: dāru is wood and āghāṭa is smashing.

Next, the cātaka has three names: saraṅga, "the cātaka"; tokaka, "the one of little water," from stoka, little, and ka, water, with the letter s dropped; and cātaka.

The domestic fowl has four names: kṛkavāku; kukkaḍa, "the domestic fowl," because it cries out "kuku"; tāmracūḍa, "the one with a copper crest"; and caraṇāyudha, "the one whose weapons are its feet."

The village sparrow has two names: caṭaka, "the sparrow," and kalaviṅka.

The cuckoo has four names: vanapriya, "the lover of the forest"; parabhṛta, which is called "nourished by another," because the crow takes it and rears it; and kokila and sika, "the cuckoo."

For the names of the crow there is one verse. Because it utters the sound "kā," it is kākva, the crow. Master Jñānavajra said that the kalaviṅka is a particular bird that dwells on the islands of the ocean; it is the one whose voice is exceedingly sweet. karaṭa is the raven. As for ariṣṭa, riṣṭa is the mark of death; since this bird has none, it is ariṣṭa, "the deathless." balipuṣṭa is "the one nourished by torma offerings." sakṛtpraja is "the one born once." dhākṣa is "the one with a voice." ātmaghoṣa is "the one whose very nature is to cry out." parabhṛta is called "the nourisher of others," because it takes the chicks of another, the cuckoo, and rears them.

balibhuk is "the eater of torma offerings." vāyasa is the crow, called "the one who abides through the years." The four beginning with droṇaka are names of the great crow, the raven. The kite, the vulture, the parrot and the rest are simply well known. kruṅ and krauñca are what is called the crane, known as koñca. The four beginning with koka are names of the ruddy goose. kādamba and kalahaṃsa are names of the water-heron. Two are names of the kurara bird: utkrośa, "heard from afar," and kurara, "the crier."

Four are general names of the goose: haṃsa, "the goose"; śvetagaruda, "the white garuḍa"; cakrāṅga, "the one with wheel-like limbs"; and mānasaukasa, "the dweller at Lake Mānasa." rājahaṃsa and the rest are names of the royal goose and so on; up to varaṭa, each line of the verse gives a name.

Next, for the names of the bat: jatuka, "the bat," and ajinapatra, "the one with leather wings."

The four beginning with bhṛgarī are the winged creature called jhiñjhika, which mostly lives in the wilderness and in the hot season of summer makes the sound "jhit" with its mouth.

For the firefly: khadyota, "light of the sky"; jyoti, "starlight"; and iṅgaṇa, "light of the heavens."

For the names of the bee there is one verse: madhuvrata, "the one vowed to honey"; madhukara, "the honey-maker"; madhuliḍa, "the honey-taster"; madhupālita, "the guardian of honey"; dvirepha, "the one with two r's," because its name bhramara, "bee," has two letters r; puṣpaliḍ, "the flower-taster"; dhṛṅga, "the bee"; ṣaṭpada, "the six-footed"; bhramara, "the one who goes in circles"; and ali, "the bee."

Next, for the names of the peacock there is one verse: mayūra, "the one who walks the ground"; barhiṇa, "the one with eyed plumes"; barhi, "the one possessed of eyed plumes"; nīlakaṇṭha, "the blue-throated"; bhujaṅgabhuk, "the eater of serpents, those that go on their arms"; śikhāvala, "the crested"; śikhi, "the one with a tuft"; kekī, "the one who cries 'keke'"; and meghanādānulāsī, "the one who dances in answer to the thunder." The peacock's cry is called kekī. The moon-shaped spot like an eye that sits on its tail is mecaka, which is called the eye of the plume; mecaka was also explained earlier as a word for black.

Next, with two and a half verses come the general names for birds. khaga is "the sky-goer." vihaṅga, vihaga, vihaṅgama and vihāyasa are "the sky-goer," "the sky-mover," "the one who ranges the sky," and so on. śakunti is "the bird." pakṣi is "the winged." śakuni, śakunta and śakunā are likewise "the bird," or "the able one." dvija is "the twice-born." patratri is "the feathered." patri is "the quilled." pataga is "the flier." satata is "the one who goes by flying." patrathara is "the one whose chariot is its wings." aṇḍaja is "the egg-born." nagauka is "the tree-dweller": naga, "the one that does not move," is a name for the tree. vajikira is "the eater of insects." viḥ is "the bird." biskira is "the winged." nīḍodbhava is "the nest-born." gatunmanta is "the one with wings." psitanta is "the one who longs to fly." nabhasaṅgama is "the one who roams the sky."

Next, harita and the rest are names of particular birds. tittira and the rest are names of the partridge. jreṃjīva and the rest are names of the shangshang bird; jīvañjī is called "the life-giver, the one who makes live," because it revives those dying of poison. The word garut and what follows, such as "feather," are names of things connected with birds.

With the two verses beginning with samūha come the names of a collection: samūha, "a collection"; nivaha, "a gathering"; vyūha, "a heap"; sandoha, "a mass"; saṃghata, "an assemblage"; and sicaya and the rest, "an accumulation," and so on.

This concludes the section on lions and other animals.

Next, from the section on human beings, for the general names of a human being: manuṣya, "the human"; manuṣa, "the mind-born"; martya, "the one endowed with knowledge"; manujā, "the son of Manu"; mānava, "the one possessed of mind"; "the human"; pumāṃs, "the male"; and pañcajana, "the one born of five," because it is born from the five elements, earth and the rest. puruṣa and pūruṣa are "the person." nṛ is "humans."

For the names of a woman: strī, "the woman"; yoṣit, "the lady"; abalā, "the one without strength"; nārī, "the female human"; sīmantinī, "the one with a parting in her hair"; vadhū, "the one who binds"; and in pratīpadarśinī, pratīpa has the meaning of kaṭākṣa, a sidelong glance, so it is called "the one who looks askance." vāmā is "the left-hand one"; vanitā is "the one who turns aside"; and mahelā is "the ground of festivity," as the Kapphiṇābhyudaya explains: maha is festivity and ilā is ground.

"In particular" refers to the terms for women of special kinds. aṅganā means śreṣṭhāṅgī, called "the one with a supreme body," because she has a body with the defining marks of a woman, most auspicious feet, hands and the rest; she is called "the fair-bodied." bhīru is "the timid," the one whose nature is fearful. kāminī is "the one full of desire." vāmalocanā is "the one with left-glancing eyes," that is, the fair-eyed. pramadā is "the one deeply intoxicated," the one with the amorous changes of manner produced by a form of the utmost pride and by good fortune. bhāvinī is "the one possessed of grace," for bhava is graceful, charming conduct, and she possesses it. kāntā is "the one with a beautiful body." lalanā is "the one with a youthful body," the one whose body is very young. nitambinī is "the one with full hips." sundarī is "the beautiful," the one endowed with perfect beauty of form. ramaṇī and rāmā are "the one who gives delight." Then kopanā is "the one who grows angry." In this way, all the names connected with women are to be understood.

The eight beginning with rajasvalā, "the one with the dust," are names of a menstruating woman.

A pregnant woman has four names: āpannasattvā, "the one into whom a sentient being has entered"; gurviṇī, "the one heavy in body"; antarvattvaṃ, "the one with something within"; and garbhiṇī, "the one bearing an embryo."

A son has five names: ātmaja, "the self-born"; tanaya, "the one who extends the body"; and sūnu, suta and putra, all likewise "son." These are they.

A father has three names: tāta, "the father," or "the protector"; janaka, "the begetter"; and pitṛ, "the father," that is, the one who guards.

A mother has four names: janayatī, "the mother," or "she who gives birth"; prasū, "she who brings forth"; mātā, "she who nourishes"; and jananī, "she who gives birth."

A sister has two: bhaginī, "the sister," or "the elder sister"; and svasā, "the sister."

A grandchild has two names: natrī and pautrī, "the child of a son."

For the general names of relatives there are six: sagotra, "the one of the same clan"; bāntapa, "the related"; jñāti, "the kinsman"; bandhu, "the kin"; sva, "the relation"; and svajana, "one's own kind," or "one's own people," or "kinsfolk."

A married couple has four names: daṃpati, "the couple"; jaṃpati, "husband and wife," where the words dam and jaṃ in these are indeclinable words that express "wife"; bhāryāpatī, "the householders"; and jāyāpatī, "the couple."

For the names of childhood there is one line: śiśu, "the child"; śaiśaraṃ, "the offspring"; and balyaṃ, "the little one."

For the names of youth there is one line: tāruṇya, "youth"; and yovanaṃ, "the vigorous," or "the one in the bloom of youth."

For the names of old age there is one line: sthāviraṃ, "the elder"; vṛddhatvaṃ, "the old"; and jīrṇa and jaran, "the aged" and "the decrepit." daśamī is "the tenth," and jyāyāna "the most aged": one who has passed beyond ninety years is in the tenth stage, the most aged.

Next, for the general names of medicine there are five: bheṣaja, "the remedy"; oṣadha, "the medicine"; bhaiṣajya, "the medicinal remedy"; agada, "the freedom from disease"; and jāyuḥ, "the life-giver."

For disease in general there are seven names: ruk, and ruja, "disease"; upatāpa, "torment," because disease torments body and mind; and roga, vyādhi, gada and āmaya are also disease, that is, fever, malaise and the like.

Next, graṇa and aru are names of a wound.

For the names of the physician there is half a verse. rogahārī is "the destroyer of disease," because he carries disease away. agadaṃkāra is "the remover of disease," because he makes one free from disease. bhiṣag is "the doctor." vaidya is "the healer," because he knows the science of life. cikitsaka is "the one skilled in healing."

Semen has six names: śukra, "semen"; tejaḥ, "the moon," or "splendor"; retasī, "the drop"; bīja, "the seed"; vīrya, "potency"; and indriya, "the faculty."

Next, māyu and pittaṃ are bile. kapha and śleṣma are phlegm.

Next, tvak is the skin: by the rule "tvaca in the sense of covering," it is so called because it covers the cage of the bones, the flesh and the blood. asṛgdhara, "the holder of blood," is the skin.

Flesh has six names. piśitaṃ is flesh in the sense of "a portion": paśa is in the sense of portion, and the suffix kta is added. tarasaṃ is flesh because it carries across: tṝ is in the sense of carrying across, and, as with devapa, the suffix asac is added. mansa is "the honored": māna is in the sense of honoring, and as with the word hasa, the suffix sa is added. palala, "the nourisher," is flesh. kravya and āmiṣa are flesh.

With half a verse come the names of blood. rudhira is "the pervading," that is, blood. asṛk: from atisṛjati, "it pours out to excess," it is changed into asṛk by the rule of pṛṣodara and so on, because if no weapon strikes, it does not fall, but if one strikes, it pours out to excess. lohita and rakta are blood because it is red. kṣataja, "born of a wound," is blood. śoṇitaṃ is "the one with channels," or "the red."

The heart has four: pukka, "the heart"; agramānsa, "the choicest flesh"; hṛdaya, "the core"; and hṛd, which is also the heart. Because it dwells within the body, it is a particular kind of flesh shaped like a lotus, facing downward. As it is said in the work called the White Lotus Garland:

"Like the anthers of a lotus,
Hollow, and facing downward:
That is what is called the heart,
The great base of all."

The channels of the body have three names: nāḍī, dhamanī and śirā; they are hollow, the winds flow through them, and like threads they bind the bones. Then kloma; and mastiṣkaṃ, "the full brain," or "the brain," which comes from the head and is like a lump of butter, known as ghibhi.

The impurities that come from the food lodged within and flow out, snot and the rest, are of twelve kinds; as it is said:

"Fat and semen, marrow and blood,
Earwax, feces, urine, snot, the nails,
Phlegm and tears, and grime:
These are the twelve impurities of humans."

With three come the names of saliva: sṛṇika, "mouth-water"; syandinī; and lālā, "saliva."

With two come the names of urine: mūtra, "urine," or "piss"; and prasrava, "the voiding," or "the steady flow."

uccāra and the rest are the names of excrement. uccāra is "that which is to be discarded." avaskara is "the pervasive." śamala is "the mixed." śakṛt is "the mover." purīṣaṃ is "excrement." gutha is "dung." bartsaskaṃ is "filth." viṣṭhā and viḍa are "excrement."

Next, for the names of the skull: karoṭi, karbaṭa and kapāla.

Bone in general has three names: kīkasaṃ; kulyaṃ, "the relics," or "the bones"; and asthi, "the bone."

The twelve beginning with kalevara are general names for the body. Among them, kalevara or karevara is "the trunk," or "the body." gātraṃ is "the mass," or "the body"; vasu, "the aggregate"; saṃhananaṃ, "the assemblage"; śarīraṃ, "the hearer"; varṣya, "the form"; vigraha, "the holder of shapes"; kāya, "the body"; deha, "the pervading"; mārti, "the body"; and tanu and tanū are also the body. The former is from tanu in the sense of expanding, with the suffix u, as in the word meru; the latter takes the suffix u by the rule "u after cami, tani, badhi and the rest."

The foot has four names: pāda and pada, "the foot," because it makes one go; caraṇa, "the mover"; and aṃghri, "the goer": from ahi in the sense of going, the suffix krin is added by the rule "from many." In the Hundred Verses in Praise of Lokeśvara it is written aṃhri, following the letter h, in the line:

kleśabhuhāhite hāhutibihita bṛhatrohiduṣyagnidāhābāhīnī stārbahubihitahitā padmahastāṃhribhabaḥ

The female organ has three names: upastha, "the near-abiding"; bhaga, "the portion," or "the fortune"; and yoni, "the place of birth."

The male organ has four names: śaśma, "the male organ"; metra, "the tail"; and mahena, "the water-shaker," because it discharges urine and semen; and śephasi, "the abode of rest": from śiṅ in the sense of lying down, with the suffix asun and the augment phuṭ.

Three are names of the testicles: muṣko 'ṇḍa, "the testicle"; koṣa, "the one with a store"; and vṛṣaṇa, "the one with a sheath."

The belly has five names: piciṇḍaṃ, "the paunch"; kukṣe, "the abdomen"; jaṭhara; and udara, "the belly," from rund and rūṇa in the sense of filling; as with adha and the rest, the suffix dan is added, together with the shortening and so on.

Next, stana and kuca are names of the breast.

The shoulder has three names. skandha is the shoulder: kaṃ is the head, and by kaṃ dadhāti skandha, "that which holds the ka is the skandha," it is the shoulder because it holds the head, which is kaṃ; by pāraskara and the rest, the augment suṭ is applied. bhujaśiraḥ is the top of the upper arm. aṃsa is the shoulder.

Next, for the names of the arm: bhuja is the arm, because it moves. bāhu is the arm because it blazes forth. praviṣṭa is the arm because it enters. doḥ is the arm because it calms: from damu, in the sense of calming, with a suffix and so on. pañcaśākha is "the five-branched," because it is like a tree with five branches. śaya is the hand, "the one that lies down." pāṇi is the hand, because it displays the transactions of daily life.

Next, kurpara is the elbow, and what lies above it is pragaṇḍa, the upper arm. Below the elbow is prakoṣṭha, the forearm. Between that and the hand is maṇibandha, called "the binding of jewels," which is the wrist. Next, the part reaching to the little finger is called karabha.

For the names of the forefinger: tarjanī, "the threatening finger," and pradeśinī, called "the one that points out."

For the finger: aṅgulī and aṅgurī, "finger," and karaśākha, called "the branch of the hand."

For the individual names of the thumb and the others: aṅguṣṭha is the thumb: from aṅge tiṣṭhati, "it stands on the body," it is the thumb because it abides on the body. For the forefinger there is pradeśinī, explained above, because it points out and makes known the dealings of the moment. madhyamā is the middle finger, because it grows in the middle of them all. anāmikā is "the nameless," because it has no name. kaniṣṭha is the little finger, because it is the last of all.

Next, for the names of the nail: punarbhava, "the one that grows again"; karaduha, "the one grown from the hand"; and nakha and nakhara, "the nail."

Next, for the names of measures: the thumb stretched out together with the forefinger is called pradeśa, "the pointer." Likewise, the thumb stretched out together with the middle finger is tālā, called "a span." The thumb stretched out together with the ring finger is karṇa, called "the cow's ear." And the thumb stretched out together with the little finger is the span called vitasti.

Next, for the names of the neck: kaṇṭha, "the throat"; gala, "the neck"; grīvā, "the neck"; śirodhi; kandhara, "the holder of the head"; kamugrīvā, "the conch-like neck"; and trirekhā, "the three-lined." These last two are names for the neck of a great being.

Next, the opening of the mouth has seven names: vaktra, "the speaker"; āsya, "the mouth"; vadana, "the speaker"; ruṇḍa; ānana, "the mouth," or "the face"; lapanaṃ, "the utterer"; and mukha, "the door," or "the mouth."

For the names of the nose: ghrāṇa, "the smeller"; gandhavaha, "the bearer of scents"; and ghoṇā, nāsā and nāsika, "the nose," in ordinary or honorific speech.

For the names of the lips: oṣṭha and adhara, "the upper lip" and "the lower lip"; radanācchada, "the covering of the teeth"; and daśanavāsasī, "the garment of the teeth," because they share the same property as those.

Next, gaṇḍa and kapola are the cheeks.

Next, for the names of the teeth: radana, "the chewer"; daśana, "the cutter"; danta, "the proud one"; and rada, "the tooth."

Next, tālu and kākuda are names of the palate.

Next, for the names of the tongue: rasajñā, "the knower of tastes"; rasanā, "the taster"; and jihvā, "the tongue."

For the names of the forehead: lalāṭa, "the forehead"; alika, "the place of ornament"; and godhi, "the holder of the eyes," for gaur netra dhīyate means "go is the eye, and it holds it."

The eye has eight names: locanaṃ, "the looker"; nayanaṃ, "the guide"; netraṃ, "the eye"; akṣiṇaṃ, "the seer"; cakṣuḥ, "the illuminator"; akṣiṇī, "the pervader"; and bṛka and dṛṣṭi, "the revealer" and "the looker."

The head has five names: uttamāṅga, "the highest limb"; śiraḥ, "the head"; śīrṣa, "the head"; mūrdhana, "the crown"; and mastaka, "the one that holds the brain."

The hair of the head has six names. cikura is hair because it is gathered and then cut: ci is in the sense of gathering, and kur in the sense of cutting; by the rule of pṛṣodara and so on, the suffix is dropped, giving cikura. kuntala is "the curling." vāla is hair because it is fine. kaca is hair because it binds: kaca is in the sense of binding, with the suffix ac. As for keśa, it comes from ke mastake śete tiṣṭhati, "on the head it lies, it stays": ke means "on the head," and śa is the syllable that is part of śete, "lies"; because it is as if lying and remaining on the head, it is "the one that lies on the head," that is, hair. śiroruha is "the one grown from the head."

Body hair has three names. tanūruha is "the one grown from the body." roma is body hair: ruha is in the sense of growing forth; by the rule "vyoma and the rest," the suffix man is added and the final is elided, and so roma is formed. That same word, by the rule "kapirika and the rest," has its r changed to l, giving loma, "hair," or "body hair."

Next, for the names of ornaments: alaṃkāra, alaṃkriyā, ābharaṇa, pariṣkāra, bhūṣaṇa and maṇḍanaṃ are general names for ornament. mukuṭa and kariṭaṃ, "the diadem"; cūḍāmaṇi, "the crest jewel"; and śiromaṇi, "the jewel on the crown of the head," are names of head ornaments.

For the names of ear ornaments: karṇika, "the one with anthers"; tālapattra, "the palm leaf"; kuṇḍala, "the coiled"; and karṇaveṣṭekaṃ, "the one wound round the ear." These are they.

Next, graiveyaka and kanṭhabhūṣa are the neck ornament.

For the bracelet: kaṭaka and valaya.

For the girdle there are two: mekhalā, "the belt," or "the ornamented girdle," and rasanā, called "the one with savor."

For the anklet: mañjira, nūpura, haṃsaka, "the one like a goose," and the rest.

Next, the four kinds beginning with skin are varieties of cloth, because they are the materials of cloth. Among these, tvaka is skin or bark, that is, bark, linen cloth and the like. phala is fruit, that which comes from the fruit of the cotton tree. kṛmi is the worm, silk and the like, since it is born from the cocoons of worms. roma is hair, that which is produced from the hair of the deer called aṅku, of sheep and of other animals. navāmbara and the rest are particular kinds of cloth, such as what is called new cloth.

For cloth in general there are eight names: naktaka, "the covering of nakedness"; kanpaṭa, "cotton cloth"; vastra, "cloth"; ācchādanaṃ, "the cover," or "the wrap"; vāsa, "the garment"; cela, "cloth"; vāsanaṃ, "the protection of nakedness"; and aṃśukaṃ, "the material for clothing."

For the names of length and breadth: dairghya is "long," or "length." āyāma and āroha are "length." pariṇāha and viśāla are "great," or "wide," or "broad."

pucela, "fine cloth," and paṭa, "silk cloth," are two names of fine cloth. varāśi, "a heap of cloth," and sthūlaśaṭa, "thick cloth," are two names of layered or coarse cloth.

Next, nicola and pracchadapaṭa are the lower garment or the wrap.

rallaka and kambala are the blanket, or woolen cloth. paridhāna and adhoṃaṃśuka are the skirt, or lower garment. prāvāra and uttarasaṃga are the upper garment, or shawl.

Next, vitānaṃ is the canopy; ullor, the overhead covering; dūṣya, the tent; and vattraviṣaṃ, the pavilion, called "a house of cloth."

Next, pratisīra and javanikā or yavanikā are the curtain, and tiraskarabhā is "that which makes unseen"; these are names of the curtain.

Next, parikarma, "thorough care," and aṅgasaṃskāra, "the grooming of the body," are general names for bathing and the like. Among these, mārṣṭi is "scrubbing clean"; mārjanī, "bathing powder"; and mṛja, "the purifier." uddhattana and ucchadana are rubbing down with unguent; these are names for removing dirt with substances. snānaṃ is bathing. āplava and plava are names for bathing in water.

For the names of the fragrant medicine smeared on the body: kuṅkuma, "saffron"; kaśmīrajanma, "born in Kashmir"; agniśikha, "the tip of the flame"; vara, "the one possessed of excellence"; bāhṭīka, "the one possessed of fire"; pītana, "the yellow"; sūrya, "the sun"; rakta, "the red"; saṃkoca, "the wholly contracted"; and piśuna, "the slanderer." These are names of saffron.

dhīra, "the steady," and lahita, "the red," are two names of red sandalwood.

Lac has six names: lakṣā, "lac"; that same word changed by the rule of kapirika and the rest, rakṣā, which is the same; jantu, "the one possessed of birth"; yāva; ālakta, "lac dye"; and drumāmaya, "the tree free of disease."

The clove has three names: lavaṅga, "the clove"; devakusuma, "the flower of the gods"; and śrīsaṃjña, "the one whose name is Glory."

Next, jāpaka, "the reciter"; kālīya, "the one beneficial in its season"; and kālānusāri, "the one that follows the time," are names of the yellow wood known among merchants and others as kālīya.

Next, for the names of aloeswood: sāmarthakaṃ, "the potent"; vaṃśika, "the one possessed of lineage"; and aguru, as it is said, laghunāmatvād aguru: "because it has the name 'light,' it is not heavy." rājārha is called "fit for a king." lohakṛmija is "the worm-born"; and there is joṅgakaṃ. Master Vyāḍi also explains kṛṣṭadṛkṣa, "the black wood," and nṛpapriya, "the delight of the lord of men." For special kinds of aloeswood, black aloeswood, maṃgalī, "the auspicious," and mallīgandhi are explained.

Next, the five beginning with yakṣadhūpa are names of white incense: yakṣadhūpa, "sacrificial incense"; sarjarasa, "resin incense"; arāla, "pratsi incense"; sarvarasa, "the one with every juice"; and bahurūpa, "the many-formed."

pṛkdhūpa, "compounded incense," and kṛtrimadhūpa, "artificial incense," are such as the ten-ingredient incense, in which natural scents such as aloeswood are skillfully blended with one another as described in the treatises on perfume.

Next, turuṣka, paṇḍṛka, sihla and yāvana are names of sihla, storax.

Next, śrīvāsa, "the glorious cloth," and the five beginning with it, are names of turpentine resin.

Next, musk has three names: mṛganābhe, "the deer's navel"; mṛgamada, "the deer's liquor"; and kastūrī, "musk."

kakkola has three names: kolakama, kakkolakaṃ and kośaphala, "the fruit in a sheath."

Camphor has five names. karbū is camphor because it is possessed of potency: kṛpu is in the sense of potency, with the suffix ūra. ghanasāra is "the essence of the cloud." candrasaṃjña is "the one whose name is the moon," for moon and camphor are synonyms of one another. sitābhra is "the one with white light." himavāluka is "snow-sand."

Next, with half a verse come the general names of sandalwood: gandhasāra, "the essence of scent"; malayaja, "born on Malaya," that is, coming from Mount Malaya; bhadraśrī, "auspicious glory"; and candana, known as tsandan, which is called "the one that satisfies": cadi is in the sense of satisfying, with the suffix luṭ.

White sandalwood has three names. tailaparṇika, called "the one with sesame-oil leaves," is the sandalwood that comes from one region of Mount Malaya. gośīrṣa, called "supreme ground" or "cow's head," grows in a region of Malaya shaped like a cow's head. As for haricandana, hari is the frog, and because it grows from a mountain shaped like one, it is called haricandana.

Next come the names of red sandalwood: tailaparṇi, "the one with sesame-oil leaves"; patrāṅgaṃ, "the one with a body of leaves," that is, with leaves red at the center; rañjanaṃ, "the dye"; and raktacandana, "red sandalwood." kucandana is the name of inferior sandalwood.

Next, for the names of nutmeg: jātī, "the jasmine," or "the jasmine tree"; koṣa, "the sheathed"; and jātīphala, "the king of spices," or what is called likshiwer. yakṣakardama is "the sacrificial unguent," a compound of camphor, musk, aloeswood and kakkola.

For the names of arrangement, or of being decked with ornaments: racanā, "arrangement"; parisyanda, "composing the forms"; ābhoga; and paripūrṇa, "the completely perfect."

dīpa, "the lamp," and pradīpa, "the blazing," are names of the lamp.

For the seat: pīṭha and āsana.

Next, samurga is the casket, and sampuṭaka the lidded box or amulet case.

prasādhanī is the comb, and kaṅkatikā "the many-toothed": the implement made of ivory and the like for grooming the hair.

For the mirror: darpaṇa, makura and ādarśa, "the all-seeing."

Next, byañjanaṃ, "the fan," and talavṛttakaṃ, "the cooling fan": these complete the section on human beings.

Next, in the section on brahmins and the rest, first, for the general names of lineage: santati, "descent"; gotra, "family"; janana, "line"; kula, "clan," or "family"; abhijana, "kindred"; anvaya, "those born after," or "those who follow after"; viśa, "family line"; anvavāya, "the line of ancestry"; and santāna, "continuity."

That lineage is of four kinds: brahmin, kṣatriya, vaiśya and śūdra.

The brahmin has six names. dvija is "the twice-born," said of brahmins, birds and teeth. agrajanma is "the first-born," for it is said that the brahmin was born first of the four castes. bhūdeva is "the god of the earth." vaḍa is "the one of the mare." visra is the brahmin. prahmaṇa is "the son of Brahmā," for it is said that brahmins were born from the mouth of Brahmā.

For the synonyms of the name "learned" there are twenty-three here: vidvān, "the one with knowledge," or "the knowing"; vipaścita, "the clear-minded"; doṣajña, "the knower of faults"; sat, "the one possessed of beauty," or "the excellent"; sudhī, "the one of good understanding"; kovida, "the one who knows everything"; budha, "the one possessed of realization"; dhīra, "the steadfast"; manīṣijña, "the knower of minds"; prajñā, "the one possessed of wisdom"; saṃkhyāna, "the one possessed of reckoning"; paṇḍita, "the learned," or "the wise"; kavi, "the poet"; dhīmāna, "the intelligent"; sūri, "the hero"; kṛtī, "the one possessed of understanding"; kṛṣṭi, "the skilled"; labdhavarṇa, "the famed," or "the one who has won renown"; vicakṣaṇa, "the clear," or "the lucid"; dūradarśī, "the one who sees far"; dīrghadirśā, "the one who sees a long way"; śotriya, "the one possessed of learning"; and chandasa, "the knower of meter."

From the section on the kṣatriyas, for the names of the king: rāja, "the king"; bahuja, "the arm-born," because the kṣatriyas are said to have been born from the arms of Brahmā. kṣatriya is also called "the protector from wounds," that is, the royal caste. pirāṭa is "the wholly victorious," and rāṭ "the shining," or "the king." pārthiva is "the lord of the earth"; kṣmābhuka, "the enjoyer of the earth"; nṛpa, "the lord of men"; bhūpa, "the protector of the earth"; and mahīkṣitaḥ, "the guardian of the land." adhīśvara is "the supreme lord," that is, a king to whom many lesser kings bow; cakravarti, "the wheel-turning monarch"; and sarvabhauma, "the ruler of all lands." These are they.

For the names of the elephant: dantī, "the one possessed of tusks"; dadhā, "the tusked"; bala, "the mighty"; hasti, "the one possessed of a hand"; dvidantā, "the two-tusked"; anekapā, "the one who drinks much"; dvipā, "the one who drinks twice"; and mātaṃgaja, "born of the she-elephant." These are they.

For the general names of the horse: ghaṭaka, "the leader"; vāti, "the water-drinker"; and turaṃ. Below: nāga, kuñjara, gaja, tharaṇa, karī, ibha, stamberama and sadmi, which are "the passionate ones." gaturaṃgama is called "the swift-goer," or "the rapid-goer," or "the one who goes swiftly." vājī is "the winged"; vāha, "the mount"; arba, the horse; gandharva, "the goer"; aśva; hayarta; and saindhava, "born of the ocean," which is also said of salt and of the sword; and saptaya, "the one possessed of seven." These are they.

For the names of particular horses: ājāneya, "the thoroughbred"; kulinta, "the kyeling"; vinīta, "the well-trained"; sādhumahina, "the one who goes well"; and vanayuja, pārasīka, kāmboja and bahnikāya are names for horses born in those lands, called respectively born in the land of Vana, born in Persia, born in Kamboja, and born in Bāhlīka.

Prosperity has four names: sampada, "abundance"; sampatti, "wealth," or "abundance"; śrī, "glory," or "good fortune"; and lakṣmī, "abundance," or "glory," or "auspiciousness."

The Kāmadhenu, the extensive commentary on the Amarakośa composed by Master Subhūticandra, is complete, as far as it was found.

Among the five fields of knowledge, the subjects that the learned are to know, this belongs to the science of language. It is an extensive commentary on the lexicographical treatise called the Amarakośa, which dispels confusion about the vast range of name-conventions known to the learned that come from the vast scriptural systems, both outer and inner: the Buddha's word together with its commentaries, the Vedas and the rest. Its root text was composed by the paṇḍita called Amarasiṃha, who compiled it from the treatises of earlier masters such as Vyāḍi, Vararuci, Candragomin and Vāmana, and made it outstanding with distinctive features of its own. For the Utpalinī and others are too condensed, the Nāmamālā and others lack the genders, the Treatise on Gender and others lack the vocabulary, the Amaramālā and others are incomplete, and the Popālita and others are lacking in sections; this one alone is distinguished above them by every good quality. Therefore it was formerly translated in full by the paṇḍita Kīrticandra and the Yarlung translator Drakpa Gyaltsen.

This commentary, the Kāmadhenu, was set out in detail by the paṇḍita called Subhūticandra, who compiled it from the vast treatises on usage. The ways of deriving the words of the individual names chiefly follow the system of Master Candragomin, as it says earlier, in the homage:

"The lexicons of endless traditions, an ocean filled with jewels,
Have been arranged as necklaces for the throat of poetry and as treasuries;
Though what glorious Candragomin composed is supreme,
I compose according to the method that he thoroughly established."

Further, it was composed in reliance on the following: the commentary on the Cāndra grammar composed by Master Dharmadāsa; its extensive commentary composed by the paṇḍita Ratnamati; the still more extensive commentary on that, in thirty-six thousand lines, composed by the paṇḍita Pūrṇacandra Jitendra; the Companion to the Verbal Roots that he composed; the commentary on the grammar of Mañjuśrī, in eight thousand lines, composed by Rājaśrī, the master from Kaliṅga; the explanation of the Kātantra composed by Durga, namely the Kalāpa commentary of Durgasiṃha; the grammatical treatise of the brahmin Pāṇini; the Amaramālā, the Ratnakośa, the Illumination of the Erotic and the Trikāṇḍaśeṣa, composed variously by Master Vararuci, Vyāḍi, Rabhasa, Vāmana, the sage Vyāsa and others; the Bhārata, the poem of ancient history; the legends of Śamba; the Rāmāyaṇa; the Mārkaṇḍeya; the Necklace of Sarasvatī; the Kapphiṇābhyudaya, root text and commentary, composed by Śabarasvāmin, "lord of the mountain hermits," or Śivasvāmin, "lord of peace"; the Kumārasambhava composed by Kālidāsa; the Ornament of Speech of Daṇḍin; and others. Reckoned by the number of verses of the great text, it amounts to about ten thousand. Of this, an Indian manuscript of somewhat more than half is kept at the temple of Nedong Tsé.

There is one who possesses outstanding wisdom and immeasurable compassion; who robs the lord of the Thirty-Three (Indra) of his pride by setting the anthers of the lotus of his footstool upon the crown jewels of all the rulers of the land amid the snow mountains; and who, through his stainless learning in the two systems, makes the teaching of the one with the ten powers (the Buddha), a wish-fulfilling jewel, and the benefit and happiness of all creatures, gods included, flourish and long endure upon the earth: the lord of all the land, lord of Dharma and Dharma king, Chenga Rinpoché Pal Ngakgi Wangchuk Drakpa, victorious in every direction, his divine presence. Relying on his command, "Translate it well," and on the excellent favorable conditions he bestowed, the monk called Dharmapālabhadra, the third Gö translator, a renunciant of glorious Zhalu, who has studied the great treatises on the grammar of the well-formed language, the Kalāpa and the Cāndra together with their commentaries, and has understood merely a portion of them, and who has seen a little of the way in which the great translators of old translated the holy Dharma, thought that dispelling the errors that come from not understanding the elements of the names in the precious scriptures, and newly showing a little of the method of the stainless treatises, would become an auspicious connection for the precious teaching not to decline, but to spread through every door. So, receiving the command of the glorious supreme protector of the land upon the crown of his head, he did as follows.

The extensive ways of deriving words he left untranslated, just as the great translators of old, considering that Tibetans would not properly understand them, appear to have left them untranslated in the Abhidharmakośa commentary of Jinaputra, the Guṇavatī commentary on the Mahāmāyā and other works. Since setting the Sanskrit beside the Tibetan for each name appears very helpful for understanding the explanation of certain points, he set out and explained both languages, even though this is not in the Indian manuscript, just as in the Guhyasamāja commentary called Clear Meaning the translation appears as "bandha bandha means 'bind!'" and so on. Because of omissions and additions, the connecting particles and case endings in between were added as an expedient, as best suited the Tibetan language. A few brief and clear derivations of words he did translate, thinking they would benefit some readers, since Shongtön Dorjé Gyaltsen and others translated the methods of grammar into Tibetan and founded a tradition of explaining them, so that there are scholars of it even today. In short, as the second volume of the Madhyavyutpatti says, "In translating the holy Dharma, do not contradict the meaning, and make it as natural as possible in Tibetan!" So it was translated and well established.

Thus, so that the fine words of the scripture
Of the one who teaches nonduality may become an ornament of speech,
By whatever virtue has been gained from translating well
This commentary on a treatise of usage,
White as the autumn moon and vast as the path of the stars,
May the darkness in the hearts of all beings without exception be dispelled,
May the light of discerning intelligence spread far,
And may they attain the excellent understanding of the analytical knowledges.

How the world arose, with karma and the afflictions as its cause, endowed with its productive cause;
And the cause that reverses karma and the afflictions, this too the Guide fully taught;
Where the suffering of birth, of aging and of decline surely does not abide,
That supreme liberation, the bull among speakers, knowing it himself, has taught.

This is the verse of the treatise.

The scribe of both the earlier and the later translations of this is the spiritual friend who speaks both languages:

The beginning of his name is the supreme letter,
Its end is adorned with ratna:
Anupamaratna,
That is, Peerless Jewel.

Translator's notes

The text is a grammatical commentary on Amarasiṃha's Sanskrit lexicon, and much of its technical vocabulary (compound types, case numbers, root classes, affixes and rule names such as erac and aṇ) is rendered as closely as the Tibetan allows; several of these grammatical remarks are terse and their exact Sanskrit referents are uncertain. "Caṇḍagomin" follows the Tibetan spelling; this is very probably the grammarian Candragomin, whose system the Kāmadhenu follows. Several evident copyists' slips are read as follows: སྐྱོ་བ as སྐྱ་བ ("oar"); བསྟན་པར་བྱ་བ as བསྟེན་པར་བྱ་བ ("to be served"), as the commentary itself goes on to say; གང་གི་ཡི་གེ as ཡང་གི་ཡི་གེ ("the word 'also'", rendering Sanskrit ca); and དུར་བ as ངུར་བ ("ruddy goose", the cakravāka), as the gloss that follows confirms. "The crescent moon of existence" may play on Bhava (Śiva), who bears the crescent moon and the Ganges. The final two sentences are obscure and are rendered literally. The commentary is highly technical Sanskrit grammar refracted through Tibetan; several sentences (the opening sentence, the remarks on the "inner" and "outer" sense, and on the present tense in praise) are terse and are rendered close to the letter. A short Tibetan gloss after "kaumodakī" (རྔོ་འོ) is unintelligible and is not rendered. The Sanskrit example words are given exactly as the Tibetan spells them, several of them evidently corrupt. Vyāḍi is spelled བྱཱ་ཌ in the verse citation and བྱཱ་ཊཱི later; both are taken as the lexicographer Vyāḍi. མཆོག་སྲེད is taken as Vararuci; སྐབས་གསུམ་གྱི་མངོན་བརྗོད is tentatively taken as the Trikāṇḍa lexicon. The "other treatises" (རྒྱུད་གཞན) here are lexical and grammatical works, not tantras. The Candragomin named here (ཙནྡྲ་གོ་མི) is the same grammarian spelled Caṇḍagomin in the opening verses. This piece comments on Amarakoṣa's introductory verses on how gender is indicated, on the avoidance of dvandva compounds and of the "retention of one member" (ekaśeṣa), and on the principle "no mixing except by order." The grammatical reasoning is compressed and several sentences are rendered close to the letter. The Sanskrit words are given exactly as the Tibetan spells them; the Ratnakoṣa citation is given in translation with its Sanskrit forms as spelled (svargga, trideva, tripiṣṭaya). In "for the sake of brevity of the text," the word ཟོར in གཞུང་ཟོར་ཡང་བ is obscure and the phrase is rendered by sense. ཆུ་སྐྱེས ("water-born") in the cited verse renders apsaras, and འཕྲོག་བྱེད is Hari (Viṣṇu). The passage glosses the Amarakoṣa's opening rules on gender markers (dvayoḥ, triṣu, niṣiddhaliṅga, the particles tu and atha). The Sanskrit quotations are heavily garbled in the Tibetan transcription and are given exactly as spelled. "Its being feminine" for astriyaṃ: the Tibetan reads "this is feminine," though the Sanskrit means "not in the feminine"; a negative may have dropped out. "Appear in the earlier translation" reads sdang as snang; this sentence is an editorial note on the Tibetan translation. "Brevity of the text" renders gzhung zor yang ba, taking zor here in a non-ritual sense of the text's burden or bulk. "Maxim of the stick and the cake" reads 'khur ba as khur ba (cake), i.e. the daṇḍāpūpa maxim. "The third" at the end follows the Tibetan skabs gsum pa, though in the Amarakoṣa the classes of humans fall in the second book. This passage of the Amarakoṣa commentary is heavily technical (Pāṇinian and Uṇādi rules) and its Sanskrit is badly garbled in Tibetan transcription; Sanskrit is given exactly as spelled. "Ray, wave, ray, vomit, dregs" and "split, in the domain of nature, chain, principal" appear to be interlinear Tibetan glosses on the Sanskrit examples that have entered the text; they are translated as they stand. In "for the lofty," the following words khyed rnams ded rnams appear corrupt and are left untranslated. In "Where the three play," the words bod 'gro are unclear and are not rendered. "Joyful" renders bde ldan, and the following skyo mad, apparently naming a rule, is left untranslated. The Elucidation of Elegance (sgeg pa gsal ba) could not be identified; its title is translated literally. candrapalba may be a personal name but is not securely identifiable. The Sanskrit grammatical rules and quotations are heavily garbled in Tibetan transcription and are given exactly as spelled. "Mahārājikas" renders phreng ba chen po, literally "great garland," taken as a calque of mahārājika (rāji, "row, line") in the Amarakoṣa's list of group gods. "Destruction" in "birth, abiding and destruction" reads 'jigs pa as 'jig pa. "Trikāṇḍaśeṣa" renders skabs gsum gyi lhag ma, the supplement to the Amarakoṣa. "Liṅgānuśāsana" renders rtags rjes su bstan pa. "Bhāgavṛtti" renders bha ga'i 'grel pa. The verses of names carry interlinear numerals counting the names; they are used to divide the names and are not read out. Names that could not be securely identified are translated literally: among the Ādityas the Wayfarer, the Praiser, Not-Former, the Noble, Human-Form and the Radiant; among the Viśvedevas the Hero, the Clear, Burden-Eye, the Person, the Left and Substance (the phrase "his own, namely" appears to be an intrusion); among the Sādhyas all but Mind, the Swan and Dharma; among the Rudras Growth, Praise, the Diligent One and Victor over Others. Here grub pa is the siddhas of the Amarakoṣa's list of semi-divine beings, not the Sādhyas. Bde mchog here is the asura Śambara, not the tantric deity Cakrasaṃvara. "Bow" (gzhu) appears to render Danu as if it were dhanu, "bow." The Terrible One (a rākṣasa), Free-of-Harsh-Sound and Chooser-of-Melody (gandharvas), the Golden One (gser can), the Protector of Beings, the Clear One (perhaps Dakṣa) and the Deliverer (sgrol ba po) are translated literally because they cannot be identified with confidence. "The division called warm" (dro ba'i dbye) is unclear. Grammatical rules and Sanskrit quotations are garbled in transcription and are given exactly as spelled. The grammatical discussion (Pāṇini, Candragomin, Maitreyarakṣita, Ratnamati) is garbled in transcription; its Sanskrit is given exactly as spelled, and some sentences (the argument about the letter of supa) are rendered literally. "The Extensive Commentary on the bhāṣa" (bhā ṣa'i rgya cher 'grel pa) and "the Ornament of Instructions on the Sūtras" (mdo sde'i man ngag gi rgyan) are not securely identified and are translated literally. The second verse on the name Buddha is obscure and is rendered closely. Mtshan in "names of the Buddha" means names, not the major marks.
